# STUDY ON FOLIOS A OF "THE ANATOMY"

Leonardo had studied anatomy in its entirety and with great depth. And when I consider with what care he studied every part of the human body, when I see the superiority of his universal genius, the way in which he excelled in mechanics and hydraulics, the attention with which he sought to understand all the things he had to account for, I am absolutely convinced that Leonardo must be regarded as the best and greatest anatomist of his time. Furthermore, he is certainly the first who inaugurated the use of anatomical drawings. <note>Before Leonardo, anatomical illustrations were rare and often schematic or symbolic. Leonardo pioneered the use of "visual language"—such as perspective and cross-sections—to convey medical information.</note> This judgment, already widely justified by the present volume (*Folios A*) <note>The "Folios A" (Feuillets A) refers to a specific collection of Leonardo’s manuscript pages, dated to around 1510, which focus heavily on the mechanics of the skeletal and muscular systems.</note>, which relates specifically to the anatomy of forms <note>The "anatomy of forms" refers to surface anatomy, where the focus is on how internal structures like bone and muscle define the outward shape and movement of the living body—a perspective essential for fine art.</note>, will be even more so by the publication of the *Folios* that are to follow, in which Leonardo approached the study of anatomy from a less specialized perspective.

->MATHIAS-DUVAL.<- <note>Mathias-Duval (1844–1907) was a prominent French professor of anatomy. His endorsement served to bridge the gap between Leonardo's reputation as an artist and his recognition as a legitimate pioneer of medical science.</note>

# ON ANATOMY

## SHEETS A

<note>original: "DELL' ANATOMIA - FOGLI A"</note>

<note>This page serves as the title header for a specific collection of Leonardo da Vinci's anatomical studies. "Sheets A" (Fogli A) refers to a group of eighteen double-sided pages dating to approximately 1510–1511. During this period, Leonardo was working in Milan and performing meticulous dissections, often at the University of Pavia, focusing heavily on the human skeletal and muscular systems.</note>

<term>Anatomy</term>: From the Greek *anatome*, meaning "to cut up." Leonardo was one of the first to combine artistic mastery with rigorous scientific dissection to understand the inner workings of the human body.

# ANATOMY Sheets A - 1. Front Side

### Anatomical Studies of the Foot

<margin>
Of the toes of the
foot and their
nerves and their
bones and their
joints, and why the
toes of the feet
are so small
compared to those
of the hands.
</margin>

<note>Anatomical study of the bones of the foot (dorsal view).</note>

Do this same thing for the foot from below, considering in how many ways the foot can turn, and what kind of nerves and muscles move it, and in what way they connect with the leg, and the nerves that descend from the brain <note>original: "cerebro"</note> for such a function, and how the same muscles move the toes just as those of the hand <note>In the original Italian, "dita" refers to both fingers and toes.</note> move the fingers; and note the position of the <term>calcaneus<gloss>the heel bone</gloss></term> <note>original: "osso chalchagnio"</note> and the other bones that are around it which compose the said foot.

<note>Anatomical study of the bones of the foot (oblique view).</note>

<note>A series of small diagrams illustrating the mechanical action of tendons and bones as levers, labeled with letters a, b, c, d.</note>

<note>Study of the lateral view of the tarsal and metatarsal bones, highlighting the ankle joint.</note>

When you have removed the skin from the foot and removed the nerves and muscles so that only the bones remain, note the shape of each one, and draw the said shape of the joints from as many viewpoints as you can; and note the locations of the openings through which the vessels and the nerves pass, and the other subtle things which are not known except through great diligence.

<margin>
Note well the nature of the bone which is called the <term>navicular<gloss>a boat-shaped bone located in the midfoot</gloss></term> <note>original: "navichulare"</note> and also the <term>cuboid<gloss>a square-shaped bone on the outer side of the foot</gloss></term> <note>original: "chubhiforme"</note>, and how the said bones fit together and are bound together so that they do not move; and even if they should move, let such movement be of little consequence <note>original: "pocha chonssideratione"</note> compared to the great force and weight that rests upon them.
</margin>

<margin>Sesamoid bones and their functions.</margin>
Nature has placed the glandular bone <note>original: "osso glanduloso"; Leonardo is referring to the sesamoid bones, small rounded bones embedded in tendons</note> beneath the joint of the big toe of the foot; for if the nerve <note>original: "neruo"; in this period, the term was often used interchangeably for nerves, tendons, and ligaments. Here, it refers to the tendon</note> where such a glandular bone is joined were without such a glandule, it would receive great damage from rubbing under such a great weight as that of a man, when in walking he raises himself upon the balls of his feet at every step.

# ON ANATOMY — FOLIO A — 1 RECTO
1*

[Fig. 10 :] *n*
[Fig. 11.]

<margin>
Extension and flexion of the toes and their mechanism.
</margin>

[Near the left margin :] When the potential line of motion <note>original: "linia potenziale del moto"; this refers to the vector or line of force exerted by a tendon</note> passes through the middle of the junctions of the movable parts <note>the phalanges or toe bones</note>, they will not be movable, but will remain fixed in their original straightness; as is demonstrated in *a n*, the motor <note>original: "motore"; the muscle or tendon providing the force</note> which passes through the center of the two movable parts *n m* and *m o* and makes them stable. But if the potential line of the motor is outside the central line of the two straight movable parts—then, since the front of the first or second movable part is hemispherical in shape, the junction of the two straight movable parts will undoubtedly become angular at their contact.

[To the right :] And if the line of the motor is outside the junction of the two straight movable parts, then the further it is removed from the length of such movable parts, the more their straightness will bend into an angle, just as the string does to its bow.

---

Extension and flexion of the toes and their mechanism.
</margin>

[Fig. 10 :] *n.*
[Fig. 11.]

[Near the left margin :] When the potential line of motion passes through the middle of the junctions of the movable parts, they will not be movable, but will establish themselves in their primary straightness, as shown in *a n*, the motor, which passes through the center of the two movable parts *n m* and *m o* and makes them stable. But if the potential line of the motor is outside the central line of the two straight movable parts, then, the front of the first or second movable part having a hemispherical shape, the junction of the two straight movable parts will without doubt become angular at their contact.

[To the right :] And if the line of the motor is outside the junction of the two straight movable parts, then the further it is removed from the length of such movable parts, the more their straightness will bend into an angle, just as the string does with its bow.

--- Page 69 ---
<meta>This page continues the anatomical investigation from the previous folio. While the preceding page (Folio 1 Verso) provided a broad overview of the arm's skeletal structure through numbered sketches, this page (Folio 222) offers a much deeper mechanical analysis of how these bones interact. Specifically, it focuses on the forearm's ability to rotate—a process we now call pronation and supination—expanding on the relationship between the humerus and the two bones of the forearm.</meta>

<note>This page contains detailed anatomical studies of the human arm and shoulder skeleton, specifically investigating the mechanical rotation (supination and pronation) of the radius and ulna.</note>

The upper arm is composed of 3 bones. One is called the humerus <note>original: *aiutorio*, an archaic term for the upper arm bone</note>, and the other two are called the "focile" bones <note>original: *focili*, likely named after the iron "flint-striker" due to their shape</note>. The larger "focile" <term>the radius</term> is located below, on the side of the thumb, and the smaller one <term>the ulna</term> is above, on the side of the little finger. This one is thinner than the larger "focile," and the upper end of the said smaller bone is called the elbow. This bone structure remains fixed, while the larger "focile" is mobile.

<note>Study of the arm skeleton with labels a, b, c indicating humerus, radius, and ulna.</note>

<margin>
a is the bone of the arm [humerus]. b is the larger "focile" [radius]. c is the smaller "focile" [ulna].
</margin>

When the arm turns, it
causes the larger "focile"
to be above the smaller one,
and when it is turned
the other way, it is
under the smaller one.
And this is the cause
of the turning of the hand,
when it turns with the palm
upward, or indeed
turns with the palm downward.

<note>Study of the arm bones showing the crossing of the radius over the ulna.</note>

Because the bone of the shoulder
is what creates the motion of the arm
at the shoulder. And when the arm
is raised, it causes this
bone to be turned upward, and when
it is lowered, it is turned downward.

<note>Study of the shoulder girdle with the scapula (labeled d) and humerus.</note>

<margin>
this is called "d," the shoulder blade <note>original: *lo scapolo*</note>.
</margin>

The arm consists of these 3 bones. One is called the humerus, and the other two are called the "focile" bones. These "focile" bones are bound together from the elbow to the hand. The larger "focile" <note>the radius</note> is the one that rotates, and the smaller "focile" <note>the ulna</note> is the one that remains still. The elbow is fixed and will not rotate, and it belongs to the smaller "focile."

<note>Full skeletal arm and shoulder view with the scapula, showing the joint architecture.</note>

Note how the bones of the fingers have this property: that when the fingers are turned, that part is turned toward the other side, and when they are extended, they face toward the same side. And this is the cause of the motion of the hand.

<margin>
<note>Geometric diagram illustrating the leverage of the arm bones.</note>
a. b. is the motion of the arm. n. m.
</margin>

<summary>This page documents Leonardo's mechanical discovery that the radius (focile maggiore) rotates around the stationary ulna (focile minore) to allow the hand to turn. It also analyzes the movement of the scapula in relation to raising and lowering the arm.</summary>

<keywords>anatomy, osteology, radius, ulna, humerus, scapula, biomechanics, forearm rotation, hand movement</keywords>

### FOLIO I — verso.

<margin>Arm and forearm. Proportional relationships.</margin>
The nerve <note>original: "nerbo"; Leonardo often used this term to refer to tendons or ligaments as well as nerves</note> *d* is attached in the middle between the shoulder joint and the tips of the fingers. —

The shoulder bone <note>original: "osso della spalla"</note> *c e* is one-third of the length of the bone *b c* <note>the humerus</note>. —

The greatest length of the shoulder blade <note>original: "padella della spalla"; referring to the scapula</note> is from *n* to *m*, and it is equal to the length of the hand from *f* to *a*. The hand from *f* to *a* is six-sevenths of the bone *a b* <note>the radius and ulna</note>. —

[Fig. 12 :] *d m f a b n c e*

The bones *a b* are five-sevenths of the length of the bone *b c* when the arm is extended with the palm of the hand turned toward the sky. —

[Fig. 13.]
<margin>Method for the study of the arm and forearm.</margin>
You shall first make these bones sawed through lengthwise and then crosswise, so that it may be seen where the bones are thick or thin; then depict them whole and separated.

# LEONARDO DA VINCI

<margin>Biceps.</margin>
...as [described] above, but through 4 aspects, so that their true shape may be understood; then go on dressing them <note>Leonardo often used the metaphor of "dressing" (vestendo) the skeletal frame with layers of soft tissue to describe the process of anatomical synthesis.</note> step by step with their nerves <note>original: "nervi"; in Leonardo’s time, this term was used broadly to refer to nerves, tendons, and ligaments.</note>, veins, and muscles.

[Fig. 14:] *a*
*a* is the muscle of the inner arm, which attaches to the shoulder blade <note>original: "padella della spalla"; literally "the pan of the shoulder," referring to the scapula.</note>. —

<margin>
Method for the study of the parts of the human body.
Spongy and solid bones.
</margin>
The true knowledge of the shape of any body comes from seeing it from various aspects. Therefore, to provide knowledge of the true shape of any member of man—who is the first beast among the animals <note>original: "prima bestia infralli animali"; this reflects Leonardo’s view of man as a biological entity within the animal kingdom, though superior in complexity.</note>—I shall observe the aforementioned rule, making 4 demonstrations of each member from its 4 sides. And for the bones, I will make 5, sawing them through the middle and showing the cavity of each of them, of which one is medullary <note>containing marrow</note>, another spongy, or hollow, or solid.

<margin>
Upper limb.
Biceps. Its insertion.
</margin>
[Fig. 15:] interior side *a b*
First make this demonstration with the bone called the furcula <note>original: "furchola"; literally "little fork," referring here to the clavicle or collarbone.</note>, and then redo it alongside that one without the furcula, that is, just as this one is.

# ON ANATOMY — FOLIO A — 1 VERSO

<margin>
Pronation and supination; the position of the bones.
</margin>

*a*, sinew <note>original: "nerbo"; Leonardo uses this term interchangeably for nerves, tendons, and sinews.</note> or cord, joins with *b*, muscle.

[Fig. 16:] *a b*

The arm <note>Referring to the assembly of the lower arm marked "B" in Leonardo's diagram.</note>, which has two bones <note>The radius and the ulna.</note> interposed between the hand and the elbow, will be somewhat shorter when showing the palm of the hand to the earth <note>downward (pronation)</note> than when showing it to the sky <note>upward (supination)</note>, when the man is standing with his arm extended. And this happens because those two bones, in showing the palm of the hand to the earth, come to intersect each other in such a way that the one that begins on the right side of the elbow goes to the left side of the palm of the hand, and the one that begins on the left side of the elbow ends at the right side of the palm of that hand.

The bone *a-b* <note>The radius.</note> makes exactly a half-turn when the palm of the hand, which was facing the sky, is turned toward the earth.

[Fig. 17:] *a b d c e*

That line penetrates less deep <note>This likely refers to the visual foreshortening of the arm's length in a drawing.</note> when it is placed in a position of greater obliquity.

---

<margin>
Pronation and supination; the position of the bones.
</margin>

*a, sinew or cord, joins with*
*b, muscle.*

[Fig. 16:] *a b.*

The arm, which has the two bones interposed between the hand and the elbow, will be somewhat shorter showing the palm of the hand to the earth than showing it to the sky, while the man stands with his arm extended. And this happens because those two bones, in showing the palm of the hand to the ground, come to intersect each other so that the one that originates at the right side of the elbow goes to the left side of the palm of the hand, and the one that originates at the left side of the elbow ends at the right side of the palm of that hand.

The bone *a-b* makes exactly a half-turn when the palm of the hand, which was facing the sky, turns toward the earth.

[Fig. 17:] *a b d c e.*

That line penetrates less deep when it is placed in a position of greater obliquity.

<meta>This page continues Leonardo's anatomical studies of the upper limb. While the previous page focused on the mechanical rotation of the forearm (pronation and supination), this page outlines his methodology for illustrating joints and provides a precise census of the bones in the arm and hand. # LEONARDO DA VINCI

<margin>
Method for the study of the bones of the arm and their joints.
</margin>
You shall make each of these 4 demonstrations in duplicate, and from this duplication you shall make one of every kind in which the heads of the bones are joined with their corresponding parts, just as nature made them; and the other demonstration you shall make with the bones separated, and by this means you will see the true shape <note>original: "figura"; refers to the three-dimensional form or morphology.</note> of the heads of the bones that join together.

<margin>
Bones of the upper limb.
</margin>
[Fig. 18:] internal view <note>original: "dimesticha"; an archaic anatomical term used by Leonardo to indicate the medial or inner side of a limb, closer to the body.</note>.
The arm <note>In this context, Leonardo uses "arm" to refer to the entire upper extremity from the shoulder to the fingertips.</note> is composed of 30 pieces of bone, for there are 3 in the arm itself and 27 in the hand. <note>The 3 bones are the humerus, radius, and ulna; the 27 hand bones consist of 8 carpals, 5 metacarpals, and 14 phalanges.</note>

---

<margin>
Normal position of the palm of the hand.
</margin>
The ordinary position of the palm of the hand is to be turned toward the horizon, and its ordinary extremes are to be turned toward the sky or toward the earth—that is, toward the head or toward the soles of the feet of the one who carries it.

---

<margin>
Method for the study of the bones of the arm and their joints.
</margin>
You shall make each of these 4 demonstrations in duplicate, and of such a duplication make one of every kind, in which the ends of the bones are joined to their correspondences, just as nature made them; and the other demonstration you shall make with the bones disjoined, and by this means you will see the true shape of the ends of the bones, which join together.

<margin>
Bones of the upper limb.
</margin>
[Fig. 18:] internal view.
The arm is composed of 30 pieces of bone, of which 3 are in the arm itself and 27 are in the hand.

<margin>
Normal position of the palm of the hand.
</margin>
The ordinary position of the palm of the hand is to be turned toward the horizon, and its ordinary extremes are to be turned toward the sky or toward the earth, that is, toward the head or toward the soles of the feet of the person who carries it.

---

<margin>
Method for the study of the bones of the arm and their joints.
</margin>
You will make each of these 4 demonstrations in duplicate, and of this duplication you will make one for each species, in which the heads of the bones will be joined with their counterparts, just as nature made them; and you will make the other demonstration with the bones separated, and by this means you will see the true shape of the heads of the bones, which join together.

<margin>
Bones of the upper joint.
</margin>
[Fig. 18:] internal part.
The arm is composed of 30 pieces of bone, for there are 3 in the said arm, and 27 in the hand.

<margin>
Normal position of the palm of the hand.
</margin>
The habitual situation of the palm of the hand is to be turned toward the horizon, and its ordinary extreme situations are to be turned toward the sky or toward the earth, that is to say, toward the head or toward the feet of the one who carries it.

<summary>Leonardo describes his scientific method for illustrating anatomy, advocating for showing joints both assembled and "exploded" to reveal their surfaces. He also provides an accurate count of the bones in the human arm and hand (30 total) and defines the neutral and extreme rotations of the palm.</summary>
<keywords>osteology, skeletal system, anatomical illustration, joint morphology, bone count, hand anatomy, humerus, radius, ulna, palm rotation</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, where Leonardo established the method for representing skeletal joints both joined and separated to understand their mechanics, this page applies that methodology specifically to the shoulder girdle. He outlines a systematic "layering" approach to anatomical dissection and illustration.</meta>

# Anatomical studies of the shoulder and arm

Every muscle has its true direction of its motion through the line of its greatest tension—that is, through the middle of its thickness; and you must do the same for every tendon <term>nervo: Leonardo frequently uses the word for 'nerve' to describe tendons, ligaments, or any cord-like fiber that exerts mechanical force</term> that pulls, such as the tendons of the muscles, and likewise for muscles perforated by tendons, such as that of the chest <note>the pectoralis major</note> which passes through the tendon of the shoulder muscle.

---

<margin>
To give a true knowledge of the shoulder and its joint, you must proceed in this way: first remove the skin, then the first muscles, then the second ones, and so on, step by step, until you come to the bare bone structure of that shoulder; and you shall do the same for all the members of the body.
</margin>

<note>Anatomical drawing of the shoulder joint from a superior/posterior perspective, showing the ligaments connecting the acromion and the humerus.</note>

Wishing to provide knowledge of the true locations of the muscles, it is necessary to strip away all flesh down to the bones; in this way, you will see the true boundaries of their origins <note>original: nancimêti, literally 'births' or points of inception</note> and endings, and where they attach to the bones, limb by limb.

<note>Anatomical drawing of the clavicle and humerus in isolation, showing the points of attachment for tendons.</note>

<note>Anatomical drawing of the deeper layers of the shoulder, illustrating the "nerves" (tendons) that stabilize the joint.</note>

If you wish to provide true knowledge of the muscles of a man, you must represent them in seven demonstrations, as you will see below for the shoulder. In the first demonstration, you will see the bare bone, stripped of its covering, without muscles, except for those small ligaments <note>têtino: refers to the connective tissues or "stay-wires" that hold the joint in place</note> that keep the bone firm at its joint. The second will show how the muscles are attached by bare tendons—by bare tendons over the said bones, without the fleshy muscles, for which these tendons serve as the beginning and end. The third will be of the thin muscles through which the tendons pass, as occurs in the two muscles of the chest.

<margin>
Demonstration of the bones and their tendons without the muscles.
</margin>

<summary>Leonardo outlines his revolutionary "layering" method for anatomical study, proposing seven successive stages of dissection to reveal how muscles and tendons interact with the skeleton. He emphasizes that muscle force acts along the central line of its greatest thickness, treating the body as a complex mechanical system of pulleys and levers.</summary>

<keywords>anatomy, shoulder joint, dissection method, tendons, biomechanics, pectoralis, scapula, humerus, muscle tension</keywords>

### ON ANATOMY — SHEETS A — 2 RECTO

<margin>Relationship of the movements of the limbs with their shape.</margin>
Which are the limbs of man that increase or decrease through their bending? And which are those that, while increasing in one part, decrease in another? And which are those that, while decreasing in one part, increase in another? <note>Leonardo is investigating the conservation of volume in muscles; as a muscle contracts and grows thicker (increases), it also grows shorter (decreases).</note>

When *m* approaches *f*, the pivot of the foot *a* stays still, and *o* moves away from that *f*.

<margin>Sensory expressions and muscles.</margin>
The <term>sensory expressions<note>original: "sentimenti"; in this context, Leonardo refers to the physical manifestation of exertion, force, or intent visible in the body</note></term> that use force must be made more evident in their muscles than those that do not use such forces. <note>This is a classic piece of advice for artists: to show a body in action, one must emphasize the muscles that are actively working.</note>

<margin>Biceps.</margin>
[Fig. 22 :] shoulder *o a* humerus <term>omero<note>upper arm bone</note></term> *n* bone *h r b n a*

<meta>This page continues from Leonardo’s previous discussion on the shoulder, specifically detailing the deltoid and the scapula. # ON ANATOMY — SHEETS A — 2 RECTO

space | **S c** attaching itself only to the extremities of the <term>scapula<note>original: "padella della spalla", literally "pan of the shoulder," referring to the flat blade of the shoulder</note></term> ———

<margin>Infraspinatus muscle.</margin>
**n**, muscle, passes between **d m** and occupies the space **f g**, stopping with its extremities at the extremities of the space that receives it ———

<margin>Attachment of the muscles in relation to their function.</margin>
The aforementioned muscles are not fixed, except at the ends of their <term>receptacles<note>original: "ricettachuli", likely referring to the muscle sheaths or the anatomical spaces they occupy</note></term> and at the ends of their <term>tendons<note>original: "corde", literally "cords"</note></term>; and the <term>Master<note>Leonardo refers to Nature or the Creator as "il maestro"</note></term> has done this so that the muscles might be free and unhindered to be able to thicken and shorten, and thin and lengthen, according to the necessity of the part moved by them ———

<margin>The human body: an admirable work of nature.</margin>
<margin>The destruction of life is nefarious.</margin>
[Right margin:] And you, man, who considers in this labor of mine the wonderful works of nature, if you judge it a nefarious thing to destroy it, now think it a most nefarious thing to take the life of a man; for if this, his composition, appears to you a marvelous artifice, consider that this is as nothing compared to the soul that dwells within such architecture. And truly, whatever it may be, it is a divine thing; let it dwell in its work at its own pleasure, and do not let your wrath or malice destroy [it...]

<summary>Leonardo describes the functional mechanics of shoulder muscles like the infraspinatus, explaining how their attachments allow for contraction and extension, before offering a powerful moral argument against the destruction of human life, which he views as a divine architecture for the soul.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, shoulder mechanics, infraspinatus, scapula, muscle function, philosophy of the soul, sanctity of life, nature as master</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the philosophical reflection begun on the previous page. While the preceding text admired the "marvelous artifice" of the human body and the soul dwelling within it, this page warns against the destruction of such a life and transitions into practical, albeit skeptical, advice on maintaining health and the role of doctors.</meta>

# LEONARDO DA VINCI — ON ANATOMY — SHEETS A — 2 RECTO

...wickedness should destroy such a life; for truly, he who does not value it does not deserve it.

Since [the soul] departs so unwillingly from the body, I truly believe that its weeping and sorrow are not without cause. <note>Leonardo often personified the soul, suggesting it grieved when forced to leave the "admirable instrument" of the body he so meticulously dissected.</note>

<margin>Preserving health.</margin>
And strive to preserve your health; you will be all the more successful in this the more you keep away from <term>physicians<note>original: "fisici". In the Renaissance, "physicians" were university-trained doctors who often relied on ancient texts and complex drug recipes rather than observation.</note></term>.

For their concoctions are a kind of <term>alchemy<note>original: "archimia". Leonardo uses this term here disparagingly to describe the chemical mixing of drugs which he found unscientific and potentially harmful.</note></term>, of which there are no fewer books than there are on medicine.

<summary>Leonardo concludes a poignant reflection on the sanctity of human life and the soul's attachment to the body, followed by a sharp warning to maintain health by avoiding the questionable medical treatments of his time.</summary>
<keywords>soul, sanctity of life, health, physicians, alchemy, medical skepticism, anatomy</keywords>

<note>This page features a collection of masterfully rendered anatomical studies. Leonardo frequently chose elderly subjects for these dissections because the lack of subcutaneous fat made the underlying muscular and skeletal structures far more visible and easier to document.</note>

<note>The drawings on this page (numbered 26 through 32 in this scholarly edition) demonstrate Leonardo's progression from surface anatomy to deep mechanical analysis:

**Studies 26 & 27:** These profile views of an elderly man’s head and neck focus on the <term>sternocleidomastoid<note>The large muscle extending from the collarbone to the base of the skull, responsible for turning and tilting the head.</note></term> and the tendons of the throat. Leonardo captures the way the skin stretches over these structures during movement.

**Study 28:** A rear view showing the muscles of the <term>scapula<note>The shoulder blade.</note></term> and their connection to the spine, essential for understanding the lifting power of the arm.

**Study 29:** A central, three-quarter view of the torso. Leonardo meticulously details the ribcage and the pectoral muscles, showing how they integrate with the complex machinery of the neck.

**Study 31:** A highly analytical diagram where Leonardo represents the muscles as "cords" or "threads." This was a revolutionary technique that allowed him to map the exact "line of force" or pull each muscle exerted on the bone, treating the body as a machine governed by laws of physics.</note>

<summary>This page contains detailed anatomical drawings of an elderly man's neck and shoulder musculature, showcasing Leonardo’s transition from observational sketching to mechanical diagrams that treat muscles as functional "cords."</summary>

<keywords>anatomy, neck musculature, shoulder, elderly man, biomechanics, muscle attachments, scientific illustration</keywords>

Veins. (Precepts.)

Begin the anatomy at the head and finish it at the sole of the feet.
Set down all the outlets that the veins make from the flesh and how they branch between the flesh and its skin; and in this way, you shall place all the veins that emerge between the flesh and the skin.

<meta>This page continues Leonardo's detailed investigation into the mechanics of the human voice, specifically comparing the larynx to a musical instrument. It builds on the previous page's discussion of dissecting the trachea—referred to here as the "rough arteries"—to observe how air compression produces sound.</meta>

# ANATOMY — Folios A. 3 — Recto.

<note>The original mirror-script text by Leonardo is extremely faded in this reproduction, appearing as light ghosting throughout the page. The transcription follows the visible traces of his handwriting, which focus on the mechanism of voice production.</note>

<note>The following text is translated from Leonardo's mirror writing at the top of the page:</note>

Just as the voice is generated in the lips of a whistle, so the human voice is generated between the position of the epiglottis and the position of the whistle, because into such a whistle the air enters through the opening of the <term>rough arteries</term><note>original: "aspre arterie"; a historical term for the trachea or windpipe, named for the ridges of its cartilaginous rings.</note>, which is then compressed by the lips of the said whistle and then expands through the void of the epiglottis.

<note>Text located near drawing 47:</note>

From this, one concludes that the voice is produced only by the striking of compressed air within the mouth of a whistle.

<note>Text located at the bottom of the page:</note>

Let a test of this be made using the <term>rough arteries</term> of deceased men, by blowing through the lungs and squeezing the aforementioned whistles.

---

### Description of Drawings

This plate contains eighteen anatomical studies, numbered 33 through 50, primarily focusing on the larynx and the mechanics of the human voice.

*   **33–36**: Detailed sketches of the larynx and vocal folds from side and back views, showing the cartilaginous structure.
*   **37–39**: Details of the laryngeal cartilages (the thyroid, cricoid, and arytenoid cartilages).
*   **40**: A large, detailed anatomical study of the right human leg (front view), showing the superficial muscles from the hip to the foot.
*   **41**: Front view of the larynx and the upper portion of the trachea.
*   **42–43**: Views from above (looking down) into the <term>glottis</term><note>The opening between the vocal folds</note> and the vocal apparatus.
*   **44–46**: Studies of the epiglottis and its function in closing the airway during swallowing.
*   **47**: A comprehensive drawing of the larynx, trachea, and associated blood vessels and nerves.
*   **48**: Isolated study of the cartilages that form the larynx.
*   **49**: Study of the trachea (windpipe) showing its characteristic ringed structure.
*   **50**: Anatomical study of the trachea in relation to the surrounding anatomy of the throat.

<note>Technical Vocabulary: Anatomy, voice, epiglottis, rough arteries (trachea), whistle (larynx mechanism), larynx, trachea, muscles, leg, glottis.</note>

The page includes detailed anatomical drawings of the throat's cartilaginous structure alongside a study of leg musculature.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s investigation into the mechanics of sound and human anatomy. While the previous page focused on experiments with the lungs and trachea of birds (swans and geese) to understand vocal resonance, this page applies those findings to human phonation and transitions into a detailed study of the lower limb's mechanical structure.</meta>

-> LEONARDO DA VINCI <-

That part of the voice which is generated in the lips of the fissure where the breath emerges will be so much higher in pitch when those lips are close to one another, and so much lower in pitch when they are further apart from one another.

<margin>
Why the anatomy of the lungs in <term>beaks</term><note>original: "bachi"; likely a variant or misspelling of "becchi" (beaks), as Leonardo frequently compared human vocal structures to those of birds like the swan mentioned on the previous page.</note> is necessary for the knowledge of the generation of the voice which is produced in the lips of that voice.
</margin>

<note>Study of the larynx and the mechanisms of the vocal cords</note>

<margin>
These are the cords of that voice which, if they are as close to each other as possible, will cause a very high-pitched voice, and the further apart they are, the lower the voice will be.
</margin>

<note>Anatomical study of the trachea (referred to as "aspre arteria") and its role in vocal pitch</note>

<margin>
It is necessary that the <term>rough artery</term><note>original: "aspre arteria"; Leonardo’s standard term for the trachea or windpipe, named for the "rough" texture of its cartilaginous rings.</note> constricts and lengthens for a high voice, and that it widens and shortens for a low voice; and this shall be proven by reason and then by experience.
</margin>

<note>Detailed anatomical study of the human leg, showing musculature, tendons, and nerves</note>

<margin>
This is the order of the anatomy of a leg from the <term>hip</term><note>original: "galone"; an archaic anatomical term for the hip or thigh area.</note> downwards with all its muscles, <term>attachments</term><note>original: "umerati"; likely referring to the fleshy parts or points of insertion for the muscles.</note>, nerves, veins, and arteries.
</margin>

All the cords of the muscles that serve the movement of the toes pass through the hollow of the instep, and there they are bound by a band of bone made in the manner of a bridge through which the said nerves pass to the said toes.

<note>Internal sectional studies of the throat and larynx at the bottom center</note>

<margin>
The movement of that voice will be without any interval as long as the movement of that trachea is continuous.
</margin>

<margin>
It is necessary to give so much power to the muscles that lengthen the trachea that they can overcome the power of the other muscles that would want to constrict it.
</margin>

<summary>Leonardo examines how the narrowing and widening of the vocal folds (the "lips of the fissure") and the movement of the trachea control vocal pitch. He also provides a detailed mechanical analysis of the human leg, specifically how tendons are held in place by a "bridge" of bone in the foot.</summary>

# ON ANATOMY — FOLIO A — 3 RECTO

<margin>Phonation, swallowing, respiration.</margin>
One cannot swallow and breathe or produce the voice at the same time —
nor can one breathe through the nose and through the mouth at the same time; and this is proven by anyone who wishes to play a <term>zufolo</term> <note>original: "zufolo"; a small flute or whistle</note> or flute with the nose and one with the mouth at the same time.

<margin>Thin voice in the elderly.</margin>
->why the voice in<-
->the elderly becomes thin<-

# LEONARDO DA VINCI — PAGE 72

<margin>Swallowing.
Relations with respiration.</margin>
[Fig. 45:] tongue
Air enters and exits through the mouth **d**; and when food passes over the bridge **d n**, some small particle could fall through the opening **d** and pass through **c**, which would be fatal; but nature has ordered the <term>sacculi</term> <note>original: "sachuli"; small anatomical pouches or sacs</note> **a b**, which receive that particle and hold it in reserve until, by coughing, the wind that exits with force from the lungs by the path **c** swirls around, pushes the drops squeezed from the walls of the sacs **a b** through the path **d**, and thus that harmful matter is snapped out of its location.

<margin>Uvula.</margin>
[Left margin, at the top:] The uvula is the dripstone from which falls the humor that descends from above; it falls by way of the <term>meri</term> <note>original: "meri"; an archaic term for the esophagus</note> to the stomach, and has no reason to go by way of the <term>trachea</term> <note>The windpipe</note> to descend into the <term>spiritual parts</term> <note>original: "lochi spiritualj"; the lungs and respiratory system, so called because they were the seat of the "breath of life" or "vital spirits"</note>.

<margin>Back of the mouth.</margin>
[Fig. 46.]
[Fig. 47.]

<summary>Leonardo describes the mechanical protection of the airway during swallowing, theorizing that "small sacs" catch stray food particles until they can be expelled by coughing. He also identifies the uvula as a "dripstone" that directs fluids toward the esophagus and away from the lungs.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, respiratory system, swallowing, uvula, esophagus, trachea, lungs, coughing mechanism</keywords>

He continues to bridge the gap between mechanical function and anatomical structure.</meta>

<summary>Leonardo outlines his methodology for studying the anatomy of the throat, focusing on the mechanics of whispering, the impossibility of breathing while swallowing, and the origin of the nerves controlling the vocal cords. He emphasizes a "bottom-up" approach to anatomy: understanding individual parts before attempting to visualize the whole system.</summary>

### ANATOMICAL STUDIES OF THE FOOT, NECK, AND SHOULDER

-> LEONARDO DA VINCI <-

Muscles of the **shoulder** and the **arm** between them.

But first, you have shown me many other muscles which must be shown to remove the **errors** <note>Leonardo frequently notes that layered or "exploded" views are necessary to correct the misunderstandings found in earlier anatomical texts</note> of those who wish to see the joints, one upon the other, in order to know the movement of the muscles.

<note>The sketches of the elderly man demonstrate Leonardo’s "character" style—deeply lined faces that allow him to map the tension of the neck muscles (such as the sternocleidomastoid) against the skin. The skeletal drawings of the foot are notable for their precise depiction of the tarsal and metatarsal bones, treated with the same mechanical curiosity as a piece of architecture.</note>

<summary>Leonardo explores the mechanical relationship between skeletal structures and musculature, focusing on the foot's bones and the neck's layered muscles. He argues that a multi-layered visual approach is essential for understanding how a healthy body moves without anatomical error.</summary>

<margin>Circulation of the blood.</margin>
The origin of the sea is the opposite of the origin of the blood, because the sea receives into itself all the rivers, which are only caused by the watery vapors raised into the air; but the "sea of blood" is the cause of all the veins. <note>Leonardo compares the body's blood to the Earth's water, but notes a key difference: rivers flow into the sea, while veins (in his view) flow out from a central "sea" of blood to nourish the body.</note>

The vein is but one, which divides into as many principal branches as there are principal places it must nourish, and these branches go on branching infinitely. ———

<margin>Movements of the neck.</margin>
The neck has 4 movements, of which the 1st is raising, the 2nd lowering the face, the 3rd is turning to the right and left, the 4th is tilting the head to the right and left. <unclear>L[...]</unclear> <note>The text is damaged here; it likely referred to "These."</note> these are mixed movements, that is, raising or lowering the face with one ear near a shoulder, and similarly raising or lowering the face while turned toward one of the shoulders; furthermore, raising or lowering the face turned toward one of the shoulders while holding one eye lower or higher than the other, and this is called a complex <note>original: "moto decomposto," referring to a movement broken down into multiple coordinate axes</note> movement —

and to these such movements will be assigned the <term>cords</term> <note>original: "corde," used by Leonardo to describe tendons or narrow muscle bundles</note> and muscles that are the cause of these movements; and thus, if a man lacks any of these movements due to some wound, it will be possible to understand with certainty which cord or muscle is impeded.

<margin>Movements of the neck.</margin>
The neck has 4 movements, of which the first is to raise the face, the 2nd to lower it, the 3rd to turn to the right and left, the 4th to tilt the head to the right and left; <unclear>L[...]</unclear> these are mixed movements, that is: raising and lowering the face with an ear near the shoulder, and likewise, raising and lowering the face turned toward one of the shoulders, and again, raising and lowering the face turned toward one of the shoulders, having one eye lower or higher than the other, and this is called a complex movement.

And one shall assign to these movements the cords and the muscles which are the cause of these movements, and thus, if one of these movements should be missing in a man, due to some wound, one will be able to understand with certainty which cord or which muscle is hindered.

<margin>
**Movements of the neck.**
</margin>
The neck has 4 movements, of which the first is raising the face, the 2nd is lowering the face, the 3rd is turning to the right and left, the 4th is tilting the head to the right and left; [from these] arise mixed movements, [namely] raising or lowering the face [combined with turning] toward a shoulder, and similarly raising and lowering the [face] turned toward one of the shoulders, and again raising or lowering the face turned toward one of the shoulders while holding one eye [lower or] higher than the other, which is called a decomposed movement <note>Leonardo uses "decomposed" (moto decomposto) to describe complex, multi-axial movements that are combinations of the primary four</note>.

And to these movements, one will assign the <term>cords</term> <note>Leonardo uses "corde" (cords) to refer to tendons or sinews</note> and muscles that are the cause of these movements; thus, if a person lacks any of these mentioned movements, one can judge with certainty [which] muscle [is] impaired.

<margin>
**Movements of the neck.**
</margin>
The neck has 4 movements, of which the first is to raise the face, the 2nd to lower it, the 3rd to turn it to the right and left, the 4th to tilt the head to the right and left; [and from] these arise mixed movements, that is to say, [to] raise or lower the face with an [inclination] toward a shoulder, and similarly, [to raise], turn, and lower the face turned toward one of the shoulders, and again to raise and lower the face turned toward one of the shoulders, having one eye lower or higher than the other, and this is called a decomposed movement.

And one will assign to these movements the cords and the muscles which are the cause of these movements, and thus, if one of these movements happens to be missing in a person, through some injury, one can understand with certainty which cord or which muscle is obstructed.

<summary>This page details the vascular anatomy of the shoulder and categorizes the four primary and various "decomposed" (complex) movements of the human neck. Leonardo emphasizes that by understanding the specific muscles and tendons responsible for these motions, one can diagnose which anatomical structure is damaged when movement is lost.</summary>

from the <term>nape</term> <note>original: "nucha"; referring here to the spinal cord or the posterior neck region</note> and go to end

and go to the chest.

are those that
pull the shoulder
straight, transversely,
and obliquely, and from

from the chest.

The same shall be
said of the nerves and
veins and arteries
that originate
from the nape, and how
they pass underneath
the bones of the shoulders,
and how they enter
into the spinal column and originate
from the brain.

The part that moves the shoulder is that thick muscle <note>The trapezius muscle.</note> that originates from the nape and goes to end upon the tip of the shoulder, and this moves the shoulder upward. And that which moves it forward is that muscle that originates from the chest <note>The pectoralis major.</note>; and that which moves it downward is that which originates from the ribs; and that which moves it backward is that which originates from the spine; and that which moves it transversely is that muscle which is under the shoulder—that is, between the shoulder and the ribs—and this same muscle also serves the downward movement. And that which moves it obliquely is that muscle which is placed above the shoulder and originates from the spine.

<column-break/>

That which moves
the arm upward
is the muscle of the
shoulder <note>The deltoid muscle.</note>, and that which
moves it downward
is the muscle
of the chest.

Therefore, you shall make
ten demonstrations of
shoulders and arms and chests,
and you will account for
the reason in every
movement of every limb.

You will also note how
the neck moves and how
its muscles are
placed one above the other.

# ON ANATOMY — FOLIO A — 4 VERSO

<sig>4*</sig>

->FOLIO 4 — verso.<-

<margin>
True knowledge of figures.
</margin>

The true knowledge of all figures is possessed by knowing their width, length, and depth; therefore, if I record the same regarding the figure of man, I shall provide true knowledge of it to every sound intellect.

<note>Leonardo is reminding himself to revise the following sentence for clarity.</note>
Comment on these words, because they are confused.

[Fig. 71.]

<margin>
Placement of the muscles of the neck and the <term>thorax<note>The chest or upper part of the trunk.</note></term>.
</margin>

Make it twice as large, with the same thickness of ribs and muscles, and it will be more intelligible.

Furthermore, this figure would be confusing if you did not first make at least three demonstrations <note>Leonardo uses the term "demonstrations" to refer to a series of progressive anatomical drawings.</note> before this one, using similar <term>threads<note>original: "fili". Leonardo developed a system of drawing muscles as cords or wires to show their points of attachment and direction of pull without hiding the bones underneath.</note></term>; of which demonstrations, the first must be of the simple bones; then follow with the muscles that arise above the ribs in the breast <note>original: "tetta". In this context, Leonardo refers to the pectoral or mammary region of the chest.</note>, and finally the muscles that arise...

# ANATOMY — Folio A. 4 — Verso

...arise from the thorax along with its ribs, and finally this one here above.
Make the ribs so thin that, in the last demonstration made with <term>threads<note>original: "fili". # LEONARDO DA VINCI — ANATOMY — Folio A
Page 83

<note>Anatomical studies of the shoulder, neck, and brachial plexus. Leonardo uses mirror writing; the transcription below reflects the text as read normally left-to-right.</note>

<margin>
The muscles that move the arm are many, and one must know which and how many are those that lift it up, and which and how many are those that pull it down.
</margin>

To show the joining of the nerves <note>original: "nervure". In Leonardo’s time, the distinction between nerves, tendons, and ligaments was not always clear; he often used this term for any cord-like structure.</note> by way of knots and loops <note>original: "bichi". Likely referring to the way tendons or nerves loop around structures like pulleys.</note> through which they slip, passing through those openings that arise in the <term>nape<note>original: "nucha". The back of the neck where the spinal nerves emerge.</note></term> behind the sight of the eyes; and in the nerves the loops run through which the muscles contract into themselves.

<note>Top right: Study of the brachial plexus and neck muscles in profile.</note>

Make sure the anatomy is [represented] by way of knots, because this is the true way of teaching the true form of these muscles; because from behind they are confused with one another, and if you make one muscle first and then the others, you will always see its true figure and you will learn from every side how thick and how thin it is, and where it arises and where it attaches.

---

<margin>
That muscle of the arm which I have attached to the bone of the <term>humerus<note>original: "omero". The long bone of the upper arm.</note></term>.
</margin>

<note>Center: Skeletal and muscular study of the shoulder joint (scapula, humerus, clavicle).</note>

<margin>
Through this demonstration, it is clearly seen what pulls the shoulder down from behind, and one sees what turns it from behind toward the front and from the front toward the back.
</margin>

It cannot be by a single muscle, [it] cannot be, and the loops of the arm.

<note>Lower right: Figure in profile looking down, showing shoulder and arm musculature.</note>

The muscles which pull the arm upward are four, and of these, two are in the chest; and this reveals the muscles that pull the arm backward are those of the shoulder, and those that pull the said arm forward are those of the chest, and that which pulls the said arm downward toward the ribs from behind, and the muscle of the chest on the part under the arm which attaches to the middle of the muscle of the arm.

<note>Bottom left: Detailed study of the chest wall and shoulder muscles.</note>

But one must know the muscles that move the arm in a circle; these come about by reason that they pull that muscle which turns the arm backward, and that which turns it forward concur together toward the top by way of the loop of the arm itself, which turns backward when it goes forward and forward when it goes backward.

<note>The sketches numbered 89 and 90 provide a profile view of an elderly man's head and neck. Leonardo meticulously renders the <term>sternocleidomastoid<note>The thick muscle that runs along the side of the neck, responsible for rotating and tilting the head.</note></term> and the various cords and tendons that become prominent in old age. # ANATOMY — Sheet A <note>original: "ANATOMIA _Fogli A."</note>
## 6 Recto

<note>This plate contains a collection of anatomical studies reproduced from Leonardo's original manuscripts. There is no legible mirror script <note>Leonardo frequently used "mirror script," writing from right to left, which is most easily read when held up to a mirror.</note> to transcribe on this specific plate; the annotations are printed reference numbers (93 through 102) added by the 19th-century editors for identification.</note>

### Description of Illustrations

- **93, 94**: Small diagrams illustrating the junction and branching of vascular tubes or vessels <term>vessels: the tubes, such as veins and arteries, that circulate blood through the body</term>.
- **95**: A prominent and detailed study of the superficial venous system of the human thorax <term>thorax: the chest area</term> and right arm. Leonardo depicts the complex, tree-like branching of the veins with great care, likely seeing a connection between the flow of blood and the branching patterns found in water and plants.
- **96**: A focused study of a major vessel and its lateral branching patterns.
- **97**: A small, rough sketch, possibly representing a landscape or a minor anatomical detail.
- **98**: A profile study of the head of an elderly person. The figure wears a tight-fitting hood or head-cloth, and Leonardo uses the sketch to emphasize the deeply wrinkled skin and the distinctive physical markers of old age.
- **99**: An anatomical study of the superficial veins in a human arm, placed near the profile in 98 to show the relationship between the surface of the body and the vessels beneath.
- **100**: A study of the musculature of a right arm, detailing the long muscles and tendons from a side perspective.
- **101**: A study of the deep muscles of the upper torso, shoulder, and neck.
- **102**: A dynamic anatomical drawing showing the intense contraction and tension of the muscles in a limb (either an arm or a leg), capturing the body in a state of physical exertion.

- Anatomy <note>original: "anatomia"</note>

- Arm <note>original: "braccio"</note>
- Torso <note>original: "torso"</note>
- Profile <note>original: "profilo"</note>
</note>

It also includes a characteristic profile study of an elderly person, showcasing Leonardo's interest in the effects of aging on human anatomy.</summary>

<note>This page is a facsimile plate containing several anatomical studies by Leonardo da Vinci. The numbers (83 through 102) are modern archival annotations used by scholars to categorize individual sketches within the sheet. The drawings are rendered in a blue-line format typical of late 19th-century scientific publications of Leonardo's work.</note>

### Description of the Studies

**The Vascular and Nervous Systems (Figures 95 and 96)**
These drawings show a human torso with a focus on the branching "tree" of the vascular or nervous system. Leonardo was fascinated by how vessels <term>vessels: the tubes, such as veins and arteries, that transport blood</term> and nerves branched out through the body, often comparing them to the roots and limbs of trees or the flow of river systems. In **Figure 95**, the intricate network is shown across the chest and into the right arm.

**Character Study (Figure 88)**
In the upper right, Leonardo includes a profile of an elderly man wearing a hood. These "character heads" often appear alongside his scientific work. Here, he captures the effects of aging on the facial structure, including the sagging of the skin and the sharpening of the features, which he studied as part of his interest in physiognomy <term>physiognomy: the study of facial features as a reflection of character or health</term>.

**Musculature of the Arm and Shoulder (Figures 89, 91, 100, 101, and 102)**
The majority of the right side of the page is dedicated to the mechanics of the human arm.
- **Figures 100 and 101** show the superficial muscles of the shoulder and upper arm, illustrating how they wrap around the bone to provide strength and rotation.
- **Figure 102** and **89** provide deeper views, likely following a dissection, to show the underlying muscles and the way tendons attach to the joints. Leonardo’s goal was to understand not just what the arm looked like, but how the "machinery" of the body functioned to create movement.

<note>There is no handwritten text by Leonardo on this specific reproduction plate; the primary content is purely visual and intended for anatomical instruction.</note>

<keywords>anatomy, vascular system, musculature, shoulder, arm, character study, physiognomy, Renaissance science, dissection</keywords>

<note>This facsimile plate contains detailed notes on the circulatory system and its connection to the limbs. Leonardo’s handwriting flows around his sketches, providing a scientific explanation for the "tree" of veins he has illustrated.</note>

### On the Branching of the Veins

<note>Anatomical study of the muscles of the neck, shoulder, and arm of an elderly man, seen from the front and side.</note>

The vein that is in the middle of the shoulder and the neck divides into two branches: one goes to the arm and the other to the brain and to the root of the tongue. It goes under the chest bone <term>chest bone: the sternum</term> and then sends branches to the lung and to the heart <note>original: "core"</note>; and this is what sends nourishment to the whole body.

---

<note>Detailed study of the veins on the torso and right arm, and a lateral view of the arm's musculature.</note>

From this vein that comes from the heart <note>original: "chore"</note>, the vein that goes to the brain, the one that goes to the arm, and the one that goes to the root of the tongue are divided. Then, it extends among the muscles of the arm and later passes above the elbow in that part which is called the **vein of the heart** <term>vein of the heart: historically known as the basilic vein, which medieval and Renaissance doctors believed had a direct connection to the heart</term>. From there, it descends as far as the fingers of the hand; it is this which nourishes the entire arm and is made of the same substance from which the whole body originates. <note>Leonardo often used the word "vein" to refer to both veins and arteries, as the modern understanding of the circulatory system was still evolving.</note>

<note>A small schematic diagram in the middle-right shows the branching of the vascular system from the heart, which Leonardo has labeled "chore" (heart).</note>

### Key Anatomical Terms
*   **Vein** <note>original: "uena"</note>
*   **Heart** <note>original: "chore"</note>
*   **Arm** <note>original: "braccio"</note>
*   **Brain** <note>original: "ceruello"</note>
*   **Lung** <note>original: "polmone"</note>
*   **Muscles** <note>original: "muscoli"</note>
*   **Nourishment** <note>original: "nutrimento"</note>
*   **Anatomy** <note>original: "anatomia"</note>

He describes the veins as the primary source of "nourishment" for the body, reflecting the medical theories of his time.</summary>

<keywords>vascular system, heart, veins, basilic vein, anatomy, Renaissance medicine, muscular structure, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

The basilic vein and its branches.

[Fig. 96:] *m e a b c n o g*. The basilic vein.
From vein *m e*, vein *e b* branches off, which descends from *e* to *b*, where the arm <note>marked with the Greek letter β in the drawing</note> separates from the chest. From this point *b*, a branch separates and ramifies between the skin and the flesh of the breast. Opposite this, branch *b* <note>Leonardo uses the same letter for a different branch in his diagram</note> arises, which ramifies between the flesh and the skin of the shoulder blade <term>scapula|Leonardo uses the phrase "paletta della spatola"</term>. Below these arises vein *c*, which ramifies between the flesh and the skin covering the ribs at the side. Somewhat further down arises vein *o*, which enters between the muscle <term>biceps|Leonardo calls this the "fish of the arm" (pessce del bracco)</term> and its skin; the main vein from which these ramifications depart is called the basilic.

# LEONARDO DA VINCI

...above the bone, and that, besides seeing the beginning and the end, it should be demonstrated in what way it moves the bone to which it is dedicated; and of this, a scientific reason should be provided with simple lines <note>Leonardo often used "simple lines" to refer to schematic or geometric diagrams that demonstrate the mechanical forces of muscles, treating the body like a machine.</note>.

<margin>Movements of the scapula <note>original: "spatola" / "omoplate," referring to the shoulder blade</note>.</margin>
[To the left of Fig. 104:] Describe the extreme movements of the edges of the scapula, that is, the movements between up and down and to the right and left.

And do the same for every movement of any limb.

# ANATOMY — Folio A. 6, Verso

<note>This page consists of a series of line-art reproductions of Leonardo da Vinci's anatomical studies focusing on the human arm and shoulder musculature. These drawings are likely from a scholarly facsimile edition such as the Sabachnikoff-Piumati (1898) publication of "The Manuscripts of Leonardo da Vinci of the Royal Library of Windsor: On Anatomy, Folios A."</note>

103
<note>Small diagram at the top right, possibly showing a cross-section of the neck or the base of the skull, which Leonardo often studied to understand the connection between the brain and the spinal cord.</note>

104
<note>Detailed study of the back-and-side view <note>posterior-lateral aspect</note> of the right arm, illustrating the deltoid (the large muscle covering the shoulder joint), the triceps brachii (the muscle on the back of the upper arm), and the muscles of the forearm extending toward the hand.</note>

105
<note>Central anatomical study showing the musculature of the shoulder and upper arm from a side-rear perspective.</note>

106
<note>Anatomical study of the upper arm and forearm, emphasizing the muscular structure and how they attach at the elbow joint.</note>

107
<note>Minor sketch or detailed study of a specific muscle group or a joint articulation <note>the way bones meet to allow movement</note>.</note>

108
<note>Horizontal study of the upper arm and shoulder musculature, showing the arm in an abducted position <note>raised out to the side, away from the body</note>.</note>

The breathing <note>The "breathing" or passage of air here refers to the way light and space interact with the depressions between muscles to define their shape</note> of the air in the gaps of some muscles makes the said muscles appear more swollen, and in others, the gaps recede and appear more bony, as happens with the muscles that thicken in such a limb even without motion.

In this demonstration of straight arms, the origins <note>The "nasicmẽti" or births of the muscles</note> of the muscles are learned; these are the bones in which the muscles are seen with such a variety of forms and positions, and as much difference as that which exists between the upper part and the lower, and from the inner part to the outer.

---

These limbs are as varied as the variety of the muscles by which the bones—to which the muscles are attached—are pulled. And this is done by the voluntary and natural <note>structural or involuntary</note> muscles.

The principle <note>original: "ragiõ"</note> of the muscle is this: that when it is pulled <note>contracted</note> by its function, it thickens, and through its thickening, it causes the limbs that are pulled by its motion to bend and extend; and when it is pulled, it pulls the muscles that are near it, and causes the neighboring muscles to place themselves in a great variety [of shapes].

<summary>Leonardo explores the mechanical principles of muscle contraction, noting how muscles thicken to move bones and how their visual appearance is affected by light and foreshortening. This page contains several detailed sketches of the deltoid and biceps from various angles to illustrate these mechanical and artistic truths.</summary>

# ON ANATOMY — FOLIO A — 6 VERSO

---

<sig>6</sig>

[Fig. 108 :] a b f

<margin>
Rules for the study of the arm.
</margin>

This arm, from the elbow *a* to *b*, should be depicted in 4 movements: that is, at its maximum height and its maximum depth, and as far back as possible, and likewise forward; and if it seems right to you to do it in even more ways, the function <note>original: "lofitio" (l'uffizio), meaning the specific task or mechanical role</note> of each muscle will be more understandable. And this will be good for sculptors <note>original: "statuari"</note>, who must emphasize the muscles that cause the movements of the limbs more than those that are not employed in such a motion. —

Represent the arm from the shoulder to the elbow when it makes a circular motion, by keeping the shoulder fixed against the wall and moving the hand with a piece of charcoal around that shoulder, holding the arm straight; and by such a circular motion, you will show all the movements of the muscles that move the shoulder. But represent this shoulder from the spine of the back to the thorax <note>The rib cage or chest cavity</note> of the chest. —

---

<margin>
Rules for the study of the arm.
</margin>

[Fig. 108 :] a b f.

This arm, from the elbow *a* to *b*, should be rendered in 4 movements: that is, at the highest elevation and at the lowest depression, and as far back as possible, and likewise forward; and if you see fit to do it in more ways, the function of each muscle will be more intelligible. And this will be useful for sculptors, who must pronounce <note>To "pronounce" here means to make more visible or prominent in the sculpture</note> the muscles that are the cause of the limbs' movements more than those that are not used in that specific movement.

Represent the arm from the shoulder to the elbow when it performs a circular motion, by pinning the shoulder to the wall and moving the hand with charcoal around that shoulder, keeping the arm straight; through such a circulation, it will produce all the movements of the muscles that move the shoulder. But render this shoulder from the spine of the back to the thorax of the chest.

<summary>Leonardo provides practical instructions for artists and sculptors on how to study the arm's range of motion to better understand muscular function. He proposes a geometric experiment using charcoal against a wall to map the muscles involved in the shoulder's circular rotation.</summary>
<keywords>arm anatomy, musculature, shoulder rotation, artistic precepts, sculptors, anatomical drawing, biomechanics</keywords>

# ANATOMY — Folio A | Front side

<note>This page contains anatomical studies of the human leg, illustrating the intricate relationship between musculature and bone structure. The drawings are rendered with the precise, fine lines characteristic of Renaissance scientific observation.</note>

### III

<note>This primary sketch shows the musculature of the human leg, extending from the upper thigh down to the ankle and foot. It highlights the definition of individual muscle groups as they would appear when tensioned.</note>

### 109

<note>A diagrammatic detail study focusing on the orientation and layering of muscle fibers. By illustrating how these fibers overlap and weave together, the artist explores how the body generates strength and stability.</note>

### 110

<note>An anatomical study of the skeletal structure of the human foot. The drawing clearly identifies the tarsals <note>ankle bones</note>, metatarsals <note>the five long bones of the mid-foot</note>, and the phalanges <note>the toe bones</note>.</note>

<note>
**Technical Vocabulary:**
*   <term>anatomy</term>: the study of the structure of living things.
*   <term>leg</term>: the lower limb of the human body.
*   <term>foot</term>: the terminal part of the leg upon which a person stands.
*   <term>muscles</term>: tissues that contract to produce movement.
*   <term>bones</term>: the rigid organs that form the skeletal system.
*   <term>metatarsus</term>: the group of five long bones in the middle of the foot, located between the ankle and the toes.
</note>

# ANATOMICAL STUDIES — Folio A | Verso

<note>This page contains anatomical studies of the human leg, illustrating the intricate relationship between musculature and bone structure. The drawings are rendered with the precise, fine lines characteristic of Leonardo's later anatomical period (c. 1510).</note>

### III

<note>Large anatomical study of the muscles of a human right leg, shown from a lateral-posterior perspective. This view from the side and back highlights the large gluteal muscles of the hip, the long muscles of the thigh (such as the biceps femoris), and the powerful calf muscles (the gastrocnemius and soleus). Leonardo pays particular attention to how these muscles anchor around the knee joint to facilitate movement.</note>

### 109

<note>Small schematic diagram to the right of the leg, showing the interlacing pattern of muscle fibers or tendons. It is annotated with small letters 'a' and 'b' to track the path of specific strands.</note>

where one crosses the other,
the other passes underneath
that first strand <note>original: "costa" — literally "rib," but here used to describe a cord-like strand of muscle or tendon</note>
and the other goes to the other side;
they go and thus they interlace
the arrangement. <note>original: "l'ordinamento"</note>

### 110

<note>Anatomical study of the dorsal <note>top</note> surface of a human foot, showing the arrangement of the metatarsals <note>the long bones of the mid-foot</note> and the tendons of the extensor muscles which pull the toes upward.</note>

<margin>Leoni</margin> <note>This is a faint collector's mark in a standard 16th-century script, referring to the sculptor Pompeo Leoni, who organized many of Leonardo’s drawings into albums after the artist's death.</note>

<note>
**Technical Vocabulary:**
*   <term>Anatomy</term>: The scientific study of the structure of the body.
*   <term>Muscles</term>: The bundles of fibrous tissue that contract to produce movement.
*   <term>Metatarsals</term>: The five long bones in the middle of the foot.
*   <term>Dorsal</term>: In anatomy, this refers to the upper side or back of a structure, such as the top of the foot.
*   <term>Interlace</term>: To cross or weave together, a key concept in Leonardo’s understanding of how tendons provide strength without bulk.
</note>

<summary>This page features a detailed anatomical study of the human right leg and foot, focusing on how muscle fibers and tendons interlace to create a functional mechanical system. It demonstrates Leonardo's mature scientific style, combining artistic rendering with schematic diagrams to explain the body's internal "arrangement."</summary>

# Anatomy — Sheets A / 7 — Verso

<note>This page contains a series of anatomical sketches by Leonardo da Vinci, specifically focusing on the musculoskeletal structure of the human foot and ankle. These drawings are from a facsimile edition—a high-quality reproduction—and include modern printed reference numbers (112-122) used by scholars to identify specific details. In the background, you may see faint blue "mirror writing"; this is "show-through" from the ink on the other side of the paper and is not part of the content intended for this page.</note>

---

<note>The drawings on this page illustrate several different perspectives of the foot's anatomy:
- **112, 113, 117:** Side (lateral) and top-side (superior-lateral) views of the foot. Leonardo highlights the tendons of the ankle and the muscles that extend the toes.
- **115, 116, 118:** Top-down (dorsal) views showing how the tendons are distributed across the surface of the foot.
- **119, 120:** Views of the heel (calcaneus) from the rear and the bottom.
- **121:** A detailed study of the bones of the mid-foot (metatarsals) and the toes (phalanges).
- **122:** A view from the inner side (medial) of the ankle joint, showing the arch of the foot.</note>

<note>
This page displays a series of high-quality facsimile anatomical drawings by Leonardo da Vinci. These sketches represent a pinnacle of Renaissance scientific illustration, where artistic precision serves anatomical truth. Leonardo uses these drawings to investigate the structure of the human foot and lower leg through <term>myology</term> (the study of muscles) and <term>osteology</term> (the study of bones). 
</note>

<note>
The drawings on this page illustrate several different perspectives of the foot's anatomy:
- **112, 113, 117:** Side (lateral) and top-side (superior-lateral) views of the foot. Leonardo highlights the tendons of the ankle and the muscles that extend the toes.
- **115, 116, 118:** Top-down (dorsal) views showing how the <term>tendons</term> (strong cords connecting muscle to bone) are distributed across the surface of the foot.
- **119, 120:** Views of the heel (calcaneus) from the rear and the bottom.
- **121:** A detailed study of the bones of the mid-foot (<term>metatarsals</term>) and the toes (<term>phalanges</term>).
- **122:** A view from the inner side (medial) of the ankle joint, showing the arch of the foot and the arrangement of the <term>tarsals</term> (ankle bones).
</note>

Through these drawings, Leonardo explores the complex mechanical relationship between the muscles of the lower leg and the skeletal framework of the foot. One sketch focuses on the dorsal tendons that lift the toes, while others examine the foot's skeletal architecture from various perspectives. He demonstrates his analytical method of layering muscle, tendon, and bone to understand the mechanics of human movement.

<summary>This page features eleven detailed anatomical sketches by Leonardo da Vinci exploring the mechanical and skeletal structure of the human foot and ankle from various perspectives. It highlights his transition from artistic representation to scientific inquiry through the layering of bones, tendons, and muscles.</summary>
<keywords>human anatomy, myology, osteology, foot mechanics, Leonardo da Vinci, tendons, metatarsals, tarsals, skeletal structure</keywords>

# ANATOMY — Sheet A Recto

### f. 35r / 180

<note>This page features several anatomical studies of the human foot and ankle. Leonardo focuses on the "cords" (tendons), muscles, and bones, illustrating how they interact to facilitate movement. The drawings are executed with precise hatching to show the depth and layering of tissue.</note>

<margin>
The tendons <note>original: *le corde*</note> of the toes under the sole.
</margin>

<note>This note describes the process of dissection required to reveal the underlying structures of the heel and sole.</note>

First remove the skin of the heel, and then the sinewy <note>original: *carne nervosa*, referring here to the tough, fibrous connective tissue of the plantar fascia</note> flesh of the sole of the foot, and then you will see how the tendons emerge from it.

<note>Leonardo frequently compared the structures of different parts of the body to find universal mechanical principles. Here, he draws a parallel between the hand and the foot.</note>

Pull a tendon of the corresponding finger of the hand, and it appears that the middle of the hand is like the sole of the foot and the heel.

<note>The following notes are situated near the skeletal and ligamentous studies at the bottom right of the page, where Leonardo examines the "ossitura" or bony framework.</note>

...if you [take] one of the feet, of the toes of the feet...

These show how the toes of the foot have their bone structure <note>original: *ossitura*</note>, and how the said muscles [join] on a tendon to a bone; and the foot of a man is made in this way.

<note>
The illustrations on this page serve as a manual for dissection:
- **Top left:** A view of the Achilles tendon and the calcaneus (heel bone).
- **Top right:** The extensor tendons on the top (dorsal) surface of the foot.
- **Center:** A deep dissection of the sole, showing the branching of the tendons that pull the toes (flexors).
- **Bottom:** Skeletal studies showing the articulation of the tarsal and metatarsal bones.
</note>

<summary>This page documents Leonardo's dissection of the human foot, specifically focusing on the mechanical connection between tendons and bones in the sole and heel. He highlights the structural similarities between the palm of the hand and the sole of the foot to demonstrate universal anatomical principles.</summary>

<keywords>anatomy, human foot, tendons, heel, dissection, bone structure, myology, Leonardo da Vinci, biomechanics</keywords>

# ANATOMY — Sheets A. 8 — Recto.

<note>This page is a facsimile plate from a scholarly edition of Leonardo da Vinci’s anatomical studies known as *Fogli A*. It features high-quality line reproductions of two specific anatomical sketches, labeled with modern reference numbers 123 and 124 for identification.</note>

<note>Anatomical study of a male torso and extended arm, viewed from the side and back.</note>
**123**
<note>This sketch (Figure 123) is a pen and ink study of the male upper torso and an extended right arm. Leonardo focuses on the musculoskeletal structure, likely examining how the deltoid and pectoral muscles shift and define themselves during the extension of the limb. This reflects his method of isolating specific muscle groups to understand their function in different poses.</note>

<note>Anatomical study of a male torso with the arm raised, focusing on the shoulder and neck musculature.</note>
**124**
<note>Figure 124 shows a male figure in profile with the right arm raised. This drawing emphasizes the tension and mechanical changes in the neck and shoulder girdle when the arm is lifted. Leonardo’s goal with these sketches was to create a comprehensive map of human kinesis <note>Greek: *kinesis*; the study of movement</note>, serving as scientific preparation for both his paintings and his formal anatomical treatises.</note>

<summary>This page features two facsimile reproductions of Leonardo da Vinci's anatomical drawings, focusing on the muscular dynamics of the male torso, shoulder, and arm during movement.</summary>
