# ANATOMY Sheets A

->8v<-

<note>This page contains a series of five scientific drawings by Leonardo da Vinci focusing on the human vertebral column <note>the spine</note>. Leonardo's work here is groundbreaking because he was among the first to correctly record the number of vertebrae and the specific curves of the back.</note>

**125**
<note>A view from the back (posterior) of the complete spinal column. It illustrates the sequence of bones from the neck down to the sacrum <note>the shield-shaped bone at the base of the spine</note> and the coccyx <note>the tailbone</note>.</note>

**126**
<note>A view from the side (lateral) of the complete spinal column. This drawing is historically significant for its accurate depiction of the spine’s four natural curves: the neck (cervical), the upper back (thoracic), the lower back (lumbar), and the pelvic (sacral) regions.</note>

**127**
<note>A study of the spinal column oriented horizontally. This perspective allows for a detailed look at the structure of the individual vertebrae <note>the interlocking bones that form the spine</note> and how they stack together.</note>

**128**
<note>A detailed anatomical drawing of the cervical vertebrae <note>the seven bones of the neck</note> focusing on their articulation <note>the way the joints move together</note>.</note>

**129**
<note>A further detailed study of the neck bones, shown from a different angle to illustrate the complex way they support the weight of the head while allowing for rotation.</note>

<summary>This page features Leonardo da Vinci’s revolutionary anatomical drawings of the human spine, providing highly accurate posterior and lateral views that identify the natural curvatures of the vertebral column. These studies represent a transition from medieval theory to modern empirical observation of the human skeletal system.</summary>

# ANATOMY Sheets A

->8v<-

<note>Leonardo continues his groundbreaking investigation into the human skeleton, specifically the vertebral column. He treats the spine not just as a biological structure, but as a masterpiece of engineering and geometry.</note>

The plane of the bone of the back <note>the spinal column</note>
from the back and from the front.
From the side, it is not a plane but is
curved in three places.
The reason for the greater and lesser
[size] of the bones of the back
diminishing or being smaller at so many points.

The constant reason for a straight line
as long as the back,
which is composed of a

O you who look upon this labor of mine, know that the knowledge of all these branches throughout the body will be of extreme confusion to you if first you do not know the beginning <note>the source or root</note> of these branches; and let the example of a tree suffice for you, which, if deprived of its roots, the nature of its branches cannot be well understood.

O you who sculpt man, make the said and the other part of bone so that the man may be well measured and equal in every part. Do not let any part suffer <note>original: "tē pēti" from pentirsi, here meaning to fail or be poorly formed</note> for distorted length, nor let any part suffer for a swollen width, nor let any part suffer for thinned-out thickness, nor let any part suffer for <unclear>straightened</unclear> fineness.

# LEONARDO DA VINCI

<margin>Instructions for the study of the cervical vertebrae.</margin>

[Fig. 129:] a b c n e S t f m h r p x S q

These three <term>vertebrae<note>original: "spondili." From the Greek 'spondylos,' Leonardo uses this traditional term interchangeably with 'ossa del collo' (bones of the neck).</note></term> should be illustrated from three perspectives, just as was done for the three views of the spine.

The vertebrae of the neck are seven, of which the first at the top and the second are different from the other five.

You shall draw these bones of the neck from 3 perspectives while they are joined together, and from 3 perspectives while they are separated. Or rather, you will draw them from two other perspectives: that is, seen from below and from above. In this way, you will provide a true understanding of their shapes, which is impossible for ancient <column-break/> or modern writers to ever provide without an immense, tedious, and confused length of writing and time. But through this very brief method of illustrating them from different perspectives, a full and true understanding of them will be given. And so that this benefit which I give to mankind [may endure], I am teaching the method of <term>reprinting<note>original: "ristamparlo." Leonardo was remarkably forward-thinking, envisioning his notebooks being mass-produced via printing presses to disseminate scientific knowledge.</note></term> them <column-break/> in an orderly fashion. And I pray to you, O successors, that greed does not force you to make the prints in [...] <note>The sentence ends abruptly; he is likely warning against using cheap materials or poor quality engravings to save money.</note>

<summary>Leonardo outlines his revolutionary "multi-perspective" method for anatomical drawing, arguing that visual illustration is far superior to the "tedious" verbal descriptions used by previous scholars. He also expresses a desire for his work to be published and preserved, warning his successors not to compromise the quality of the prints out of greed.</summary>

<keywords>cervical vertebrae, anatomical illustration, scientific method, printing, multi-perspective drawing, anatomical history</keywords>

<note>This page contains a series of anatomical studies of the human leg, focusing on the <term>osteology<note>The study of the structure and function of bones.</note></term> and <term>myology<note>The study of the muscular system.</note></term>. The drawings are part of Leonardo's systematic investigation into the mechanics of the human body. <note>Figures 130–134: A row of five detailed pen-and-ink studies of the bones of the human lower limb. Leonardo illustrates the leg from multiple viewpoints—lateral (side), medial (inside), anterior (front), and posterior (back)—to fully understand the three-dimensional form of the skeleton. The precision in the rendering of the joints, particularly the knee and ankle, reflects his deep interest in human mechanics.</note>

135
<note>Figure 135: A study of the leg bones—the <term>femur<note>thigh bone</note></term>, <term>tibia<note>shin bone</note></term>, and <term>fibula<note>calf bone</note></term>—in a state of flexion (bending). This drawing focuses on how the skeletal structure changes orientation during movement, a key aspect of Leonardo's research into how humans walk.</note>

136
<note>Figure 136: In this diagram, Leonardo uses simple lines or "cords" attached to the skeletal frame to represent the functional pathways of muscles and tendons. This abstraction, often called a "string diagram," allowed him to analyze the mechanical forces required to move the limbs as if they were parts of a machine.</note>

137
<note>Figure 137: A study of the <term>superficial musculature<note>The muscles closest to the surface of the skin.</note></term> of the leg. Leonardo depicts the thigh and calf muscles with fine hatching to show the volume and direction of the muscle fibers.</note>

<note>Figures 138–140: Minor sketches detailing specific bone articulations and cross-sections, including a close-up of the knee joint bones, providing further clarity to the larger studies on the page.</note>

<summary>This page features a comprehensive study of the human leg's anatomy, utilizing multiple perspectives and mechanical "string diagrams" to illustrate how bones and muscles interact during movement. It demonstrates Leonardo's pioneering method of treating the human body as a complex mechanical system.</summary>

## STUDIES OF THE LOWER LIMB

<note>Study of the bones of the leg (femur, tibia, fibula, patella) and feet from five different perspectives (anterior, posterior, medial, lateral).</note>

<margin>
The knee <note>original: "ginocchio"</note>
</margin>

<margin>
Nerve <note>original: "neruo"; in this period, Leonardo often used this term to refer to tendons as well as nerves.</note> from the back part of the leg <note>original: "gāba"</note>.
</margin>

<margin>
This is not among those that have the function <note>original: "offitio"</note> of bending the bone of the leg.
</margin>

Whoever draws the leg of a man from different sides should first make the bones, then the muscles, and then the veins and arteries; and if possible, make the muscles of various kinds <note>referring to different layers or stages of dissection</note> to provide an understanding of their position <note>original: "situatiō"</note> and their functions.

---

<note>Anatomical study of the leg muscles, showing the posterior view of the thigh and calf.</note>

<margin>
Of all the muscles that emerge from under the head of the thigh bone <note>the femur</note> from the back part, none of them are among those that have the function of bending the bone of the leg or the knee.
</margin>

<note>Two diagrams showing the femur, tibia, and fibula in articulation, illustrating the mechanical principles of the leg joints.</note>

<margin>
But there are some among those that have the function of bending the feet from below the heel <note>original: "calcagno"</note>, as does the muscle that pulls the heel to itself <note>likely the gastrocnemius or "calf muscle" and the Achilles tendon</note>, of which we will speak in their proper places.
</margin>

<margin>
You will see here through these four demonstrations how the muscles have their seat <note>original: "sedia"</note> below and above the bone of the thigh to give better knowledge of their function <note>original: "vfitio"</note>.
</margin>

*b c* (the lever) fits twice into *b a*; a counter-lever of 400 pounds <note>original: "libbre"</note> of force at *c* creates a power of 200 pounds at *a*, with the man standing on one foot.

<margin>
Arrangement of the kneecap when a man kneels.
</margin>

(I) The space from the lower limit of the kneecap <note>original: "patella"</note> to the upper part of the thigh bone <note>the femur</note> increases, when a man kneels, by the entire thickness of that kneecap <note>original: "padella"</note>.

Relationship of the kneecap to the muscles.
</margin>

[*Fig. 138 :*] a
The kneecap of the knee *a a* has "powers" <note>forces or points of tension</note>, of which the first is the "fish" of the thigh <note>original: "pessce della cosscia"; likely referring to the rectus femoris muscle belly</note> above it; the second and third are the right and left muscles lateral to the aforementioned <note>the vastus muscles</note>; the fourth is the lower tendon <note>original: "neruo"</note> joined to the kneecap, which originates in the shaft of the leg <note>the tibia</note>. This kneecap has less sensitivity than any other bone in a man.

# ANATOMY — Folio A. 9 — Reverse

<note>The original manuscript text on this page is extremely faint, appearing mostly as ink bleeding through from the front side. It is not legible enough for a definitive translation of Leonardo's own words. The notes below describe the drawings, which have been assigned modern reference numbers for study.</note>

<note>This page consists of a series of anatomical studies of the neck, shoulders, and arms, focusing on muscular structure and surface anatomy. The drawings are executed in pen and ink.</note>

### Anatomical Studies

**Neck and Shoulder Sketches (Top Row):**
- **141**: A sketch of the right shoulder and neck, showing the collarbone <term>clavicle</term> and the large muscle of the upper back and neck <term>trapezius</term>.
- **142**: A partial sketch focusing on the neck and the nape <note>the back of the neck</note>.
- **143**: A detailed study of the neck muscles from a frontal perspective.

**Arm Musculature Studies (Main Row):**
- **147**: A full study of the left arm and shoulder viewed from the side and back <note>lateral and posterior view</note>. It details the shoulder muscle <term>deltoid</term>, the muscle on the back of the arm <term>triceps brachii</term>, and the various muscles of the forearm.
- **146**: A study of the left arm and torso from a rear-angled view <note>posterior-oblique view</note>, showing how the shoulder muscles integrate with the back.
- **145**: A full study of the right arm from a front view <note>anterior view</note>, clearly defining the "two-headed" muscle <term>biceps brachii</term>, the underlying arm muscle <term>brachialis</term>, and the complex musculature of the forearm and hand.
- **144**: A study of the right arm and chest from the front, showing the large chest muscle <term>pectoralis major</term> and its connection to the upper arm bone <term>humerus</term>.

**Geometric Diagram (Bottom Right):**
- **148**: An eight-pointed star diagram, possibly a compass rose or a geometric exercise, containing the reference number.

<note>Faint traces of Leonardo's mirror writing <note>writing that is legible only when held up to a mirror</note> are visible in the background, likely transferred from the opposite page or appearing as bleed-through from the front.</note>

LEONARDO DA VINCI

<margin>Various positions of the arm.</margin> 
I turn an arm in 8 aspects <note>meaning: viewpoints or perspectives</note>, of which 3 are from the outside, 3 from the inside, 1 from the back, and 1 from the front.

[Fig. 148.]

And I turn it another 8 times when the arm has its two <term>focili</term> <note>original: "fucilj." Leonardo uses this term to refer to the two long bones of the forearm: the radius and the ulna.</note> crossed <note>This refers to the action of pronation, where the radius rotates over the ulna.</note>.

<margin>Relationships between the movements of bones, muscles, and tendons.</margin> 
There are as many muscles as there are <term>tendons</term> <note>original: "corde," literally "cords." Leonardo often uses this term for tendons or sinews.</note>, or rather, as many as there are bones that are moved by them.

There are as many divisions of the tendons—and of a single muscle—as there are the specific movements that can be performed by a single bone.

<margin>Movements of the humerus. Muscles of the chest and the shoulder blade.</margin> 
All the muscles originating in the chest and on the outer <term>scapula</term> <note>original: "spatola," meaning the shoulder blade.</note> serve the movement of the <term>humerus</term> <note>original: "aiutorio," an archaic anatomical term for the upper arm bone.</note>, which is the bone positioned between the shoulder and the elbow.

<summary>Leonardo outlines a plan to illustrate the arm from eight different angles to capture its three-dimensional form, specifically noting the changes when the forearm bones cross. He also posits a direct mechanical correlation between the number of tendons/muscles and the variety of bone movements they produce.</summary>

<keywords>anatomy, arm, humerus, scapula, tendons, forearm bones, radius and ulna, biomechanics, anatomical illustration</keywords>

### ON ANATOMY — FOLIOS A — 9 VERSO

<margin>Muscles of the scapulo-humeral girdle.</margin>
But the muscles originating in the anterior part of the <term>scapula</term> <note>original: "spatula," the shoulder blade</note>, part of them serve the motion of the <term>humerus</term> <note>original: "fucile pel sale," an archaic term for the upper arm bone</note> to raise it, and part serve the anchoring of the scapula to the ribs of the chest.

---

<margin>Movements of the forearm. Muscles of the arm.</margin>
All the muscles originating in the bone of the <term>humerus</term> <note>original: "aiuturio," the bone of the upper arm</note> serve the motion of the two <term>forearm bones</term> <note>original: "fucili," referring here to the radius and ulna</note> of the arm.

---

<margin>Extensor muscle of the arm.</margin>
Muscle **n** does not appear clearly defined or contracted unless the arm is extended, because it is the one attached to the extremity of the elbow, created for the purpose of the extension of the arm.

---

<margin>Muscle of flexion.</margin>
Muscle **m** is created for the purpose of bending the arm.

---

<margin>Biceps brachii muscle, rotator and flexor of the forearm.</margin>
And muscle **o** is designed to turn the arm from the elbow downwards, and it turns it whether the arm is extended or bent; and if the hand shows you the <term>outer side</term> <note>original: "parte silvestra," referring to the back or dorsal side of the hand</note>, this muscle then assists the power of muscle **m**.

# ON ANATOMY — FOLIO A — 10 RECTO

### FOLIO 10 — recto.

<margin>Circular movement of the fingers.</margin>
Every finger has in itself a circular motion with its tip; that is, by keeping the hand still in the air, supported upon the thumb <note>original: "dito grosso"</note> on a flat surface, with each fingertip you will describe a circle, because in them there are 4 cords <term>cords: Leonardo uses the word "corde" to refer to the tendons or muscles that pull the bones into motion</term>.

<margin>Coitus.</margin>
The act of coitus and the members employed for it are of such ugliness <note>original: "bruttura"</note> that, were it not for the beauty of the faces and the ornaments of the participants <insert>and</insert> the restrained disposition, nature would lose the human species.

<margin>Abduction <note>The movement of pulling the fingers away from the midline of the hand.</note> of the fingers of the hand.</margin>
[Fig. 149:] a c b n m
Remember to illustrate the cause of the spreading <note>original: "dilatabile"</note> motion of the fingers *a b* and *b c*.

<margin>Instructions for the study of the hand.</margin>
And with a similar rule, describe all the expansions of the other fingers and of the other limbs; and remember that the demonstrations of the inside of the hand must be 10 in total.

### ON ANATOMY — FOLIO A — 10 RECTO. 117

...how they attach to the first bones of the fingers, and how they bend them, and how the nerves penetrate into them.

<margin>
Why he considers only one position of the hand.
</margin>

These 10 demonstrations of the hand would be better presented facing upwards; but I am constrained by the first general demonstration of man <note>Leonardo refers here to his "universal" anatomical drawing of the whole human figure, which established the orientation for the subsequent detailed studies.</note>, which I had to depict with the hands facing downwards. In order not to deviate from that starting principle, I have been moved to make these also facing downwards.

<margin>
Movement of the phalanges <term>phalanges: the bones that make up the fingers and toes</term> of the fingers.
</margin>

I have represented here the cause of movement for the first and second pieces of the finger bones; what is missing is the depiction of the movement of the third, larger bone, which comprises half the length of these fingers. In truth, it is not shown here how that bone moves, unless one refers to the second figure of the second hand.

But I am only left in doubt that its muscles have no tendon <term>tendon: Leonardo uses the word "corda" (cord) to describe the fibrous tissue that connects muscle to bone</term>, nor do they correspond to the fingers of the hand as seen here; and in this case, the foot has demonstrated them very well.

Describe how many <term>panniculi: small membranes or layers of tissue</term> are situated between the skin and the bone of the hand.

If you cross the fingers $c$ and $d$ in such a way that points $a$ and $b$ touch the same object between them, and that object is pressed so firmly that the two fingers feel it, I say that this object will cause a sensation in three places and will seem to be two objects. <note>Leonardo is describing "Aristotle's Illusion." When fingers are crossed, the brain receives tactile information from surfaces that are normally on opposite sides of the hand, leading it to misinterpret a single object as two.</note>

And this happens because point $a$ receives its sensation from the nerve that passes through the elbow, while point $b$ receives its sensation from the nerve that passes through the inner joint of the elbow. <note>Leonardo correctly identifies that the sensory information is carried by different nerve pathways—the ulnar and median nerves—though his specific path descriptions reflect the anatomical knowledge of his time.</note>

### ANATOMY _ Folio A

<note>This page is a plate from a scholarly edition of Leonardo da Vinci's anatomical studies. It contains eleven separate drawings of the anatomy of the human hand and wrist, numbered 149 through 159 for reference.</note>

### Anatomical Studies of the Hand

The drawings on this folio focus on the skeletal structure, ligamentous connections, and the complex system of tendons in the hand.

*   **149**: A schematic diagram of finger <term>phalanges: the bones of the fingers</term> and joints.
*   **150**: Detailed dorsal view <note>original: "visto da sopra" (viewed from above)</note> of the hand, illustrating the extensor tendons, the <term>extensor retinaculum: a thickened band of tissue at the wrist that holds tendons in place</term>, and the interosseous muscles between the bones.
*   **151**: Lateral view of a finger showing the mechanics of tendon attachments.
*   **152**: Dorsal view of the hand, similar to 150, showing the distribution of tendons to the fingers.
*   **153**: Small study of the ligaments of the thumb and index finger bones.
*   **154**: Oblique view of the <term>carpal: wrist</term> and <term>metacarpal: palm</term> bones with their associated ligaments.
*   **155**: Study of the skeletal structure of the thumb and its articulation with the wrist.
*   **156**: Palmar view <note>original: "visto dal palmo" (viewed from the palm)</note> of the hand, highlighting the <term>transverse carpal ligament: also known as the flexor retinaculum</term> and the tendons passing beneath it into the palm.
*   **157**: Side profile of a flexed finger, showing the bones.
*   **158**: A simplified geometric/mechanical diagram of a finger joint.
*   **159**: Detailed skeletal drawing of the hand and the distal portions of the <term>radius: the outer forearm bone</term> and <term>ulna: the inner forearm bone</term>, showing the arrangement of the eight carpal bones.

Describe how many membranes are interposed between the skin and the bone of the hand.

If you interlace the fingers **c** and **d**, so that **a** and **b** touch the same object between them, and this object is made so heavy that the two fingers are pained by it, I say that this object will hurt in three places, and the sensation will only report two.

And this happens because **a** has the sensitivity of the nerve which passes through the elbow, and **b** that of the nerve which passes through the inner joint of the elbow.

[Fig. 155.]

To better demonstrate where the muscles of the palm of the hand attach to the bones, draw them in the form of threads.

**a** is a strong, fleshy muscle, which begins on one of the bones of the arm...

Those vessels that carry nourishment to the muscles of the hand have their roots above the elbow, between the end of the shoulder and the elbow, and there the said vessels have their origin. <note>Leonardo is referring to the brachial artery and its branches which provide the blood supply ("nourishment") to the forearm and hand.</note>

In this demonstration, the nerves intersect one another in such a way that the one that went to the right side of the finger goes to the left side, and the one that went to the left goes to the right.

<note>Study of the dorsal (back) of the hand showing the extensor tendons</note>

<margin>
Muscles that move the fingers of the hand: these muscles all originate in the arm, that is, between the elbow and the joint of the hand.
</margin>

These muscles that are between the joints of the fingers of the hand—which fingers are composed of three bones, except the thumb, which has two bones—these muscles, I say, serve to open and close the fingers of the hand. <note>Leonardo correctly identifies that the fingers have three <term>phalanges: the individual bones of the fingers</term>, while the thumb has only two.</note>

<note>Detailed study of a finger showing tendon attachments</note>

<note>Study of the palmar surface of the hand showing the median and ulnar nerves and flexor tendons</note>

<margin>
The nerves that descend to the palm of the hand are three, and these divide into branches that go to each finger, except the thumb, which has only two. <note>Leonardo identifies the radial, median, and ulnar nerves, though his mapping of their specific branches to the digits varies in accuracy compared to modern medicine.</note>
</margin>

Note how the nerves of the arm divide into the muscles that move the fingers, and how these nerves pass under the <term>carpal ligament: the tough band of tissue forming the "roof" of the carpal tunnel</term>.

The 8 bones of the <term>carpus: the wrist</term> are arranged in two rows, that is, 4 in each row.

These are the bones of the hand seen from the inside, that is, from the side of the palm.

Muscle **a** moves the thumb outward.
Muscle **b** moves the thumb inward.
</margin>

The muscle that moves the thumb is of great strength, and for this reason it is thick and short, and originates in the bone of the wrist. <note>Leonardo is likely describing the <term>adductor pollicis: the powerful muscle that draws the thumb toward the palm</term>.</note>

--- Page 201 ---
<meta>This page follows a discussion on the "thread" method of drawing muscles. Here, Leonardo outlines his systematic plan for a series of eight anatomical demonstrations of the hand, progressing from the skeletal structure to the external skin at different stages of life.</meta>

ON ANATOMY. — FOLIOS A. — 10 VERSO.
9*

->### FOLIO 10 — verso.<-

->#### Illustration of the hand<-

<margin>Precepts for the study of the hand.</margin>

The 1st demonstration of the hand will be made of its simple bones.
The 2nd of the ligaments and various "chaining" of nerves <note>original: "nervi" — in this context, Leonardo likely refers to ligaments or connective tissues that bind bones, as he later uses the same term for sensation.</note> that tie them together.
The 3rd will be of the muscles that originate upon these bones.
The 4th will be of the first tendons <term>corde</term> <note>Leonardo uses the word "corde" (literally "cords") to describe tendons.</note> that rest upon these muscles and go to provide motion to the tips of the fingers.
The 5th will be that which shows the 2nd level of tendons, which move all the fingers and terminate in the penultimate pieces of the bones of the fingers <note>Referring to the insertion of the flexor digitorum superficialis on the middle phalanges.</note>.
The 6th will be that which demonstrates the nerves that provide the sense of touch <note>original: "sentimento" — sensation or feeling.</note> to the fingers of the hand.
The 7th will be that which demonstrates the veins and arteries that provide nourishment and the vital spirit <note>In Renaissance physiology, "spirit" (spirito) was a subtle substance believed to be carried by the blood to animate the body's functions.</note> to the fingers.
The 8th and last will be the hand clothed in skin, and this will be depicted for an old man, a young man, and a child; and for each shall be given the measurements of length, thickness, and width for each of its parts.

---

->### FOLIO 10 — verso.<-

->#### Illustration of the hand.<-

<margin>Precepts for the study of the hand.</margin>

The first demonstration of the hand will be made of its simple bones. The 2nd of the ligaments and various chainings of nerves, which bind them together. The 3rd will be of the muscles, which originate upon these bones. The 4th will be of the first tendons, which rest upon these muscles and go to give motion to the tips of the fingers. The 5th will be that which shows the 2nd level of tendons, which move all the fingers, and terminate in the penultimate pieces of the bones of the fingers. The 6th will be that which demonstrates the nerves that give sensation to the fingers of the hand. The 7th will be that which demonstrates the veins and arteries, which give nourishment and the spirit to the fingers. The 8th and last will be the hand clothed in skin, and this will be depicted from an old man and from a young man and from a child, and to each will be given the measure of length, thickness and width of each of its parts.

---

->### FOLIO 10 — verso.<-

->#### Illustration of the hand.<-

<margin>Precepts for the study of the hand.</margin>

The first demonstration of the hand will be made by its simple bones. The 2nd by its ligaments and the different enchainments of nerves, which relink them together. The 3rd will be with the muscles, which are born on these bones. The 4th with the first tendons, which pose themselves on these muscles and go to carry the movement to the ends of the digits. The 5th will show the 2nd series of tendons, which move all the fingers and which end at the second-to-last pieces of the bones of the fingers. The 6th will demonstrate the nerves, which give sensibility to the fingers of the hand. The 7th will be that which demonstrates the veins and the arteries, which give the food and the spirit to the fingers. The 8th and last will be the hand revested with the skin, and this one we will make of an old man and one of a young man and one of a child, and for each we will give the measure of the length, of the thickness and of the width of each of its parts.

<summary>This folio contains Leonardo's detailed plan for eight sequential anatomical demonstrations of the hand, moving from the "simple bones" to the final layer of skin. He outlines a systematic study including ligaments, muscles, two layers of tendons, nerves for sensation, and blood vessels.</summary>

### LEONARDO DA VINCI

<margin>Inner surface of the fingers.</margin>
Finger viewed from the side that can be touched by the adjacent finger
[Fig. 160 :] *a b c d e*  
[Fig. 161 :] *a b c d e*

<margin>Muscles for the flexion and extension of the fingers. Vessels and nerves.</margin>
*a* . is the tendon <term>chorda</term> <note>Leonardo uses the word "corda" or "chorda" (literally "cord") to describe tendons.</note> that straightens the bent finger  
*e* . is the tendon that bends the straight finger  
*b* . is the vein that nourishes the finger —  
*d* . is the vein <note>In Renaissance anatomy, "vein" was often used generically for blood vessels; here it refers to an artery carrying "vital spirits."</note> that gives the vital spirits to said finger —  
*c* . is the nerve that gives feeling <term>sentimento</term> and which, if it were cut, the finger would feel nothing even if it were in a fire; and for this, careful nature provided by placing them between one finger and the other so that they would not be cut —

### Transcription and Translation of Leonardo's Notes

#### Italian Transcription (Diplomatic)
<note>This section represents the literal transcription of Leonardo's original 15th-century Italian mirror-writing.</note>

> finger viewed from that side | which can be touched | by the adjacent finger
> [Fig. 160] a b c d e
> [Fig. 161] a b c d e
> 
> a . is the cord that straightens | the bent finger
> c . is the cord that | bends the straight finger.
> b . is the vein that | nourishes the finger —
> d . is the vein that gives | the vital spirits to this | finger. —
> e . is the nerve that gives | the feeling etc. | which being cut the finger
> feels nothing even if it were in the fire and for this | diligent nature
> provided to put it between one finger and the other | so that it would not be cut/

#### French Translation

> Finger seen from the side by which it can be touched
> by the lateral finger.
> [Fig. 160] a b c d e
> [Fig. 161] a b c d e
> 
> a is the tendon that straightens the bent finger.
> c is the tendon that bends the extended finger.
> b is the vein that supplies the finger.
> d is the vein that gives the vital spirits to this finger.
> e is the nerve that gives sensitivity, etc.
> Which being cut, the finger no longer feels anything, even if it were put in the fire; and for this reason nature had the foresight to place it between two fingers, so that it would not be cut.

--- Page 205 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s investigation into the mechanics of the hand, moving from the specific structure of the fingers (detailed on folio 12v) to the complex systems of the forearm. He focuses here on the neurological origins of movement and the ingenious mechanical design of the radius and ulna which allow the hand to rotate.</meta>

-> diminution of movement <-

The nerves that go to the fingers and move them within the bones originate and arise from the spinal cord <note>original: "frossone"; Leonardo uses this term to refer to the spinal cord or the primary nerve bundle</note>. This spinal cord is that nerve <unclear>spinal bundle</unclear> which comes from the brain through the center of the spine <note>original: "nucha"; refers to the spinal cord within the neck</note> to the opening of the shoulder. There, it divides into 4 branches of nerves, of which one goes to the muscles that flex the arm, another goes to the muscles that raise it, another goes to the muscles that rotate it, and the other goes to the muscles that flex the hand. The fifth nerve is the one that comes through the middle of the muscles of the <term>axilla</term> <note>the armpit area</note> of the shoulder and goes to the bend of the elbow. There, it divides into 4 branches, of which one goes to the muscles that flex the arm, another to those that extend it, another to those that rotate it, and another to the muscles that flex the hand and the fingers.

<note>Anatomical study of the bones and tendons of the human hand and forearm</note>

<margin>
reduction of the branches and of the <unclear>ligaments</unclear> of the fingers
</margin>

<note>The following text is positioned between the two large hand drawings</note>
This is the inside of the hand with its bones, nerves, <unclear>skin</unclear>, and muscles that move it; and this is the outside of the same hand with its ligaments, bones, and muscles that move it; and this is one of the sides of the same hand with its bones, nerves, and muscles that move it; and this is the other side of the same hand with its bones, nerves, and muscles that move it.

<margin>
How many nerves go to the fingers and why the "eye" of the nerve divides into 5 branches through the palm of the hand. There it divides into 10 branches, there into 20 branches, there into 40 branches, there into 80 branches, there into 160 branches, there into 320 branches, there into 640 branches, there into 1280 branches, there into 2560 branches, and there into 5120 branches. <note>Leonardo is illustrating a geometric progression to suggest the extreme degree of branching required for fine sensitivity.</note>
</margin>

When the arm is rotated so that the palm of the hand faces the sky, one of these bones of the arm turns over the other. This rotation is performed at the elbow and at the hand, and not in the middle of the arm. This is done because if such rotation were in the middle of the arm, the muscles that move the hand would be twisted and pulled out of their positions. Because the arm is composed of two bones, of which one is fixed and the other is rotatable, the rotatable one <note>the radius</note> circles over the fixed one <note>the ulna</note>. This is the bone that comes from the elbow on the side of the thumb and goes to the hand on the side of the little finger; and the other bone, which comes from the elbow on the side of the little finger, goes to the hand on the side of the thumb. This is done so that when the hand rotates, the nerve that comes from the elbow to the hand crosses and rotates together with the rotatable bone. This nerve is the one that gives sensation to the entire inside of the hand and to the fingers. This nerve is the one that comes through the middle of the elbow and goes to the hand on the side of the little finger; and this nerve is the one that gives sensation to the entire outside of the hand and to the fingers. This nerve is the one that comes through the outer part of the elbow and goes to the hand on the side of the thumb.

<margin>
<note>Next to the sketch of the fist at bottom right</note>
this is the clenched fist
</margin>

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the neurological supply to the arm and the mechanical rotation of the forearm, identifying how the radius crosses the ulna to allow the hand to turn without twisting the muscles. He also speculates on the mathematical branching of nerves to explain the hand's sensitivity.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, neurology, forearm rotation, radius and ulna, nerve branching, biomechanics, hand structure</keywords>

### ON ANATOMY — FOLIO A — 10 VERSO

...having separated and distinct, and well understood, the sizes and shapes of each bone from four aspects; and of those you will note the part of them connected with the surrounding [bones], and also the part of them that is not connected with those surrounding ones, which must move for the benefit of whatever action of the hand.

The first bone of the thumb <note>original: "polise"; modern Italian: pollice</note> and the first bone of the index finger rest upon the basilar bone *e*, and are ready helpers, so much so that this help is received by bone *i* from bone *K*, which in turn receives it from bone *f*.

-> Hand viewed from the inside <-

[Fig. 163 :] *a b c d e f g h i K L m n o p q r S v x y z 4 [?] 7 9 8 6.*

-> (Hand viewed from the outside) <- <note>This text appears upside down in the original manuscript</note>

<margin>Precepts for the study of the hand.</margin>
First represent these hands with their bones detached from one another, and somewhat spread out, so that the number and the shape of their bones may be clearly intelligible, both from the inside and the outside; and represent the ligaments <note>original: "legature"</note> of the bones.

<summary>Leonardo provides instructions on how to illustrate the hand's skeletal structure from four different perspectives to understand how bones articulate and move together. He emphasizes the need to draw bones slightly separated to clearly show their individual shapes and the ligaments that connect them.</summary>
<keywords>osteology, hand anatomy, thumb, index finger, ligaments, anatomical drawing techniques, bone articulation</keywords>

--- Page 208 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s osteological study of the hand from the previous folio. Having previously instructed the artist-anatomist to draw the bones slightly separated to understand their individual shapes, he now provides a precise census of the bones and describes the mechanical ranges of motion available to the wrist and hand.</meta>

# LEONARDO DA VINCI

---

<margin>Bones of the hand.</margin>
27 bones, namely: 8 in the <term>rasetta</term> <note>original: "rasetta"; refers to the carpus or wrist bones</note> *a b c d — e f g h —* 4 in the <term>pettine</term> <note>original: "pettine"; literally "the comb," referring to the metacarpal bones of the palm</note> *K L m n* 15 in the 5 fingers *i p q r S — o v x y z | 4 7 9 8 6*

And I give 3 bones to the thumb <note>original: "polise"; modern Italian: pollice</note>, just as with the other 4 fingers, because in it there are 3 mobile pieces of bone, just like the 3 of every other finger of the hand.

<margin>Movements of the hand.</margin>
[Fig. 164 :] *a b c d*
The hand, while holding the fist clenched, has 4 principal motions, of which the first is toward the <term>domestic</term> <note>original: "parte dimestica"; the inner or medial side of the arm</note> part of the arm; the second is toward the <term>wild</term> <note>original: "parte silvestra"; the outer or lateral side of the arm</note> part of that arm; of the other two motions, one is toward the head and the other toward the feet <note>Leonardo uses "head" and "feet" as directional vectors for what we would now call upward and downward flexion or extension relative to the body's axis</note>; or rather, one is between the domestic and the wild part toward the <term>greater fossil</term> <note>original: "magiore fucile"; the ulna bone of the forearm</note> of the arm, the other is between the domestic and the wild part toward the <term>lesser fossil</term> <note>original: "fucile mjnore"; the radius bone of the forearm</note> of that arm. After these, he marks the composite motions, which I call "composite" because they participate in two of the aforementioned motions; and these are infinite, because they occur throughout the space of continuous quantity interposed between the said [principal motions].

---

#### [Italian Transcription]

<margin>Ossa della mano.</margin>
Ossi 27, cioè : 8 nella rasetta *a b c d, e f g h*; 4 nel pettine *K l m n*, 15 ne’ 5 diti *i p q r S o v x y $\bar{z}$ 4 7 9 8 6*.

E do al polise 3 ossi, come alli altri 4 diti, perchè in lui è 3 pezzi d’ osso mobili, come li 3 di ciascuno altro dito della mano.

<margin>Movimenti della mano.</margin>
[Fig. 164 :] *a b c d*.
La mano, tenendo il pugno stretto, ha 4 moti principali, de’ quali il primo è inverso la parte dimestica del braccio, il secondo è inverso la parte silvestra d’ esso braccio, de li altri due moti l’ uno è inverso la testa e l’altro inverso li piedi, overo l’ uno è tralla parte dimestica e la silvestra, inverso il maggiore fucile del braccio, l’ altro infralla parte dimestica e la silvestra, inverso il fucile minore d’ esso braccio. Dopo questi, segna li moti compositi, li quali nom’ io compositi per essere participi di due de’ predetti moti; e questi sono infiniti, per essere loro fatti in tutto lo spazio di quantità continua, interposti

#### [French Translation]

<margin>Os de la main.</margin>
27 os, c’est-à-dire : 8 dans le poignet, *a b c d, e f g h*; 4 dans la paume *K L m n*, 15 dans les cinq doigts *i p q r S, o v x y $\bar{z}$ 4 7 9 8 6*.

Et je donne 3 os au pouce, comme aux autres 4 doigts, parce qu’il a 3 pièces d’os mobiles, comme les 3 de chacun des autres doigts de la main.

<margin>Mouvements de la main.</margin>
[Fig. 164 :] *a b c d*.
La main, en tenant le poing fermé, a quatre mouvements principaux, dont le premier est vers le côté intérieur du bras, le deuxième vers le côté extérieur dudit bras; pour les autres deux mouvements, l’un est vers la tête et l’autre vers les pieds, ou à la vérité, l’un est entre la partie intérieure et la partie extérieure, vers le cubitus du bras, l’autre entre la partie intérieure et l’extérieure, vers le radius dudit bras. Après ces mouvements dessine les mouvements composés, que je nomme composés parce qu’ils participent de deux desdits mouvements, et ceux-ci sont infinis, car ils se produisent dans tout l’espace de quantité

# ON ANATOMY — FOLIO A — 10 VERSO

Among the aforementioned four principal motions, there follows the rotary motion of the hand. None of the previously described muscles are used for this; rather, only the motor muscles of the two <term>fucili</term> <note>original: "fucili"; literally "tubes" or "firelocks," Leonardo’s term for the two long bones of the forearm, the radius and the ulna.</note> are those which turn the hand around. Next follow the muscles and <term>cords</term> <note>original: "corde"; Leonardo uses this term to describe tendons, which he viewed as mechanical "strings" pulling the bones.</note> that move the fingers.

[Fig. 165.]
[Fig. 166.]

---

Among the aforementioned four principal motions, there follows the rotary motion of the hand. None of the previously described muscles are used for this; rather, only the motor muscles of the two <term>fucili</term> are those which turn the hand around. Next follow the muscles and tendons that move the fingers.

[Fig. 165.]
[Fig. 166.]

Among the aforementioned four principal motions, there is a continuous interposing motion. Then comes the rotary movement of the hand, for which none of the aforesaid muscles are employed; instead, only the motor muscles of the two bones of the arm are those that cause the said hand to turn. Next come the muscles and the motor tendons of the fingers.

[Fig. 165.]
[Fig. 166.]

<summary>Leonardo explains that the rotation of the hand (pronation and supination) is not controlled by the wrist muscles, but by the muscles acting on the radius and ulna. He then transitions his anatomical survey to the tendons and muscles of the fingers.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, muscles, motor muscles, hand, fingers, rotary motion, tendons, radius, ulna</keywords>

# ANATOMY <note>original: "ANATOMIA"</note> _ Folios <note>original: "Fogli"</note> A / 11 _ Recto.

<note>This page features four detailed anatomical studies of the human foot, focusing on the complex network of tendons and ligaments in the dorsal (top) and lateral (side) regions.</note>

167
**Study of the dorsal tendons of the foot**
<note>This sketch illustrates the extensor tendons as they fan out toward the toes. Leonardo was particularly interested in "functional anatomy"—how the mechanical arrangement of these cords allows for the complex movement of the toes.</note>

168
**Anatomical study of the foot and lower leg tendons (lateral view)**
<note>A meticulous side-perspective drawing showing the path of tendons from the lower leg, traveling across the ankle joint and extending into the individual digits.</note>

169
**Detailed study of the lateral foot and ankle musculature**
<note>Leonardo focuses here on the lateral side of the foot, illustrating how the tendons pass behind and beneath the <term>malleolus<note>The bony prominence on each side of the human ankle</note></term>. This arrangement acts like a pulley system to provide stability and power during locomotion.</note>

170
**Focus study of the heel and Achilles tendon region**
<note>A smaller, targeted sketch detailing the insertion points of the tendons around the <term>calcaneus<note>The heel bone, the largest bone of the human foot</note></term>.</note>

# Anatomical Studies of the Foot

<note>A detailed study focusing on the tendons and the internal mechanical structure of the foot.</note>

For one who wishes to distinguish the <term>nerves<note>original: "nervi"; in Renaissance anatomy, this term frequently referred to tendons or ligaments as well as nerves</note></term> of the feet well,
place the feet upright above the buttocks <note>This likely refers to a specific anatomical positioning of a cadaver or model to better observe the tension in the lower limb</note>
and you will see the ends of the said nerves
spread out and pulling here and there through
their fixed and joined places;
and move the foot into different
positions and you will see the
movement of these
<term>instruments<note>Leonardo viewed the body as a complex machine, often referring to muscles and tendons as "instruments" of movement</note></term>.

<margin>
the narrower the foot, the more distant will be the <term>little worms<note>original: "uermingoli"; a reference to the lumbrical muscles, which are small, worm-like muscles in the hand and foot</note></term> that pull the toes of such a foot
</margin>

---

The <term>instruments<note>mechanical components like tendons and muscles</note></term> of movement for the
toes are contained within much less space than those of the hand, by many degrees; and this occurs
because the foot is meant to remain firm, while the hand is meant to move
in various and many tasks; and for this reason the instruments
of movement for the fingers of the hand are very long
and distant from one another.

---
<note>Below the central drawing is a smaller sketch of the heel bone and its associated tendinous attachments.</note>

*   **Nerves:** <note>original: "nervi"</note> Tendons or ligaments.

*   **Extremities:** <note>original: "sstremiti"</note> The ends of the tendons.
*   **Little worms:** <note>original: "uermingoli"</note> The lumbrical muscles.
*   **Instruments:** <note>original: "sstrumēti"</note> The mechanical parts of the body.
*   **Movements:** <note>original: "movimēti"</note>
*   **Hand:** <note>original: "mā"</note>

Sole of the foot.</margin>

# ON ANATOMY. — FOLIO A. — 11 VERSO.

->centered<-
### FOLIO 11 — verso.

<margin>
Function
of the
triceps surae <note>The triceps surae is the group of muscles that forms the calf of the leg.</note>.
</margin>
Here is given the reason for the strength and fatigue that the muscles feel.
[Fig. 171 :] *f d o g v n m a b c*
When muscle *d*, with its associated parts, deflates <note>Leonardo conceptualized muscle contraction as "inflation" and relaxation as "deflation."</note>, it will extend and lengthen itself until the heel <note>original: "calcagno"</note> descends to the ground at point *c*.

<margin>
Equilibrium
of the body
while standing
on the
tips of the toes.
</margin>
When a man raises himself upon the tips of his toes, the muscles *f d* feel, through their pulling, half of the man's weight in one of the legs; and this is proven by the balance <note>original: "equilibra"; referring to a lever or weighing scale.</note> *a b c*, of which the fulcrum <note>original: "polo"</note> is at *b*, and the ends equidistant to that fulcrum are *a* and *c*. Because by placing *m*, the ball of the foot <note>original: "petto del piedi"; literally "chest of the foot," referring to the metatarsal region.</note>, upon *a*, [the point] *v*, the pivot of the foot, cannot descend. The tendon <note>original: "neruo" (nervue); Leonardo used this term for both nerves and tendons, here specifically the Achilles tendon.</note> *f d* that binds the heel does not want to stretch; and thus the ankle, or joint of the foot *v*, undergoes a weight of two hundred pounds, of which 100 pounds is the natural weight <note>Leonardo is demonstrating a "class 2" lever. If the ball of the foot is the fulcrum, the muscle must pull up with a force equal to the body weight to maintain balance, resulting in the ankle joint supporting the sum of both forces.</note>.

<summary>Leonardo applies the principles of mechanical levers to the human body, specifically calculating the force exerted on the ankle joint. He concludes that when standing on tiptoe, the ankle joint bears double the body's weight due to the mechanical advantage required by the calf muscles.</summary>
<keywords>biomechanics, triceps surae, Achilles tendon, heel, ankle joint, levers, equilibrium, weight distribution, anatomy</keywords>

# LEONARDO DA VINCI

<margin>Extension and flexion of the foot on the leg.</margin>
[The weight is] natural in *m*, that is to say in *a*, and there are 100 [units] of accidental weight <note>In Leonardo’s mechanics, "natural weight" refers to the gravity of the object itself, while "accidental weight" refers to the force or pressure produced by motion or a lever.</note> in *n*. Both of these forces are directed upwards, because *m* would want to move toward *g*, and *n* is pulled upwards by the calf of the leg.

The tendons <note>original: "nervi"; Leonardo frequently used this term to describe tendons, as the distinction between nerves and tendons was not always strictly defined in his vocabulary.</note> that move the foot toward the ankle or the joint of the foot are *nf*; therefore *f* is common to two movements, namely lifting and bending the foot. And here you see the sagacity of nature, how she has provided two causes of motion for every limb so that, if one fails, the other—if not entirely, then at least in part—may supply the need.

<margin>Muscles and tendons in the posterior region of the leg.</margin>
Observe the rows of muscle fibers that unite together, as shown in the inner calf <note>original: "pompa dimestica della gamba"; Leonardo uses "pompa" (literally "pump" or "bulge") to describe the belly of the muscle.</note> of the leg; it forms *m* with *c* at the position *S*. See which fibers are common and which are specific.
[*Fig. 172:*] *h K m c n S o f a.*

<margin>Instructions for the study of the foot.</margin>
Make a demonstration of these feet without the membrane <note>original: "pannicolo"; here referring to the periosteum or the deep fascia that wraps the bones.</note> that clothes the bones, which membrane hides those bones from us by interposing itself between them.

---

<margin>Extension and flexion of the foot on the leg.</margin>
...in *m*, that is to say in *a*, and 100 of accidental weight in *n*, and both forces are directed upward, for *m* would want to go to *g*, and *n* is drawn upward by the calf of the leg.

The tendons, which move the foot toward the ankle or joint of the foot, are *nf*; *f* is therefore common to two movements, that is to say, of raising and of bending the foot; and you see here the sagacity of nature, as she has provided each limb with two causes of movement, so that if one fails, the other supplies it, if not for everything, at least in part.

<margin>Muscles and tendons in the posterior region of the leg.</margin>
Observe in the fibers of the muscles, which unite together, as one sees in the inner calf of the leg that *m* makes with *c* at the place *S*, and see which fibers are common and which are specific.
[*Fig. 172:*] *h K m c n S o f a.*

Make a demonstration of these feet, without the membrane that covers the bones, a membrane which occupies these bones by interposing itself between them.

# LEONARDO DA VINCI
## ANATOMY — Folio A. 11 Verso

<note>This page is a facsimile plate featuring four anatomical and mechanical drawings, reproduced as clear line-art tracings based on Leonardo's studies in the Royal Collection. The background contains ghosted mirror-writing from the original manuscript.</note>

### THE MECHANICS OF THE FOOT AND LEG

<margin>Extension and flexion of the foot on the leg.</margin>

[The weight is] natural in *m*, that is to say in *a*, and there are 100 units of accidental weight in *n*. <note>In Leonardo’s mechanics, "natural weight" refers to the gravity of the object itself, while "accidental weight" refers to the force or pressure produced by motion or a lever.</note> Both of these forces are directed upwards, because *m* would want to move toward *g*, and *n* is pulled upwards by the calf of the leg.

[*Fig. 171: Small anatomical study of the lower leg bones and musculature*]

The tendons <note>original: "nervi"; Leonardo frequently used this term to describe tendons, as the distinction between nerves and tendons was not always strictly defined in his vocabulary.</note> that move the foot toward the ankle or the joint of the foot are *nf*; therefore *f* is common to two movements, namely lifting and bending the foot. And here you see the **sagacity of nature**, how she has provided two causes of motion for every limb so that, if one fails, the other—if not entirely, then at least in part—may supply the need.

[*Fig. 172: Large detailed anatomical study of the human leg and foot, showing the calf muscles and the complex network of tendons on the dorsal surface of the foot*]

<margin>Muscles and tendons in the posterior region of the leg.</margin>

Observe the rows of muscle fibers that unite together, as shown in the inner calf <note>original: "pompa dimestica della gamba"; Leonardo uses "pompa" (literally "pump" or "bulge") to describe the belly of the muscle.</note> of the leg; it forms *m* with *c* at the position *S*. See which fibers are common and which are specific.

[*Reference to Fig. 172:*] *h K m c n S o f a.*

[*Fig. 173: Diagrammatic representation of a joint or mechanical lever system*]

[*Fig. 174: Schematic diagram of a pulley or pivot mechanism, illustrating mechanical principles applied to anatomy*]

<margin>Instructions for the study of the foot.</margin>

Make a demonstration of these feet without the membrane <note>original: "pannicolo"; here referring to the periosteum or the deep fascia that covers the muscles and bones.</note> so that the anatomical structure remains clear and the mechanical functions of the joints are visible.

<summary>This page explores the biomechanics of the human leg and foot, specifically focusing on how muscles and tendons act as mechanical levers. Leonardo highlights "nature's sagacity" in providing redundant biological systems to ensure movement even if one part is injured.</summary>

<meta>This folio continues Leonardo’s detailed investigation into the biomechanics of the lower limb, moving from the theoretical leverage of the foot (seen on the previous page) to the specific muscular and vascular anatomy of the calf and heel. # LEONARDO DA VINCI
## ANATOMY FOLIO A
## LEONARDO DA VINCI

<note>This page contains anatomical studies of the human leg and foot, focusing on the musculature and tendons of the calf and heel. Leonardo's mirror script is transcribed here into standard left-to-right Italian text.</note>

<margin>
All the tendons <note>original: "nervi"; Leonardo used this term for nerves, tendons, and sinews interchangeably</note> that pull the muscles are in the middle of the muscles, within their flesh; and those that pull the bones arise from the ends of the muscles. These tendons are made of branches of the said muscular tendons, which are small but many and strong. When the said muscles contract by virtue of sensation, they come to pull with the force of so many tiny and strong tendons, among which their flesh is placed.
</margin>

<note>The following text refers to the large central drawing where the calf muscle is labeled with the letter 'r'.</note>

<margin>
The muscle **r**, which forms the calf of the leg, is fixed above the knee joint on the inner side. One sees that in the muscles of the leg, there are thicker muscles than in any other part of the living body, because the muscles of the thigh would not have the power to sustain the weight of the man if they were not aided by the muscles of the leg. These muscles, with the great tendons they send out, attach themselves to the heel of the feet. Then, the muscles of the foot steady the weight of the man and support it above the lower part of the leg. Having said this, I will give the reason in the chapters on weights and their supports.
</margin>

<margin>
The movements of the toes are made in the muscles of the leg and are performed by tendons which pass over the ankle <note>original: "noda del piede" (knot of the foot)</note>. The veins of the leg go to the inner foot and through the toes; there is one in the toes which is seen quite well, and if this one breaks, the nostrils <note>original: "frosscie del naso"</note> of the nose lose much blood <unclear>because</unclear> there is much cord <note>tendon/sinew</note> in the foot and little flesh; there are large tendons and many veins, and for every vein, there is its reason and its utility.
</margin>

<note>Annotation near the smaller sketch at top right.</note>

<margin>
Anatomy of the calf of the leg of a living person from the inside.
</margin>

<margin>
Regarding the muscles of the leg, which should not go outside their line, they have a slight convexity. On the inside of these muscles runs a vein which branches through the said muscles, and one branch comes out over the calf of the leg. This vein is very visible in old men; it descends as far as the ankle on the inside, and there a branch comes out which goes through the middle of the foot, while the other part descends to the toes. Here there are many movements because there are many tendons and veins, and it goes <unclear>r</unclear>ecutting through the tendons and through the bone of the toes. Those tendons have their origin in the calf of the leg; those that bend them <term>flexors</term> have their origin in the muscles of the leg on the front side, and those that extend them <term>extensors</term> have their origin in the muscles of the leg on the inner side; and for every tendon, there is its reason and its utility.
</margin>

<margin>
The ankle from the outside is the bone called by many the heel <note>the calcaneus</note>, and from the outside comes a great tendon <note>the Achilles tendon</note> from the muscles of the leg, which is attached to that heel. When wanting to raise the man upwards, it pulls and lifts the heel and the whole weight of the man above the ankle. Through the toes of the foot pass many tendons, but there are fewer veins and less flesh because there is more movement there than in any other part of the foot. The toes move by tendons which have their origin in the muscles of the leg. It is true that those tendons that extend the toes have their origin in the muscles of the front of the leg, and those that bend them have their origin in the calf of the leg. I will give clearer notice of this in the pages of anatomy.
</margin>

---

->From the ankle joint downwards, the tendons of the calf of the leg and the leg below the foot are almost always bare of flesh, because there is a great variety of movements and because flesh is weak in movements while tendons are strong; and because flesh would have no resistance to the great weights of the man upon the said feet. Think how much power is in the tendon that pulls the heel and that lifts the weight of the man upon the ankle!<-

### ON ANATOMY — FOLIO A — 11 VERSO

...lever than *b a*, and it has so much more power as it is longer, as is proven in the 5th [proposition] of the 4th [book] of the **Mechanical Elements**. <note>Leonardo frequently references his own theoretical studies on mechanics—which he called "Elements of Machines"—to explain the "geometry" of the human body.</note>

<margin>
The reason 
for the joining 
of two muscles.
</margin>

There are many instances where two muscles are joined together, even though they must serve two different members <note>meaning limbs or body parts</note>; and this was done so that if one muscle were hindered by some injury, the other muscle might partially fill the place of the one that is missing.
[Leoni] <note>This name refers to Pompeo Leoni, the 16th-century sculptor who collected and organized Leonardo's loose manuscripts into volumes.</note>

<summary>Leonardo explains the mechanical advantage of muscle attachments using the principles of levers and discusses Nature’s "fail-safe" design, where muscles are paired so that one can compensate if the other is injured.</summary>

### Anatomical Studies of the Foot

<note>Study of the muscles and tendons of the lower leg as they connect to the foot bones.</note>
This part of the leg muscles must first be drawn from the nude and then from the dissected state <note>original: "dj sscarnato"; referring to a body stripped of skin to reveal the underlying structure</note> so that one can well understand where the bones, nerves, and muscles attach and where they originate.

<note>Detailed view of the bones of the foot (tarsals, metatarsals, and phalanges) seen from above.</note>
<margin>This bony structure of the foot from the inside must be drawn from four directions so that every part of it is well understood; and the same must be done for every limb of which a drawing of man is to be made.</margin>

<note>View of the foot bones from the medial (inside) perspective.</note>
The <term>nerve</term> <note>Leonardo frequently used the word "nerve" (neruo) to refer to what we now categorize as tendons</note> that pulls the big toe upward is the one we have drawn in the middle of the leg, extending above the bone, just as we drew on the inside of the arm.

<note>Two large horizontal studies of the foot skeleton, one from a lateral/oblique view and another from a medial view.</note>

The first bony structure of the foot that we draw is the one from above, and its bones must be numbered; the second is from the inside, the third is from below, and the last is from the outside. The same must be done for all the bony structures of the limbs in the entire anatomy of man.

And for all the aforementioned four directions of the feet, an equal number of dissected versions must be made so that the origin and the end of the muscles are well understood, and where they attach to their bones. This is of the highest value to the science that is practiced through such drawings.

<note>Small sketch of a foot at the bottom right.</note>
<margin>from below</margin>

<summary>This page outlines Leonardo da Vinci's rigorous methodology for anatomical study, advocating for the depiction of limbs from four distinct perspectives and in both 'nude' and 'dissected' states. It specifically applies this method to the bones and tendons of the foot, illustrating how the big toe is articulated by tendons in the leg.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, foot bones, dissection, musculoskeletal system, scientific illustration, tarsals, tendons</keywords>

# LEONARDO DA VINCI

Make the bones of the feet somewhat **separated** <note>original: 'spiccate'. In modern anatomy, this is known as a disarticulated view, where bones are pulled apart to show their hidden surfaces.</note> from one another, in such a way that one can be distinctly discerned from the other; and this will be the method that will provide knowledge of the number of each of the bones of the foot and of their shape.

[Fig. 182.]
[Fig. 183.]

At the end of every representation of feet, you shall give the measurement of the **thickness** and **length** of each of its bones and their position.

The <term>aspects</term><note>original: 'aspetti'. Leonardo consistently used a system of six primary viewpoints—front, back, both sides, top, and bottom—to provide a complete spatial understanding of anatomical structures.</note> of the foot are 6, namely: from below, above, inside, outside, behind, and in front; and to these are added the 6 demonstrations of the bones separated from one another; and there are bones sawed lengthwise <column-break/> in two ways, that is, sawed from the side and straight through—in order that the entire thickness of the bones may be demonstrated.

[Fig. 184.]

<summary>Leonardo outlines his methodology for anatomical illustration, requiring six standard perspectives, disarticulated views of individual bones, and cross-sections to reveal internal bone density and structure.</summary>

# ON ANATOMY. — FOLIO A. — 12 RECTO.

<margin>Glandular bones <note>original: 'ossiglandulosi'. These are now known as sesamoid bones—small, rounded bones embedded in tendons where they pass over joints, acting like pulleys.</note></margin>
Always the glandular bones are placed near the end of the **tendons** <note>original: 'chorde'. Leonardo uses this to describe the cord-like appearance of tendons.</note> when they bind with the bones; of which eight are found in the composition of human bones (2): namely, two in the **tendons** <note>original: 'nervi'. In Renaissance anatomy, the term 'nervi' was often used interchangeably for nerves, tendons, and sinews.</note> called 'shoulders of the neck' when they join at the upper heads of the bone called the <term>aiutorio</term> <note>The 'aiutorio' is an archaic term for the humerus, or upper arm bone.</note>, and two others at the ends of the muscles originating in the <term>alcatin</term> <note>An Arabic-derived term ('al-katim') used by medieval and Renaissance anatomists to refer to the pelvis or hip area.</note> and ending at the knee, and 4 in the feet, that is, two for each big toe on the underside—

### Anatomical Studies

**The Superficial Veins of the Arm**
This study illustrates the superficial veins and the musculature of a right arm, shown horizontally. Leonardo meticulously details the cephalic and basilic veins—the primary veins of the arm—and the clear definition of the deltoid and biceps muscles in a relaxed state.

**The Arm in Tension**
This sketch depicts an outstretched right arm with the hand firmly gripping a vertical staff. This is a classic example of Leonardo’s interest in "anatomy in action," highlighting how the muscles of the forearm and upper arm change shape and increase in tension when performing a physical task.

**Muscles of the Neck and Torso**
The drawings on the lower half of the page focus on the neck and shoulder. One view highlights the <term>platysma</term><note>A broad, thin sheet of muscle in the neck that pulls down the corners of the mouth and tightens the skin of the neck.</note> and the <term>sternocleidomastoid</term><note>The thick muscle visible on the side of the neck that allows the head to turn and tilt.</note>. These were central to Leonardo’s studies on how anatomy facilitates human expression and movement.

**The Shoulder Connection**
A lateral view of the neck and shoulder musculature shows the connections of the <term>trapezius</term><note>The large triangular muscle extending over the back of the neck and shoulders.</note> and the deltoid. Leonardo uses this to provide a three-dimensional perspective of how the shoulder girdle attaches to the torso.

<margin>
This shoulder shown above will be displayed at the end from various aspects, so that the entire connection of the <term>nerves</term><note>original: "nerbi"; in Leonardo’s anatomical writings, this term often encompasses nerves, tendons, and sinews, as the precise neurological distinctions were still being discovered.</note> and the muscles that arise from the neck may be perfectly understood, and how they form the beginning of the nerves of the arms and hands.
</margin>

<summary>This page illustrates Leonardo’s detailed observation of the arm's vascular system and the muscular mechanics of the neck and shoulder. He focuses specifically on how "nerves" (tendons and nerves) and muscles integrate to facilitate the complex movements of the upper body.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, anatomy, musculature, vascular system, cephalic vein, platysma, shoulder mechanics, nerves, anatomical drawing</keywords>

# ANATOMY FOLIO A
## 18 VERSO

<note>This folio features four masterful anatomical drawings by Leonardo da Vinci, showcasing his revolutionary approach to scientific illustration. The sketches explore the superficial and deep muscle layers of the human arm in various positions of extension and flexion.</note>

### The Mechanics of Muscle and Tendon

The drawings on this page serve as a functional analysis of the human arm. Leonardo's cross-hatching technique brings a three-dimensional quality to the muscles, illustrating how they shift and change shape under different physical conditions.

**Study of the Flexed Arm**
The lower sketches focus on the arm bent at the elbow, viewed from both the side and the middle. These drawings reveal the complex interaction between the biceps and triceps, as well as the intricate layering of the forearm muscles. Leonardo is particularly interested in how these muscles interact with the skeletal structure to produce movement.

**The Functional Connection**
Leonardo’s notes at the bottom of the page reflect his proto-scientific attempt to define the "origin" and "insertion" of muscles—concepts that remain central to modern anatomy. He observes that muscles do not exist in isolation but are defined by their two endpoints and their connection to the nervous and vascular systems.

<margin>
These muscles of the arm are drawn from below and from above; those are drawn above because the muscles originate at one <term>terminal point</term> <note>Leonardo uses the word 'termine' to describe the attachment points of a muscle, known in modern anatomy as the origin and insertion.</note> and end in another terminal point, and the other way around.
-> the muscle as formed arises through the nerves and <term>tendons</term> <note>original: "corde". Leonardo frequently uses "cords" to describe tendons or sinews.</note> <-
</margin>

---

<note>Leonardo's observation that muscles "arise through the nerves and tendons" highlights his belief that the "nerves" (which he sometimes conflated with tendons) were the mechanical agents that transmitted the force of the "animal spirits" to move the limbs.</note>

<summary>This page features Leonardo da Vinci's detailed studies of arm musculature, specifically examining the origin and insertion points of muscles and their relationship to tendons and nerves.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, anatomy, myology, human arm, musculature, tendons, nerves, Renaissance science, biomechanics</keywords>

<margin>
Motor muscles of the hands and wings.
</margin>

No movement of the hand or of its fingers is produced by muscles located from the elbow upward; and thus it is in birds, and therefore they are so powerful, because all the muscles that lower the wings originate in the chest, which in themselves have greater weight than all the rest of that bird. — <note>Leonardo identifies that the strength of a bird's flight comes from the massive pectoral muscles, drawing a parallel to how the fine motor control of the human hand is powered by muscles in the forearm.</note>

# Manuscript Facsimile: Anatomical Studies of the Human Skeleton

From these three demonstrations, the internal view will never be obscured, because the overlapping <term>overlapping|Leonardo uses the word 'ribattimento' to describe how bones hide or interfere with one another's visibility when viewed from a single perspective</term> of the bones within themselves is only made clear through these multiple demonstrations <note>Leonardo refers to his drawings as 'demonstrations,' a term implying a scientific proof or visual explanation.</note>.

<margin>
**[Top left, near posterior thorax]**
This shall be the true position of the neck of the humerus <note>the upper arm bone</note>, the scapula, and the spine of the back throughout the entire view.
</margin>

<margin>
**[Top right, near lateral thorax]**
And this overlapping will be seen through the cords <note>tendons or ligaments</note> of the muscles which, by pulling the bones, also draw them toward one another.
</margin>

<margin>
**[Below previous]**
From the arm, one will also see the true distance that exists from one shoulder to the other.
</margin>

<margin>
**[Center, between rib cages]**
The true positions of the bones of every part shall be found in these demonstrations; namely, that one arm is seen from the upper side and the other from the lower side.
</margin>

<margin>
**[Left of central spine]**
This shall be viewed from below and from the front, and it shall be the true form of the ribs, the scapula, and the shoulder blade <note>original: "spatola," an archaic term Leonardo uses interchangeably with "scapula."</note>.
</margin>

<margin>
**[Bottom left]**
The true positions of the whole reality will serve for the anterior, posterior, lateral, and inferior demonstrations; and this shall be the true science, and it shall be led to a complete and uninterrupted knowledge.
</margin>

<margin>
**[Bottom center, near pelvis]**
Here shall be the true form of the spine as seen from below, and the true position of the bones of the thigh <note>the femurs</note>.
</margin>

<margin>
**[Bottom right, near lateral pelvis]**
The ridge of the scapula, the humerus, the spine, and the ribs from every angle.
</margin>

<summary>On this page, Leonardo da Vinci explains his revolutionary anatomical method of using multiple viewpoints—front, back, side, and from below—to fully reveal the complex structure of the skeleton without one bone obscuring another. He specifically applies this to the shoulder assembly, the rib cage, and the pelvis, asserting that this systematic approach creates a "true science" of anatomy.</summary>

<keywords>anatomy, skeletal system, scapula, humerus, rib cage, spine, pelvis, scientific demonstration, Renaissance medicine, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

<margin>Scapulohumeral articulation <note>The joint connecting the shoulder blade and the upper arm bone.</note></margin>
Show the bone of the arm <note>original: "aiutorio," an archaic term for the humerus.</note> how its head <note>original: "fronte," literally 'brow' or 'front'.</note> is encased in the mouth <note>the glenoid cavity or socket</note> of the shoulder blade <note>original: "spatula," the scapula</note>, and the utility of the lips <note>the edges or rim of the socket</note> of that shoulder blade marked *o t*, and of the part marked *a*, where the muscles of the neck are joined—

<margin>Scapulohumeral articulation.</margin>
Demonstrate the bone of the arm, how its head fits into the mouth of the shoulder blade, and the utility of the lips of this shoulder blade *o t*, and of the part *a*, where the muscles of the neck are joined.

<margin>Scapulohumeral articulation.</margin>
Demonstrate the bone of the humerus, how its head fits into the mouth of the scapula, and the utility of the lips of this scapula *o t*, and of the part *a*, where the muscles of the neck attach themselves.

<margin>Muscular insertions.</margin>
You will make a second illustration of the bones, in which the attachment of the muscles on these bones is shown.

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s systematic study of the musculoskeletal system.
