# ON ANATOMY — FOLIO 13 VERSO.

<sig>12*</sig>

### FOLIO 13 — verso.

<margin>Arrangement of voluntary muscles and their various ways of attachment to the bones.</margin>

There is one type of muscle that begins as a tendon and ends as a tendon <note>original: "corde." Leonardo uses "corde" (cords) to refer to tendons or sinews.</note>. These are divided into two kinds: one has its tendons flattened and turned into cartilage <note>Leonardo is likely describing aponeurosis, which are broad, flat sheets of connective tissue that look like thin, pearly-white cartilage.</note>, and the other has its tendons round, used in the manner of a cord.

The second type of muscle has its tendons only on one end. These are also divided into two kinds: in one, the tendon is flattened into the shape of a thin cartilage, and in the other, it remains round.

There is another type of muscle that attaches by its underside along its entire length to the surface of the bone it covers. In these, each of its individual parts <note>referring to the muscle fibers or fascicles</note> has different lengths; the shortest is the most powerful. [Fig. 196 :] *a b p n*, and it sits, as seen in *a b n*, attached over the bone of the arm <note>original: "osso del braccio," referring here to the humerus.</note> *p m*; for the part *a b* is longer than the part *b n*.

---

### FOLIO 13 — verso.

<margin>Arrangement of voluntary muscles and their various modes of attachment to the bones.</margin>

There is one type of muscle that begins in tendons and ends in tendons; these are divided into two parts, of which one has its tendons flattened and converted into cartilage, and the other has its tendons round, in the manner of a cord.

The second species of muscle has its tendons only on one side <note>meaning only at one end of the muscle belly</note>, and these are also divided into two parts: the tendon of one is flattened in the shape of thin cartilage, while the other remains round.

Another type of muscle exists which, through its whole length, attaches from the underside onto the bone that it covers; of these, each of its parts has various lengths, and the shortest is the most powerful, [Fig. 196 :] *a b p n*, and it stands as seen in *a b n*, attached above the bone of the arm *p m*, because the part *a b* is longer than the part *b n*.

---

### FOLIO 13 — verso.

<margin>Arrangement of voluntary muscles and their different ways of insertion on the bones.</margin>

There is a species of muscles that begin with tendons and end with tendons; and these are divided into two classes, one of which has its tendons dilated and transformed into cartilage, and the other has its tendons round, in the guise of cords.

The second species of muscles only has tendons at one end, and these tendons are further divided into two classes, one of which has the tendons dilated like thin cartilages and the other of which simply keeps them round.

There is another species of muscles, which are fixed along their entire length and by their lower part onto the bone they cover; and, for these, each of their particles has varied lengths; and the shortest is the most powerful, [Fig. 196 :] *a b p n*, and remains, as one sees in *a b n*, attached above the bone of the arm, *p m*; for the particle *a b* is longer than the particle *b n*.

<summary>Leonardo categorizes muscles into three main types based on whether they have tendons at both ends, one end, or attach along their entire length to the bone. He notes the mechanical principle that shorter muscle fibers within these attachments provide greater power.</summary>

<keywords>muscles, tendons, bones, insertion, anatomy, humerus, mechanical power, cartilage</keywords>

[Fig. 197 :] a b c d

Precepts for the study of the bones and the muscles.
</margin>

Note where the lowest parts of the shoulder muscles *a b c d* are fixed, and which are those that are fixed upon the bone called the <term>aiutorio</term> <note>The "aiutorio" (literally the "helper bone") is an archaic anatomical term for the humerus, the long bone of the upper arm.</note>, and which are fixed upon other muscles.

Create for each bone, taken separately, its muscles; that is, the muscles that originate upon it.

Illustrate all the causes of the motion that occur in the skin, flesh, and muscles of a face, and whether its muscles receive their motion from the nerves that come from the brain, or not <note>Leonardo was deeply interested in the physiological source of emotion and expression, attempting to trace the physical path from the brain to the facial muscles.</note>.

And do this first in the horse, which has large muscles and parts that are very distinct <note>Leonardo frequently used comparative anatomy; because the horse’s muscles are more prominent and powerful than a human's, they provided a clearer map for his initial studies.</note>.

One shall be the movement of the arm forward with the shoulder that rises toward the ear, and the other shall be the movement forward with the shoulder still.
The third shall be the movement of the arm backward with the shoulder fixed, and the fourth shall be the same movement with the shoulder moving backward.

The muscles that move the arm are of two types <term>ragioni: literally "reasons," used here to mean categories or types</term>, namely intrinsic and extrinsic.
The intrinsic ones are those that have their origin <note>original: "nascimento," literally "birth"</note> and end in the bone of the humerus <term>omero: the bone of the upper arm</term>.
The extrinsic ones are those that have their origin outside of said bone, such as in the back, in the chest, and similar parts.

### ON ANATOMY — FOLIO A — 13 VERSO

<margin>Dilator muscle of the nostril.</margin>
Observe whether the muscle that raises the nostrils of the horse is the same as the one found here in man at $f$, which emerges from the hole in the bone $f$.

[Fig. 198 :] $f$ a $n$

<margin>Facial muscles in relation to expression <term>mimica: the study of how facial movements convey emotion</term>.</margin>
$h$ is the muscle of anger; $p$ is the muscle of pain; $g$ is the muscle for biting; $g, n, m$ are one and the same muscle; $o, t$ is the muscle of anger.

[Fig. 199 :] $p$ $h$ $g$ $p$ $o$ $b$ $o$ $c$ $t$ $n$ $r$ $S$ $a$ $m$

<margin>Muscles of the face.</margin>
Note which nerves are those that serve the muscles $b, o, c$ of the human cheek, placed here above.

$a$ is the muscular flesh that originates in the upper gums and ends in the lower gums, and ends in the jaw and the mouth. <note>Leonardo is likely describing the buccinator muscle, which makes up the bulk of the cheek.</note>

<margin>Masseter and temporal muscles.</margin>
The muscle $m$ is thicker than $p$ because it has to endure more labor <note>original: "fatica". Leonardo correctly identifies that the masseter, used for chewing, is one of the strongest muscles in the body for its size.</note>.

<margin>Pterygoid muscle.</margin>
I remove muscle $m$, and its site remains, as seen in the other head at $n$.

<margin>Zygomatic <note>cheek</note> muscles.</margin>
And I remove the two muscles $o, c$, and the bone $f$ remains.

[Fig. 201 :] 6

Have you seen here the diligence of nature in having situated the nerves, arteries, and veins on the sides of the fingers and not in the middle, so that, through the operation <note>movement or manual work</note> of the fingers, they would not in some way happen to be pierced or cut.

[Fig. 202 :] a o b c d
The arrangement of the vein *a b c d* is such that *c d* goes along the inside of the hand as far as the fork of the fingers, that is at *a o*, and then it divides at *o* and creates three branches, of which the branch *o b* passes to the outside <note>the back</note> of the hand, and the two *o c* and *o d* run along the inside of the fingers.

[Fig. 203 :] 5$^a$ c a n m b
Observe whether you believe that such a sense <note>the sense of touch</note> is distracted in an organ player, while the soul <note>Leonardo uses 'anima' to refer to the conscious processing center of the mind</note> at such a time attends to the sense of hearing. This is because the same object is touched by side *b* of the finger and side *a* of the [second] finger.

It seems to be two <note>Referring to the tactile sensation of a single object feeling like two</note> if touched by *n* and *a*; and if touched by *n* and *m*, it seems to be one. This is because *n m* arises from a single nerve, and *a b* arises from two nerves.

# ON ANATOMY — FOLIO A — 13 VERSO

It seems to be two <note>Referring to the tactile sensation of a single object feeling like two when the fingers are crossed</note> if touched by *n* and *a*; and if touched by *n* and *m*, it seems to be one. This is because *n m* arises from a single nerve, and *a b* arises from two nerves.

->here is motion and sensation<-

Here, by some incision in the hand, sometimes the sensation in its fingers is hindered and not the motion; and sometimes the motion and not the sensation; and sometimes both sensation and motion. <note>This is a significant early observation of the functional specialization of nerves—knowing that one can lose feeling without losing the ability to move.</note>

Here is motion and sense.
Here, through some incision in the hand, sometimes the sensation in the fingers is impeded and not the motion; and sometimes the motion and not the sensation; and sometimes the sensation and the motion.

# ANATOMY Folio A, Page 6

<note>This page features detailed anatomical studies of the shoulder and arm musculature in various positions. Leonardo explores how the muscles interact as mechanical levers to lift the weight of the limb.</note>

<margin>
Movements of the
shoulder and which
are the
muscles
<note>original: "moti della spalla de' quali sono muscoli"</note>
</margin>

<note>Six distinct sketches show the anatomy of the right shoulder and arm from different perspectives, emphasizing muscular tension and the points where they attach to the bone. Leonardo uses fine hatching—closely spaced parallel lines—to give the muscles a three-dimensional, fleshy appearance.</note>

¶ Or how from the traditional function <note>original: "anticho ufitio" — "Office" or "duty" was a common Renaissance term for the specific mechanical task a muscle was designed to perform</note> of the muscles arise infinitely many other muscles <note>Leonardo is likely observing that a single large muscle is composed of many smaller fibers, each contributing to complex movements</note>

and which of these muscles will overcome the resistance <note>The physical force or weight that must be moved</note> of the weight of the arm and will raise it; and it will consider the direction of that movement being made in more than one way.

<note>A geometric diagram of a circle with axes marked with letters (A, B, C, D) appears here. Leonardo used these diagrams to map out the "vectors" or lines of force. By treating the shoulder as a central point in a circle, he could calculate how different muscles pull the arm up, down, or sideways.</note>

DE ANATOMIA. — FOLIO A. — 14 VERSO.
13*

### FOLIO 14 — *verso.*

<margin>
The necessity of drawing and describing.
</margin>

And you who wish to demonstrate, through words, the form of the human body and all the aspects of its limb-structure <term>membrificazione: the arrangement and articulation of the limbs</term>, discard such an idea. For the more minutely you describe, the more you will confuse the mind of the reader and the more you will lead them away from the knowledge of the thing described. Therefore, it is necessary both to illustrate and to describe.

[Fig. 212 :] p.ª <note>likely an abbreviation for 'prima' or first figure.</note>

If the natural object, being in relief <note>original: 'rilevato'; referring to the three-dimensional nature of a real body compared to a flat drawing</note>, seems more recognizable to you than this drawing—a recognition that arises because one can see the object from different perspectives—you must understand that in my illustrations, the same result will be achieved by showing those same perspectives. Thus, no part of these limbs will be hidden from you.

---

#### FOLIO 14 — *verso.*

<margin>
The necessity of drawing and describing.
</margin>

And you who wish to demonstrate with words the figure of man with all the aspects of his limb-structure, discard such an opinion, because the more minutely you describe, the more you will confuse the mind of the reader, and the more you will remove him from the knowledge of the thing described; therefore it is necessary to illustrate and describe.

[Fig. 212 :] p.ª

If the natural object, because it is in relief, seems more familiar to you than this drawing—which knowledge results from being able to see the thing from various aspects—you have to understand that in this illustration of mine, that same quality will be found through those same aspects; therefore, no part of these limbs will be hidden from you.

---

#### FOLIO 14 — *verso.*

<margin>
It is necessary to draw and to describe.
</margin>

And you who claim, through words, to demonstrate the figure of man with all the aspects of the arrangement of his limbs, cast away this opinion, for the more minutely you describe, the more you will confound the spirit of the reader, and the further you will distance him from the notion of the thing described; it is therefore necessary to depict and to describe.

[Fig. 212 :] p.ª

If the natural object, because it is in relief, seems better known to you than this which is drawn—a notion that results because one can see the object from different aspects—you must understand that, in the depictions I give of it, one will obtain the same effect for the same aspects; therefore no part of these limbs will be hidden from you.

<summary>On this page, Leonardo da Vinci provides a famous methodological manifesto, arguing that anatomical knowledge requires visual illustration because verbal descriptions alone are too confusing for the human mind. He asserts that his multi-perspective drawings provide the same level of information as observing a three-dimensional body in relief.</summary>

<keywords>anatomical method, illustration vs. description, three-dimensional relief, perspective, scientific visualization, human form</keywords>

<margin>
Auxiliary
muscles
of
respiration.
</margin>

The nerves, or muscles <note>Leonardo often uses the word 'nerve' to describe what we now call tendons or muscle fibers</note>, originating at the last vertebrae <term>spondili: vertebrae</term> of the neck, serve the breath when a man stands upright; and they serve this breathing together with the muscles that originate in the last vertebra of the spine of the back. 

The operation of these muscles of the chest—which are *or, ot, oq*—are joined to the tip of the shoulder blade <term>spatola: the scapula or shoulder blade</term> *o*. This tip rests upon the front of the humerus <term>aiutorio: the humerus bone of the upper arm</term> *h*; and such humerus is supported by the shield <term>scuto: likely the acromion or the protective part of the shoulder girdle</term> *c*, and this *c* is joined to the bone of the clavicle <term>forcula: the clavicle or collarbone</term> *nd*. This bone is supported at its extremity *n* by the nerves, or rather muscles *an*, which originate in the last vertebrae of the neck.

<summary>Leonardo describes the anatomical relationship between the neck vertebrae, the shoulder girdle, and the muscles used for breathing while standing upright. He uses specific labels to link his text to his drawings of the scapula, humerus, and clavicle.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, shoulder, respiration, vertebrae, humerus, clavicle, biomechanics</keywords>

The nerves or muscles <note>Leonardo often used the word 'nervi' (nerves) to refer to tendons or the cord-like parts of muscles, as the distinct functions of the nervous and muscular systems were not yet fully understood in the early 16th century</note> born from the last vertebrae <term>spondili: from the Greek 'spondylos', meaning vertebrae</term> of the neck serve for respiration when the man stands upright; and they serve this same breath together with the muscles that originate in the last vertebra of the spine of the back. The operation of these muscles in the chest—which are labeled *or*, *ot*, and *oq*—are joined to the tip of the shoulder blade <term>spatola: the scapula</term> *o*. This tip rests upon the front <note>original: 'l’afcoro', an archaic or possibly misspelled anatomical reference; the French translator interprets this as the 'front' or head of the bone</note> of the humerus <term>aiutario: a medieval anatomical term for the humerus or upper arm bone</term> *k*. And such humerus is supported by the shield <term>scuto: referring to the acromion process or the 'shield' of the shoulder</term> *c*, and *c* is joined to the fork of the clavicle <term>forchila: literally 'little fork', the collarbone</term> *nd*. This [structure] is aided at the extremity *r* by the nerves or muscles *an* which arise from the last vertebrae of the neck.

-># LEONARDO DA VINCI: ANATOMICAL STUDIES<-

[Top Center]
One must see how this humerus <term>omero: the upper arm bone</term> is moved by 4 principal muscles, of which the two upper ones pull the bone upward and the two lower ones pull it downward; and then the two lower ones each divide into two, so that they pull the humerus forward or backward according to what is necessary for its function.

[Top Right]
The muscle that pulls its tendon will always be powerful enough to pull the object attached to it; and if the obstacle is more powerful than the muscle, that muscle will pull itself toward the obstacle. This is seen in someone who pulls themselves up a rope with their arms: they pull themselves to the rope rather than the rope to themselves, because the rope is tied and is stronger than the man.

[Middle Left - written vertically along the arm]
<margin>
Those muscles which move the arm by means of the bundle <term>siringa: literally 'tube' or 'syringe', used by Leonardo to describe bundles of muscle fibers or complex muscular structures</term> of the shoulder muscles, some of which were not a little difficult for you to find; and for this reason, you have depicted some of those that are on the outside so as not to impede the view of those on the inside.
</margin>

[Middle - near shoulder joint]
*m*, *n*, *o*, *p* are the 4 principal muscles of the humerus.

[Middle Right - next to the upright arm]
To see the shoulder well, and into how many parts it is divided, and how many are bundles <note>original: 'siringe'</note>, and how many are nerves <note>As noted previously, Leonardo often uses 'nervi' to describe tendons or cord-like structures</note>, and how many are veins...

[Bottom Left - study of forearm]
<note>Study of the musculature and tendons of the forearm and hand.</note>
This is the skeletal structure <term>ossame: the framework of bones</term> of the arm with its muscles and nerves...

[Bottom Right - study of the foot]
<note>Lateral view of the bones and ligaments of the human foot.</note>
This is the skeletal structure of the foot...

[At bottom, under the foot]
No animal can move itself if it does not bend some of its joints; and that bending will be all the more vigorous <note>original: 'gagliardo'</note> as the weight it has to move, or the motion it wishes to make, is greater...

<summary>This page explores the mechanical principles of the human body, detailing how the four muscles of the shoulder act as pulleys to move the humerus and outlining the necessity of joint flexion for animal movement. Leonardo uses the analogy of a man climbing a rope to explain the physics of muscular tension and resistance.</summary>

-># LEONARDO DA VINCI: ANATOMICAL STUDIES<-

One must see how this **humerus** <term>humerus: the upper arm bone (Italian: *omero*)</term> is moved by 4 principal muscles, of which the two upper ones pull the bone upward and the two lower ones pull it downward; and then the two lower ones each divide into two, so that they pull the humerus forward or backward according to what is necessary for its function.

The muscle that pulls its tendon will always be powerful enough to pull the object attached to it; and if the obstacle is more powerful than the muscle, that muscle will pull itself toward the obstacle. This is seen in someone who pulls themselves up a rope with their arms: they pull themselves to the rope rather than the rope to themselves, because the rope is tied and is stronger than the man. <note>Leonardo is applying the principles of mechanics and physics to the human body, treating muscles as levers and ropes.</note>

</margin>

<margin>Muscles and tendons.</margin>
[Fig. 220 :] b a
The muscles are of 2 <note>The transcription suggests Leonardo may have originally written '3' and corrected it to '2'.</note> shapes, with 2 different names, of which the shorter is called "muscle" (the *middle* part) and the longer is called the "lacertus" <term>lacertus: an anatomical term derived from the Latin for 'upper arm.' Leonardo uses it to distinguish the long, fleshy bundles of a muscle from the central 'belly' or the tendon.</term>.

<margin>Muscles of fat and thin individuals.</margin>
->Nature of muscles<-

The cords <term>cords: Leonardo uses the term *chorde* (cords) to refer to tendons.</term> of the muscles are of lesser or greater length according to whether the man has more fat or thinner fleshiness. And—

<summary>Leonardo outlines a "window" technique for anatomical drawing, where thin muscles are depicted with gaps to reveal deeper structures. --- Page 274 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s investigation into the physical mechanics of the musculoskeletal system. Following the previous discussion on the appearance of muscles in thin and fat individuals, he now defines the relationship between the fleshy "belly" of the muscle and its transition into the "cord" (tendon), as well as the numerical correspondence between muscles and tendons at a joint.</meta>

# LEONARDO DA VINCI

...fleshiness always withdraws toward its origin from the fleshy side in cases of leanness. And in the acquisition of fat <note>original: *pinguedine*</note>, it extends toward the origin of its cord <term>cord: Leonardo’s term for a tendon, the tough fibrous tissue connecting muscle to bone.</term>.

<margin>Termination of the muscles in tendons.</margin>
->How the muscles<-
->bind themselves to the joints of the bones<-

The end of every muscle is to transform into a cord, which binds the joint of the bone to which that muscle is joined.

->On the number of cords<-
->and of the muscles<-

There are as many cords—which one after another, layered over each other, cover one another and together cover and bind the joint of the bones to which they are joined—as there are muscles that converge toward that same joint.

<summary>Leonardo explains how muscle tissue shifts depending on body fat and defines the tendon as the functional "end" of a muscle that binds joints. He also observes that the number of individual tendons wrapping a joint corresponds exactly to the number of muscles serving that joint.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, muscles, tendons, cords, joints, physiology, bone structure, body composition</keywords>

While previous notes might have focused on the surface appearance of thin versus muscular men, here he begins to define the underlying mechanical structure—specifically how the "flesh" of the muscle transitions into the "cord" (tendon) to act upon the skeleton.</meta>

# LEONARDO DA VINCI

The fleshy <unclear>parts</unclear> in cases of thinness always pull back toward their origin from the fleshy side, and the result of fatness <note>original: *pinguedine*</note> extends toward the origin of the cord <term>cord: Leonardo's term for a tendon, the fibrous tissue that connects muscle to bone.</term>.

->the joints of the bones<-

The end of every muscle is to transform into a cord, which binds the joint of the bone to which that muscle is joined.

->On the number of cords<-
->and of the muscles<-

The number of cords which successively, one above the other, clothe and bind the joint of the bone to which they are attached—the number of muscles converging at the same joint corresponds to this same number.

<summary>Leonardo defines the relationship between muscles and tendons (which he calls "cords"), explaining that muscles terminate in tendons to bind to bone joints. He establishes a mathematical correspondence, noting that the number of tendons protecting a joint equals the number of muscles acting upon it.</summary>

--- Page 277 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s exploration of myology, specifically focusing on the lower limbs and hip joint. Building on the previous page's discussion of how muscles transition into tendons (cords) at the joints, Leonardo here applies those principles to the mechanics of the thigh and the complex range of motion permitted by the hip.</meta>

<note>Anatomical study of the leg muscles and lower torso of a male figure, shown from the front. Leonardo uses mirror writing and various labels (a, b, c, d, e, f, g) to identify specific muscles and bones. The text discusses the mechanics of movement, particularly the action of muscles on the femur (referred to as "fessolo") during walking.</note>

<margin>
The muscles of the hips and the buttocks are not visible in this view because they are covered by the view of the said muscles descending from the back of the thigh and the hip. The buttock muscles cannot be demonstrated unless the thigh is removed.
</margin>

<margin>
When muscle **a-b** contracts, it pulls toward itself the bone **d-e**, which creates the movement of the thigh from the front; and **a** acts as a reinforcement of reciprocal tensions <note>The concept of "reciprocal tension" refers to Leonardo's observation that muscles often work in opposing pairs to stabilize a joint.</note> or rather resistances in the opposite parts at the back of the thigh. These tensions are created by the said buttocks as they contract to raise the thigh straight up. It is also achieved with the help of muscle **f-g**, which, not being moved itself, does not pull because it is not pulled by other muscles; these muscles become tense by their own action or by the movement of the thigh.
</margin>

The reinforcements and the other musculatures of the hips end upon the head of the <term>fessolo: an archaic anatomical term used by Leonardo to refer to the femur or thigh bone.</term> of the thigh. They are designed to move the thigh in various directions: that is, straight forward and straight backward, and crosswise toward the hips, and likewise upward and downward, moving the **fessolo** bone in a circular motion through the movements of the hip. Therefore, it can be said that such muscles which join with the head of the **fessolo** of the thigh are made to provide the necessary movements to the entire thigh. These movements are infinite because they are circular; however, I will provide the principal ones here—that is, 4 or 8—moving the thigh through the motions of the hip: downward, upward, straight, or obliquely toward the sides, both on the inside and the outside.

<margin>
The reinforcements of the thigh that are on the outside are thicker than those on the inside, because the weight <note>original: *caricho*</note> of the body hangs more upon the outside than the inside when a man walks.
</margin>

Muscle **a-b** pulls the thigh forward, and muscle **c-d** pulls it backward.

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the musculature of the hip and thigh, describing the femur (fessolo) as a mechanical lever capable of infinite circular motion. He explains how muscles work in reciprocal tension to stabilize the body's weight during locomotion.</summary>

<keywords>anatomy, femur, myology, biomechanics, hip joint, locomotion, reciprocal tension, human movement</keywords>

### ON ANATOMY. — FOLIO A. — 15 RECTO.
14

<margin>Muscles of the anterior <note>front</note> region of the thigh.</margin>
If the junction of muscle **b** is made with the bone of the thigh and truly with muscle **a**, or rather if muscle **b** and muscle **a**, after they have been joined together, are united and stabilized upon that bone of the thigh; and this 3rd way is more useful for the benefit of the motion of that thigh and more secure, because if muscle **a** were cut or injured in another way, muscle **b** by itself would move the thigh, which it could not do if it were separated from the bone between **b** and **a**.

<margin>Great trochanter <note>The bony projection at the top of the femur (thigh bone) where major muscles attach.</note></margin>
The pole *n* <note>Leonardo often used the term "pole" to refer to a center of rotation or a specific landmark</note> will always be found, in well-proportioned men, level with the fork of the thighs.

<margin>Ileo-inguinal region.</margin>
The prominence of the flank *m* consists of skin and thin flesh, but it is made prominent by the colon on the left side and by the <term>monoculus<note>original: "monochulo". A historical anatomical term for the cecum, the pouch-like beginning of the large intestine, so-called because it has only one "eye" or opening.</note></term> on the right.

<margin>Linings of the joints.</margin>
There are as many membranes <note>original: "panniculi", referring to layers of connective tissue or fascia</note> covering the joints of the bones, one on top of the other, as there are muscles that converge at the end of each bone.

<margin>Rules for the study of muscles.</margin>
Describe each muscle individually: its shape, the locations where it terminates <note>its points of attachment to the bone</note>, and its substance; and ensure that this is never lacking. You shall proceed as I have illustrated below.

[Fig. 222 :] *a b c d*

<margin>
A muscle of the hip.

Piriform muscle.
</margin>
$b\ c$ is a fleshy muscle, and it is round on the outside and obtuse-angled on the inside, in this manner [Fig. 223.]; its fibers <note>original: "fili", literally "threads"</note> converge at the end and the beginning of this muscle—

<margin>
Tensor of the broad fascia.
</margin> <note>original: "M. tensor fasciae latae"</note>
$c\ d$ is a round tendon; it originates at the extremity of the muscle below and terminates in the ligament that wraps <note>original: "fascia", meaning to bandage or wrap</note> the knee.

<margin>
Muscles of the hip, and crural extensor.
</margin>
There are four muscles that descend from the hip $m$ and terminate in the ischium <note>original: "scia" or "ascia", referring to the lower and back part of the hip bone</note> $n$, which are $a, b, c, d$; $e$, a muscle, originates in the ischium $n$ and attaches along the entire length of the thigh bone.

To him for whom it seems too much, let him take away; to him for whom it seems too little, let him add; to him for whom it is sufficient, let him praise the first composer of such a <term>machine<note>Leonardo viewed the human body as a masterpiece of mechanical engineering, often using the word "machine" (machina) to describe its functional design.</note></term>.

. $h$ . is joined with the skin more than . $e$ . or any other part.

<meta>This page continues Leonardo's systematic investigation into the mechanical and artistic representation of the human body. While the previous page focused on the specific muscles of the hip, this sheet broadens the scope to discuss the methodology of anatomical drawing and the functional relationship between muscles, tendons, and bones. It serves as a bridge between pure scientific dissection and the practical application of anatomy for painters.</meta>

<note>Anatomical study of the leg and torso showing the superficial and deep musculature. Leonardo explores how to represent these for painters and examines the functional connection of muscles and tendons.</note>

### [Note on Anatomical Representation]

In this demonstration, let the muscles be small so that their true location and that of others may be seen, and so that one does not hide another.
In this other demonstration, let the muscles be thicker so that the true movement and true function <note>original: "uffitio," meaning the specific duty or mechanical role of a body part</note> of each may be understood.
In this other one, let the muscles be divided from one another so that their true origin <note>original: "prynçypyo"</note> and true end [insertion] may be understood.
In this other, let the muscles be shown as the branches of a single muscle, as if they were branches of the same nerve <note>Leonardo often used the word "nerve" (neruo) to describe what we now distinguish as nerves, tendons, or sinews, viewing them as a single continuous system of tension.</note>.

---

<note>Sketches of muscle attachments and fiber directions</note>

<margin>
**[Top right column]**
Those hollows that are formed in the nude figure <note>the naked human form</note> must not <unclear>because</unclear> be made <unclear>with a line</unclear>, but they must be the origins of those muscles.
</margin>

---

<note>Text integrated with the central leg drawing</note>

<margin>
**[Left of central leg]**
The nude study of this figure must have the muscles well-expressed, so that the painter may demonstrate with truth the power <term>valitudine<note>from the Latin 'valitudo', meaning health, strength, or physical robustness</note></term> of each, not showing one muscle for another. And especially in the violent movements of that man, he [the painter] should have it in his mind—having no doubts <note>original: "pēti," likely short for 'pentimenti', meaning corrections or hesitations</note>—to show how that limb must hang <note>original: "pichare," to hang or attach</note>; and he must do the same with the other views already agreed upon, placed in whichever nude figure he wishes.
</margin>

<margin>
**[Between the two legs]**
That part of the muscle which attaches to the bone should not be muscle, but a very hard cord <note>original: "chorda," Leonardo's term for a tendon</note> which does not change during the expansion or the shortening of the muscle; nor should it be understood as acting in the nude figure as the rest of the muscle does, but it remains always the same in position and appearance.

If muscle .a. .b. were a single muscle, it could not <unclear>because</unclear> while it pulled to attach; therefore, it is necessary that they be divided by branches of members <note>sections of the muscle</note> which, as you see, attach into the same bones.
</margin>

---

### [Reflection on Nature]

O speculation of this Nature! O how many times you have made me ponder and ponder again <note>original: "pēdare e ripēdare," to weigh or consider repeatedly</note> what might be the reason why the muscles of the upper members are parts of the same member, and from which branches they divide, and how they enclose themselves and attach to the same bones, and how these muscles are not parts of the same member nor of the same bone.

# ON ANATOMY — FOLIO A — 15 VERSO

---

<margin>Precepts for the study of anatomy.</margin>
The views of this figure should be five: from the front, from the back, in profile, one that is between a profile and a back view, and one that is between a profile and a chest view.

<margin>The forward flexion movement of the thigh. Precepts.</margin>
When the two muscles **a r** <note>These letters refer to labels on a drawing (not included in the transcription) showing the quadriceps or hip flexors.</note> pull, the leg is raised forward, and the two muscles **b c** relax while **d** lengthens. Describe this rule for the function of all muscles, and you will be able to depict nearly every movement without error, almost without even needing to see a living model.

<margin>The buzzing of flies.</margin>
That flies have their "voice" in their wings, you will see by clipping them a little, or truly by smearing them slightly with honey so that their flight is not entirely prevented. You will see that the sound made by the movement of the wings becomes hoarse, and the "voice" will change from high-pitched to deep in proportion to how much the wings are hindered.

<margin>The relationship between nerves and muscles.</margin>
The nerve always enters the muscle at its thickest part, and it directs its branching toward the <term>corda</term> <note>original: "corda"; Leonardo uses this term to refer to the tendon.</note> that originates from that muscle.

---

<summary>Leonardo outlines a five-point perspective method for anatomical illustration, explains the reciprocal action of muscles (agonist and antagonist) during leg movement, and describes an experiment using honey to prove that a fly's buzz is produced by its wings.</summary>
<keywords>anatomical illustration, muscle mechanics, flexion, insect acoustics, tendons, neuro-muscular anatomy</keywords>

# LEONARDO DA VINCI

<margin>Diagram of the relationships between muscles, nerves, and vessels.</margin>

[Fig. 224 :] o m S t c h K
**o** — sensation || nerve
**m** — force | <term>tendon</term> <note>original: "corda"; Leonardo used this word to describe the tough, fibrous tendons that transmit the "force" of the muscle to the bone.</note>
**S** — nourishment / vein
**t** — the vital spirit — artery <note>original: "lo spirito"; according to the medical theories of Leonardo's time, arteries carried "vital spirits" (a sort of life-force or refined air) rather than just blood.</note>
**c** — motion || muscle

First, illustrate the shape of the muscle along with its tendons; then show within it the location of the arteries, tendons, and veins; and then describe its necessary function.

And first, create the "tree" <note>Leonardo uses the term "tree" (albero) to describe the branching, organic structure of the vascular and nervous systems.</note> of these nerves, veins, and arteries.

<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page's identification of the internal components of a muscle—nerves, tendons, veins, and arteries—Leonardo now applies these principles to the specific anatomy of the chest wall. ### ON ANATOMY — FOLIO A — 15 VERSO

[Fig. 228 :] a b c n m o
True arrangement of the muscles.

[Fig. 229 :] a b c d n m o
The muscles **n m o** are 7 <note>Leonardo identifies seven specific muscle segments in this sketch of the intercostals.</note>, which bind the ribs **a b c** that are attached to the thorax.

<margin>
Their action in respiration and the mechanism of respiration.
</margin>

These muscles have both voluntary and involuntary motion, because they are the ones that open and close the lungs. They open [the lungs] when they cease their function—that is, <term>contracting</term> <note>original: "raccordarsi"; Leonardo uses this to describe the shortening of muscle fibers during active tension.</note>. At that time the ribs, which were previously pulled and constrained together by the shortening of these muscles, are then set free and return to their natural distance. Then the chest expands; and because a <term>vacuum</term> <note>original: "vacuo"; Leonardo adheres to the classical physical principle that "nature abhors a vacuum."</note> cannot exist in nature, the lung, which touched the ribs from the inside, must necessarily follow their expansion. Thus, as the lung opens like a <term>bellows</term> <note>original: "mantace"; specifically a forge bellows, a common mechanical metaphor in Leonardo's work.</note>, it draws in the air that fills the space created for it.

---

[Fig. 228 :] *a b c n m o*.
True arrangement of the muscles.
[Fig. 229 :] *a b c d n m o*.
The muscles *n m o* are 7, which bind the ribs *a b c*, attached to the thorax.
<note>This section repeats the content above, originally provided in a French translation within the source manuscript.</note>

<margin>
Their action in respiration and the mechanism of respiration.
</margin>

These muscles have a voluntary and involuntary movement, for it is they which open and which compress the lung; they open it when they suspend their function, that is to say, of shortening themselves; for, meanwhile, the ribs, which were at first drawn and forced together by the shortening of these muscles, then remain at liberty and return to their natural distance, then the chest dilates; and because there can be no vacuum in nature, the lung, which on the inside touches the ribs, must follow their dilation; and thus the lung, in opening like a forge bellows, attracts the air, which fills the space provided to receive it.

<summary>Leonardo describes the mechanical process of breathing, explaining how the intercostal muscles contract and relax to move the ribs. He invokes the physical principle that "nature abhors a vacuum" to explain how the expansion of the chest cavity forces the lungs to draw in air like a bellows.</summary>

<keywords>respiration, anatomy, lungs, intercostal muscles, thorax, bellows, vacuum, biomechanics</keywords>

--- Page 290 ---
<meta>Continuity: Building on the previous description of the intercostal muscles as a "bellows" for the lungs, Leonardo now explains how this same respiratory cycle acts as a mechanical pump for the digestive system, using the diaphragm as the intermediary.</meta>

# LEONARDO DA VINCI

<margin>
Influence of respiration on the movements of the intestine.
</margin>

[Fig. 230 :] *b d a c e*

The shortening and extension performed by the aforementioned muscles are the cause of the continuous motion of the <term>fecal matter</term> <note>original: "fecce"; Leonardo is describing peristalsis, the involuntary constriction and relaxation of the muscles of the intestine.</note> within the intestines. This is proved [as follows]: **a b c d** is the space where the lung sits in the chest; **b a** and **b c** are the ribs of the chest, which open and close the space **a c**. 

As stated above, when this space **a c** opens, the **diaphragm** <note>original: "diafrãma"</note>—a thick <term>membrane</term> <note>original: "pannicol grosso"; Leonardo uses this to describe the muscular/fibrous sheet of the diaphragm.</note> that is placed between the intestines and the lung—becomes straightened due to the dilation of the ribs. In thus straightening itself, it diminishes the space **a d c e** in which the aforementioned intestines are enclosed; and so, as this space decreases and increases, the intestines also expand and then contract when they are pressed. This process continues for as long as there is life.

<margin>
Influence of respiration on the movements of the intestine.
</margin>

[Fig. 230 :] *b d a c e*.
The shortening and extension of the said muscles are the reason for the continuous movement given to the waste in the intestines. This is proven: **a b c d** is the space occupied by the lung in the chest; **b a** and **b c** are the ribs of the chest, which open and tighten the space **a c**. As mentioned earlier, by opening this space **a c**, the diaphragm, a thick membrane interposed between the intestines and the lung, comes to stretch itself out through the expansion of the ribs. By extending in this way, it restricts the space **a d c e** in which the said intestines are confined. Thus, through the shrinking and enlargement of this space, the intestines also dilate and then restrict when they are pressed, and this function lasts as long as life itself.

<summary>Leonardo explains the mechanical relationship between the respiratory system and the digestive tract, illustrating how the movement of the ribs and diaphragm physically compresses the intestines to aid in the passage of waste.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, diaphragm, respiration, intestines, mechanics of the body, peristalsis, ribs, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

LEONARDO DA VINCI
f. 90r

# 102. b d a c e

The shortening and the stretching performed by the <unclear>ligaments</unclear> <note>original: "legami"; Leonardo often uses this term to describe muscle fibers or tendons.</note> called muscles are the <unclear>cause</unclear> of providing <unclear>continuous</unclear> motion to the waste of the <unclear>intestines</unclear>, as is proven. In **be**, in the space where the lung stays in the chest within the ribs—which precisely enclose the space as mentioned above—in the <unclear>lower</unclear> part by the diaphragm <note>original: "diafrāma"</note>, a thick membrane <term>pannicolo</term> <note>A Renaissance anatomical term for a membrane or layer of tissue.</note> that is placed between the intestines <unclear>and</unclear> the lung: during the expansion of the ribs, [the diaphragm] becomes straightened, and being thus straightened, it diminishes the space at **c e** in which the aforementioned intestines are enclosed. -> ...the continual movement of the excrement of the intestines. This point is proven. <summary>Leonardo explains how the diaphragm and ribs act as a mechanical pump during breathing, asserting that the physical compression of the abdominal cavity is what moves waste through the intestines.</summary>

--- Page 293 ---
<meta>Continuity: While the previous page examined the internal pressure exerted by the diaphragm on the digestive system, Leonardo now shifts his focus to the external mechanics of the torso, specifically the complex muscular "pulleys" that control the movement of the neck, shoulders, and upper back.</meta>

Every muscle moves its bone along the line of that muscle; and if such a muscle should have several heads, as do the muscles of the shoulder, that movement will be performed by the force of the line of whichever head of the muscle is being pulled.

<note>Top center drawing: Study of the posterior shoulder muscles.</note>

This muscle **b c d f** will be that which pulls the arm backward and upward; and if it is pulled along with muscle **m n**, there will be a mixed movement between backward and upward, and between backward and downward.

<note>Top right drawing: Lateral view of the shoulder and upper arm muscles.</note>

These muscles **n o p** are those that raise and move the neck backward.

<note>Middle row of drawings: Three anatomical sketches showing the muscles of the neck and chest from anterior and lateral perspectives.</note>

<margin>
Note how the cords of the muscles that move the neck forward are bound with the ribs and with the shoulder.
</margin>

The muscle **a b c** will be that which pulls the humerus <note>original: "omero"; refers to the upper arm bone or the shoulder assembly.</note> toward the shoulder; and this muscle has many heads, and therefore can perform many movements.

<note>Lower middle: Small diagram showing lines of force (vectors) diverging from a central point, labeled with letters p, o, q, r, s, t.</note>

The muscles **n o p q r s t** are those that support the neck so that it does not bend in any direction except with effort.

<note>Bottom right drawing: Detailed study of the muscle cords and nerves descending from the neck to the shoulder and chest.</note>

If you remove muscle **m n o p q r**, you will find beneath it muscles that are bound with the vertebrae <note>original: "spondili"; the individual bones of the spine.</note> of the neck, with the ribs, and with the shoulder.

# ON ANATOMY — FOLIO A — 16 RECTO.

### FOLIO 16 — *recto*.

<margin>General precepts.</margin>
Begin your anatomy with the perfect man, then make him old and less muscular; then go on stripping him by degrees down to the bones.

And the child you will do later, with the demonstration of the <term>womb</term><note>original: "matrice"; refers to the uterus or the environment of the fetus.</note>.

<margin>Muscles of the back.</margin>
Describe and illustrate the muscles of the inner neck included between the spine and the <term>esophagus</term><note>original: "meri"; a term Leonardo adopted from Medieval Latin translations of Arabic medical texts (al-marī') to refer to the gullet or esophagus.</note>.

<margin>Respiratory muscles.</margin>
When you have finished the muscles that serve the movement of the <term>scapula</term><note>original: "spatula"; the shoulder blade.</note>, remove that scapula and illustrate the 3 muscles *n m o*, which serve only the benefit of the breath.

<margin>Mechanism of movements for the muscles.</margin>
[Fig. 231:] 1st, 2nd rib
Every muscle moves the limb joined to it along the line of the fibers of which that muscle is composed.

<margin>Respiratory muscles.</margin>
The 3 muscles *n m o* of the 3rd demonstration raise and open the 3 <note>The text cuts off here, but refers to the ribs mentioned in the diagram.</note>

<summary>Leonardo outlines a systematic approach to anatomical dissection, moving from the ideal male form to various stages of life, while defining his "mechanical" law that muscles only pull along the path of their constituent fibers.</summary>

<margin>Relationship between the volume of the muscle and its function.</margin>

*n m o* are 3 muscles which pull the tendons fixed to the <term>vertebrae</term><note>original: "spondili"; from the Greek 'sphondylos', referring to the individual bones of the spine.</note> | of the neck, and as the vertebrae cannot move toward | the muscle, the muscle lifts itself | together with the rib where it | is joined toward that vertebra; and this | causes the turning of the chest around while sit- | ting ———

***

You will note whether the muscles that connect the ribs together, interposing themselves in their intervals, whether their lengths are directed toward the neck, or not.

<margin>Relationship between the volume of the muscle and its function.</margin>
In all the places where man has to exert himself with greater effort, there nature has made the muscles and the tendons of greater thickness and width.

<margin>Muscles of the posterior region of the neck and their function.</margin>
*n, m, o* are three muscles, which pull the tendons fixed to the vertebrae of the neck, and not being able to move the vertebrae toward the muscle, the muscle lifts itself together with the rib, where it is joined toward such a vertebra, and this causes the turning of the chest around, while sitting.

The vertebrae that the spine of the 5th demonstration has, have the muscles that pull them in a contrary motion to that which the muscles of the 4th demonstration perform; and this is done because such vertebrae would break in the bowing of the head, if the muscles below did not exert a force in the opposite direction.

***

You shall observe the muscles that connect the ribs together, by placing themselves in their intervals, whether their lengths are directed toward the neck or not.

<margin>Relationship of the volume of the muscle with the function.</margin>
Nature has made the muscles and the tendons thicker and wider in all the parts of man by which he must exert himself with more difficulty.

<margin>Muscles of the posterior region of the neck and their function.</margin>
*n m o* these are three muscles which pull the tendons fixed to the vertebrae of the neck; and as the vertebrae cannot move toward the muscle, the muscle lifts itself, with the rib to which it is attached, toward this vertebra, and this is the cause of the turning movement of the chest when one is sitting.

The vertebrae which are in the spine of the 5th demonstration have the muscles which pull them by a motion contrary to that made by the muscles of the 4th demonstration; and that is done because these vertebrae would break when the head inclines, if the muscles from below did not make an effort in the contrary direction.

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the mechanical action of the muscles connecting the ribs and neck, explaining how they facilitate the rotation of the torso and act as stabilizing "counter-forces" to prevent injury to the spine when the head moves.</summary>
<keywords>muscles, ribs, neck, vertebrae, anatomy, biomechanics, counter-force, tendons</keywords>

# ON ANATOMY — FOLIA A — 16 VERSO

### FOLIO 16 — *verso.*

<margin>
The function of the muscles in respiration.
</margin>

Man is treated here according to the <term>instrumental method</term><note>Leonardo approaches anatomy as a branch of mechanics, viewing the body as a machine or "instrument" governed by physical laws.</note> and not <note>The text likely intended to say "not merely descriptive" or "not philosophical," as he focuses on how the body works rather than just how it looks.</note>.

Concerning the muscles, it happens almost universally that they do not move the member where they are fixed; instead, they move the member where the <term>nerve</term><note>original: "neruo"; in this context, Leonardo uses this term to refer to the tendons or cord-like sinews that transmit the muscle's pulling force to the bone.</note> produced by the muscle is joined—except for that which raises and moves the ribs for the service of breathing.

All these muscles are elevators of the ribs, and the lifting of the ribs is the dilation of the chest; and the dilation of the chest is the expansion of the lungs; and the expansion of the lungs is the drawing in of the air, which penetrates through the mouth into the increased capacity of those lungs.

[Fig. 239.]

<margin>
Muscles of the back.
</margin>

And these same muscles hold the spine of the neck straight, when <note>The sentence in the original manuscript is left incomplete.</note>

<summary>Leonardo explains the mechanical process of respiration, describing how specific muscles lift the ribs to expand the chest and lungs to draw in air. He also identifies the secondary function of these muscles in maintaining the posture of the neck.</summary>
<keywords>respiration, muscles, lungs, rib cage, mechanical anatomy, spine of the neck, inhalation, instrumental method</keywords>

# ON ANATOMY — FOLIO A — 16 VERSO.

...tendons, and how many are the functions of the actions of this neck; and this first one, which is noted here, is how the ribs in their strength support the spine of the neck upright. By means of the tendons that rise to that spine, these tendons perform a double office: that is, they support the spine by means of the ribs, and they support the ribs by means of the spine. Such a duality of powers, situated at the opposite ends of such a tendon, works with that tendon no differently than a string works with the ends of a bow. But this gathering of muscles at the spine supports it upright, just as the ropes of ships support their mast; and the same ropes, tied to the mast, also support in part the sides of the ships to which they are joined.

<margin>
Because nature cannot produce the movement of any limb with the <term>cord-like nerves</term> <note>original: "nerbi"; Leonardo used this term to refer to tendons and muscle fibers as well as nerves, viewing them all as part of the body's mechanical tension system.</note> that cause it to move, without the help of other nerves which hold firm that part from which the first nerve moves...
</margin>

<margin>
Whether it is necessary that every movement of the bone of the neck be made by opposing nerves...
</margin>

The motor muscles of the **B** <note>Referring to a labeled diagram, likely the humerus or upper arm.</note> to straighten it or bend it; then separately show the muscles originating in that **humerus** <note>original: "aiutorio"; a term from medieval anatomy derived from the Latin "adjutorium," used by Leonardo to refer to the upper arm bone.</note> which only serve to rotate the **B** when it turns the hand upside down. Then figure in the **B** only the muscles that move the hand up and down, and here and there, without moving the fingers in it; then figure the muscles that only move the fingers, those clenching or extending or dilating or approaching. But first figure the whole, as is done in **cosmography** <note>original: "chosmografia"; Leonardo uses the analogy of mapping the whole world before detailing specific regions to explain his systematic anatomical method.</note>, and then divide into the aforementioned parts, and the same you shall do for the thigh, leg, and feet. ———

->### On the muscles established in the ribs figured<-
->### above ———<-

<margin>
Considerations on the arrangement of the muscles of the neck and the spinal column.
</margin>

I have for a long time, and not without cause, doubted whether the muscles that are established under the **shoulder blades** <note>original: "spatole"; referring to the scapulae.</note> above the 3rd, 4th, and 5th right ribs, and as many on the left side, are made for the benefit of supporting the spine upright—

--- Page 305 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page's discussion on the anatomical structure of the arm and chest, Leonardo shifts his focus here to the science of optics and perspective, specifically how atmosphere and light affect our perception of distant objects, before returning to brief anatomical and mechanical observations in the margins.</meta>

# Of the first figures that appear to human sight
->at their initial distances<-

Of the first figures that present themselves to human sight at their initial distances <note>Leonardo is referring to the point where an object first becomes visible as it approaches or as the eye focuses on the distance.</note>, they will be without any particular knowledge of their limbs, but will only be known by their universal shape; and among these, the darkest will be known first; and these will be less dark than those in the darkest places. And so, among the brightest, the clearest and those most distinct from the air and dark places will be known first; but those that are darker against light air will be better known than those that are lighter against dark air. 

A visible object without mist appears darker at a long distance than in nearby locations; and this happens because it is bordered by air more luminous than itself. But if it is within mist, it appears so much lighter the more remote it is, and this happens because the whiteness of the mist is interposed between the eye and the object.

->Way of seeing figures<-
->and other things in the mist<-

If you see figures in the mist, know that the one closest to you will be darkest and will have, among the others, the most defined outlines. But if the sun is in the mist, then the figure closest to you will be lighter and its boundaries less defined; and this will be due to the solar rays passing through that mist and radiating in the air of the mist which is near the eye, which is between the eye and the figure; and the same happens in turbid water. 

From mist radiated by the sun, the dark part of the visible object is seen more than the light part, because one is seen through more radiated air, and the other is seen through less radiated air. If you see figures or other visible bodies within the thickest mist, know that the further away they are, the less they will be seen and they will be of a more uncertain color; and this arises because the mist mixed with the light of the sun appears whitish, and that which is mixed with the dark air appears of a bluish color <note>original: "azzurroggiante". Leonardo's observation of the "blue of the distance" is a cornerstone of his technique of atmospheric perspective.</note>.

# Of visible objects further away in the mist
->as it appears in the dark<-

If you see figures in mist that is radiated by the sun, that radiated mist will be so much lighter the closer it is to the sun; therefore the figures seen in it will be so much darker the closer they are to the sun. But if these figures are placed in a location further from the sun than the seeing eye is, those figures will be so much lighter the further they are from the seeing eye; and this arises because the air will be more radiated the closer it is to the sun; therefore the visible object seen through that air will be seen through lighter air. 

But on the opposite side toward the darkness, you will see the figures so much lighter as they are closer, and so much darker as they are more remote; and this arises because the air between the eye and the visible object is lighter the closer it is to the light. But the air that is in a dark place will be so much darker the further it is from the light. However, in luminous air, the eye sees through air that is so much lighter the further it is from the eye; therefore the visible object seen through lighter air will be lighter, and that which is seen through less light air will be darker. But in dark air, the nearby thing seen through the lighter air will appear lighter; and that which is seen through darker air will appear darker. 

Through thick mist, dark things are seen more than light things; and in thin air, light things are seen more than dark things; and this happens because dark things contrast with the whiteness of the mist, and in the light things of thin air, nothing contrasts.

---

ON ANATOMY — FOLIO A — 17 RECTO.

### FOLIO 17 — recto.

[Fig. 242.]
[Fig. 243.]

<margin>Lower limb. Forearm and hand. Rules for the study of the hand.</margin>
When you represent the hand, represent with it
the arm as far as the elbow, and with that the nerves <note>original: "nervi"; in this context, Leonardo often uses "nerves" to refer to tendons or sinews</note>
and muscles which come to move it from outside the
elbow ——— and do the same in the demonstration of the foot.

<margin>Insertion of the muscles according to their position in the limbs.</margin>
All the muscles that arise in the shoulders, shoulder blade <note>original: "spatola" (scapula)</note>, and chest
serve the movement of the arm from the shoulder to the elbow. And
all the muscles that arise between the shoulder and the elbow
serve the movement of the arm between the elbow and the hand. And
all the muscles that arise between the elbow and the hand
serve the movement of the hand. And all the muscles
that arise in the neck serve the movement of the head and of the
shoulders ———

<margin>Rules for the study of the leg.</margin>
When you represent the muscles of the thigh, represent with
them the bone of the leg <note>referring to the tibia and fibula below the knee</note> so that it may be known where such
muscles connect with these bones of the legs.

<summary>Leonardo outlines fundamental rules for anatomical illustration, explaining that muscles should be drawn alongside the bones and joints they move to clarify their mechanical function. He establishes a hierarchical rule: muscles originating in one section of a limb are responsible for moving the section immediately following it.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, muscular system, musculoskeletal mechanics, drawing precepts, forearm, hand, thigh, shoulder, scapula</keywords>

# LEONARDO DA VINCI

Then you shall represent the leg with its muscles joined to the bones of the foot, and represent the bones as bare. You shall follow a similar rule for all the nerves <note>original: "nervi"; Leonardo typically uses this term to refer to tendons or sinews rather than the modern neurological definition</note>.

<margin>Muscles for the movement of the foot.</margin>
The muscles of the foot serve the movement of its toes, and in such movement they are aided by the cords <note>tendons</note> originating from the muscles of the leg.

<margin>Precepts for the demonstration of the muscles of the leg and foot.</margin>
Identify which are the muscles of the leg that serve only the simple movement of the foot, and which are those of that leg that serve only the simple movement of the toes of such a foot. And remember, when clothing <note>the process of layering anatomical structures over the skeleton</note> the bones of the leg with its muscles, to first depict the muscles that move the foot, which you shall join to those feet.

<margin>The foot of man and of other animals.</margin>
Depict here the foot of the bear, the monkey, and other animals, showing how they differ from the foot of man; also include the feet of certain birds.

<margin>Number of the muscles of the lower limbs.</margin>
There are as many muscles in the leg, from the knee to the joint of the foot, as there are cords <note>tendons</note> that join to the upper part of the toes, and likewise below, adding those that move the foot upward and—

--- Page 313 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page follows the anatomical observations from the previous leaf (f. 19013r), continuing Leonardo’s deep investigation into the lower limbs. While the previous page focused on the mechanical structure of the leg, this verso (back) side provides a famous and deeply personal defense of his scientific method, specifically regarding the anatomy of the foot.</meta>

<margin>
**Anatomy of the nerves of the sole of the foot**

These are the nerves of the toes, and they are located near the toes, not through the center of them, but they divide and go to the sides of the said toes; and the veins and the arteries do the very same.
</margin>

O you who claim it is better to see an actual <term>dissection</term><note>original: "notomia"; in this context, it refers to the act of cutting open a body for study</note> performed than to see such drawings: you would be right if it were possible to see all these things that are demonstrated in such drawings in a single dissection. In that single act, with all your industry, you would see and gain knowledge of only a few veins. To obtain a true and complete knowledge of these, I have dissected more than ten human bodies, destroying all other members and consuming, in the smallest particles, all the flesh that was found around those veins, without making them bloody—except for an imperceptible staining from the <term>capillary veins</term><note>The smallest blood vessels; Leonardo was among the first to observe the intricate network of the vascular system at this scale</note>. A single body did not last long enough, so it was necessary to proceed in stages over many bodies, so that complete knowledge might be achieved; I repeated this twice to observe the differences <note>Leonardo is noting his use of comparative anatomy and the scientific method—checking his results against multiple specimens to account for individual variation</note>.

And even if you had a love for such things, you might be prevented by your stomach <note>Referring to the nausea and physical revulsion caused by the smell and sight of decaying remains</note>; and if that did not prevent you, you would perhaps be prevented by the fear of living through the night hours in the company of such dead bodies, quartered and flayed and terrifying to behold. And if this did not prevent you, perhaps you would lack the skill in drawing which is necessary for such representation; and even if you had the skill in drawing, it might not be accompanied by <term>perspective</term><note>The technique of representing three-dimensional objects on a two-dimensional surface</note>; and if it were so accompanied, you might lack the method of geometrical demonstrations and the method of calculating the forces and power of the muscles; and perhaps you would lack patience, so that you would not be diligent. Whether all these things have been found in me or not, the **120 books** composed by me will give the verdict of "yes" or "no." In these, I have been hindered neither by avarice nor by negligence, but only by time.

->Farewell.<-
<note>original: "Vale"; a traditional Latin closing</note>

<summary>Leonardo da Vinci defends his anatomical illustrations as superior to live dissections, citing the immense labor of dissecting over ten bodies and the various technical and psychological hurdles he overcame to produce his 120 notebooks.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, dissection, nerves, foot, scientific method, Renaissance art, medical illustration, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

--- Page 314 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page transitions from Leonardo’s spirited defense of his anatomical method—relying on multiple dissections to reveal truths hidden by blood and tissue—into a practical application of his findings. Here, he shifts from the vascular system of the foot to the biomechanics of the lower leg, treating the human body as a sophisticated machine governed by the laws of physics and leverage.</meta>

<note>Schematic diagram of a foot, illustrating the mechanical leverage of the Achilles tendon. The diagram shows the foot as a rigid bar pivoting on the ankle joint.</note>

<margin>
Nothing moves
unless it is pulled or pushed;
and if a thing moves
more than the force
that moves it, its
motion will be weak
compared with the force of the other. <note>Leonardo is reflecting on the principles of inertia and the relationship between a "motor" (muscle) and the resistance it overcomes.</note>
</margin>

<margin>
And if a man
rises up on
the tips of his feet,
all the weight
of the man goes
upon the toes
of the feet.
</margin>

...and a single cord pulls the heel,
which is attached to the <term>calcaneus</term><note>original: "chalchagnio"; the heel bone</note> of the foot,
by which the foot moves
with great force and with little
effort by the <term>motor</term><note>The muscle providing the power; in this case, the calf muscles.</note> because
the foot is a <term>lever</term> and its
<term>fulcrum</term><note>original: "fultimento"; the pivot point of a lever.</note> is in the <term>ankle-joint</term><note>original: "nocha"</note>
of the foot, and the weight is in the
tip of the foot, and the force
is in the bone of the heel; the
said cord, by being
quite distant from the
fulcrum, moves the foot with
great force.

And this is proven because
the further the cord is
distant from the fulcrum, the
more force it uses to move its
weight, and the more slowly
it moves. <note>Leonardo correctly identifies that a longer lever arm provides greater mechanical advantage but requires more time or distance to complete the movement.</note>

O, see how Nature
has made the <term>Achilles tendon</term><note>original: "chorda del tallone"; literally the "cord of the heel."</note>
so thick because
it must sustain the whole
load of the man when raising
himself on tip-toes.

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the human foot as a mechanical lever, explaining how the Achilles tendon acts upon the heel bone to lift the body's weight onto the toes. He marvels at the biological engineering of the tendon, which is sized precisely to withstand the immense forces required for movement.</summary>
<keywords>biomechanics, Achilles tendon, calcaneus, lever, fulcrum, physics of motion, ankle anatomy, Renaissance engineering</keywords>

--- Page 315 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous study (A 17) where Leonardo analyzed the foot as a mechanical lever powered by the calf muscles, this page moves deeper into the internal anatomy. He transitions from the external "motor" forces to the delicate "inner workings"—what he calls the soul of the foot—focusing on the specific distribution of muscles and the structural foundation of the bones.</meta>

<note>Anatomical study of the bones, tendons, and muscles of a human foot, medial perspective.</note>

O you who discourse on the nature of the foot,
do not find it burdensome to consider for a moment the **soul**
<note>original: "anima"; Leonardo uses this term here to refer to the internal arrangement or the "vital design" that makes the foot function, rather than a theological soul.</note> of the foot which I have here <unclear>laid out</unclear> and depicted in this [drawing];
and if it pleases you, thank the supreme author of nature
who has given you such a tool and such a function. See how
nature has distributed the nerves, the muscles, and the tendons
in such a way that nothing is lacking and nothing is superfluous;
and consider also the reason for these cords, how they
pull and release in various ways according to the needs
of the **joint** <note>original: "artichulazione"</note> of the foot.

Why did nature not make the 10 muscles of the sole of the foot
all of the same shape, and have them all insert into the same
toes? The reason is that the toes of the foot do not all have
the same functions; that is, the big toe has to support the weight
of the body while walking more than the other toes, and for this
reason it is made larger and with more powerful muscles. The other toes
serve more for the stability of the sole of the foot than for the
strength of walking, except in certain cases of jumping or
running fast where they are all utilized.

How well ordered are the bones of the foot,
and how well they are joined one to the other to make a firm foundation.

<note>List of technical terms used in the study:</note>
*foot, nature, soul (inner mechanism), muscles, tendons, nerves, function, joint, bones, foundation*

<summary>Leonardo explores the biomechanics of the human foot, arguing that its complex arrangement of tendons and bones is a perfect design where every part serves a specific functional purpose. He specifically highlights the unique role of the big toe in bearing weight during locomotion compared to the stabilizing role of the smaller toes.</summary>

<keywords>anatomy, biomechanics, foot, tendons, musculoskeletal system, big toe, nature's design, Renaissance science</keywords>

### ON ANATOMY. — FOLIO A. — 17 RECTO.

...feet; and likewise below, adding those which move the feet up and down, and this way and that; of which those that raise the toes are five. And there are as many muscles of the feet, below and above, as there are toes when the number is doubled; but because I have not yet finished such a discussion, I will leave it for the present, and this winter of 1510 I believe I shall finish all such anatomy. <note>Leonardo often set ambitious deadlines for his anatomical treatises, though many remained unfinished at his death.</note>

<margin>
Extensor and flexor muscles of the foot.
</margin>

The tendons <term>tendons: Leonardo uses the word *corde* (cords or strings) to describe these fibrous tissues</term> that lower the toes originate from muscles born in the sole of those feet; but the tendons that raise those same toes do not originate in the external <note>original: *silvestra*, a term used in older anatomy to refer to the outer or lateral part of a limb</note> part of the thigh, as has been written by some, but they originate in the upper part, called the bridge <note>original: *dorso*, the dorsum or top of the foot</note> of the foot; and if you wish to be certain of this, grasp the thigh with your hands, somewhat above the knee, and move your toes upward, and you will feel that the flesh of your thigh has no movement within itself, in its tendons or muscles; but it is quite true...

### ON ANATOMY. — SHEETS A. — 18 RECTO.

---

->FOLIO 18 — recto.<-

<margin>Question on the internal nature of the phenomenon of contraction.</margin>
(What is that which so quickly swells the muscles?)

They say it is wind; and where does it flee to when the muscle diminishes with such speed? Into the nerves of sensation <note>original: "nervi del sentimento", referring to the sensory nerves</note> which are hollow <note>original: "busi", meaning pierced or tubular</note>. Therefore it would be a great wind that swells and lengthens the rod <note>original: "verga", a common historical term for the penis</note>, making it as dense as wood, in such a way that no great quantity of air, when reduced to such density, would be sufficient; therefore not only that [air] of the nerves, but even if the body were full of air, it would not be sufficient. And if you wish it to be the air of these nerves, what air would that be which flows through the muscles and reduces them to such hardness and power at the time of the carnal act? Because I once saw a mule that could hardly move due to the fatigue of a long journey under a heavy load, and seeing a mare, it immediately swelled its rod and all its muscles in such a way that its strength was multiplied so much...

# 182 LEONARDO DA VINCI

...that it acquired such speed that it won a race against a mare that was fleeing before it, and she was compelled to submit to the desires of that mule.

<margin>
Precepts for the study of the foot.
</margin>

Apply the same rule to the foot that you applied to the hand; that is, first illustrating the bones from 6 perspectives: from the back, from the front, from below, from above, from the inside, and from the outside.

<margin>
Considerations on the origin of the muscles of the foot.
</margin>

Mondino <note>Mondino de Luzzi (c. 1270–1326), an Italian physician whose 1316 book 'Anathomia' was the standard anatomical text of the Middle Ages and Renaissance.</note> says that the muscles which lift the toes are located in the outer <note>original: "silvestra", literally "wild" or "wooded," an archaic anatomical term for the external side of a limb</note> part of the thigh; and then he adds that the top of the foot has no muscles because Nature intended to make it light, so that it might move easily; for if it were fleshy, it would be heavier. And here experience <note>Leonardo frequently uses "experience" to mean direct observation or experimental proof</note> shows that muscles *a, b, c, d* move the second segments of the bones of the toes; and the muscles of the leg, *r, S, t*, move the tips of the toes. Now, it is necessary to see here why necessity has caused them not to all originate in the foot, or all in the leg, or why those of the leg, which move the tips of the toes, do not...

<summary>Leonardo concludes a discussion on muscle expansion and arousal before transitioning to systematic instructions for drawing the foot and critiquing the anatomical theories of Mondino de Luzzi regarding muscle placement.</summary>
<keywords>Mondino de Luzzi, foot anatomy, muscle contraction, drawing techniques, skeletal structure, biomechanics</keywords>

## ON ANATOMY — FOLIO A — 18 RECTO

...which they serve, that is the mover <note>original: "motore"</note> of the tip of the big toe and of its middle or first bone, etc. And once you have such knowledge, you will illustrate, alongside this, the true shape and size and position of each muscle; but remember to make the threads that represent the muscles in the same positions as the central lines of each muscle; and thus, these threads will demonstrate the shape of the leg and their distance, made clear and known.

Extensor and flexor muscles of the foot.

The muscles that move only the foot in lifting [it] are, in the front, **m** and **n**, originating in the leg from the knee downward; and those that bend it toward the outer ankle <note>original: "chiavatura di fori," literally the outer locking/joint</note> are the muscles **f** and **n**; therefore **n** is common to both of these movements.

</margin>
I have flayed <note>original: "spogliato di pelle," literally stripped of skin</note> one who, through a disease, had wasted away so much that the muscles were consumed and remained in the manner of a thin film, so that the tendons <note>original: "corde," literally cords</note>, instead of turning into muscle, turned into wide skin, and when—

Topography of the muscles and the motor and sensory nerves of the lower limb.

Muscles *n* and *m* only extend themselves to move the whole foot upwards.

You will first illustrate the bones separated and slightly dislocated so that the shape of each individual piece of bone can be better distinguished. Then, join them together in a way that they do not deviate from the first demonstration, except for those parts that are hidden by their contact with each other. <note>This section features a masterful anatomical study of the tendons and musculature of the human lower leg and foot, viewed from a side-and-top perspective.
