This exemplifies Leonardo's view of the human body as a perfect machine.</note>

<meta>Continuity: Following the previous study of the extensor tendons and ligaments of the lower leg, this page continues the investigation into the mechanical control of the foot, specifically focusing on how muscles in the leg and foot manage the complex articulation of the toes.</meta>

<margin>
The movement
of the toes of the
foot arises
from muscles
of the leg,
and the movement
of the fingers
of the hands
arises from
muscles of the
arm.
</margin>

From this muscle, four <term>cords|tendons</term> arise, which go to extend the fourth joints of the four outer toes. And the <term>nerves|Leonardo frequently used the word 'nerve' (neruo) to describe tendons, ligaments, or sinews, as the precise neurological distinction was still being mapped</term> that are attached to these joints are of two kinds: one is a cord that runs straight along the length of the toe, and the other, to which this cord attaches, is a kind of nerve provided by muscles placed <term>transversely|running across or at a right angle to the axis of the limb</term> to the joint of the toe.

<note>Large anatomical drawing of the human foot (dorsal view) illustrating the complex network of extensor tendons. Various tendons are labeled with letters 'a', 'b', 'c', 'd'. Leonardo's draftsmanship reveals how the tendons fan out from the ankle to the extremities of each digit.</note>

The nerves of the toes are of two kinds, which arise from two sources. The first and closest to the <term>sole|the flat underside of the foot</term> are straight and go directly to extend the toes; these are supported by the other kind of nerves which do not run in a straight line but transversely and obliquely, attaching themselves under the said straight nerves. From these are formed [the points] where the straight nerves attach to the <term>denticles|small, tooth-like serrations or projections in the tissue</term> of the toes, and the oblique ones attach to the joints of the toes—no less than the muscles from which the oblique nerves arise, which muscles do not fail to pull upon the straight nerves. And the same is seen in the member of the fingers of the hands, which is supported by the palm of the hand.

<margin>
These are
the muscles
of the sole
of the foot.
</margin>

<margin>
One and the same
muscle
bends
and branches out into
many branches.
</margin>

One and the same
nerve extends
the feet and the
hands and the arms
and the legs, and
forms one and the
same muscle
of one and the same
substance, of one
and the same
nature.

### Key Anatomical Terms
*   **Nerve/Tendon** (*neruo*): Leonardo’s term for the fibrous cords of the body.
*   **Muscle** (*muscolo*): The contractile tissue that produces movement.
*   **Joints** (*giūture*): The points where bones meet and articulation occurs.
*   **Sole** (*piana*): The flat surface of the foot or hand.
*   **Transverse/Oblique** (*trauerso/oblique*): Terms describing the direction of the fibers across the limb.

<keywords>anatomy, tendons, biomechanics, human foot, myology, muscle structure, Renaissance medicine, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page's focus on the tendons and mechanics of the foot, Leonardo now moves up the limb to investigate the complex mechanics of the knee, thigh, and hip. He shifts focus from the extremities to the larger muscle groups responsible for significant bodily movements, such as kneeling and rising.</meta>

<note>Large anatomical study of the muscles and bones of the leg (thigh and knee) shown in a flexed position.</note>

The bone of the thigh <note>the femur</note> and that of the leg <note>the tibia and fibula</note> are shown in this arrangement for the action of a man rising who is kneeling upon a single knee, as shown in the example <insert>sketch</insert> alongside it; see in what way the muscles become tense or slack in such a motion.
The muscle **.f.** shall be that which pulls the bone **.m. .n.** in **.d. .d.**, and this **.m. .n.** shall be the muscle that emerges through **.f.** and through **.h.**; and the muscle **.o.** shall be that which is pulled from below by the movement of the bone **.f. d .h.**, because the bone **.f. d .h.** shall be moved toward **.f. d .h.**

<margin>
<note>Text to the left of the leg drawing</note>
If a man kneels on a single knee with the body upright and arms extended and then rises to his feet, the <term>nerves|Leonardo uses 'nerve' (neruo) to refer to tendons, ligaments, and sinews</term> **.f. .g. .e. .d. .c. .f.** will be tense and the nerve **.a.** will be slack, and the muscle **.s.** will be among those primarily responsible for the movement of the man as he rises, like **.f. .d. .h. .i.**
And the muscles **.k. .l. .m. .n. .o. .p. .q. .r. .s. .t. .v. .x. .y. .z. .&.** shall be those that prevent the over-extension of the knee, and this because the principal nerves are hindered by its excessive extension.
</margin>

<margin>
<note>Text to the right of the leg drawing</note>
I want first to represent the bone of the man and then the bone "clothed" with these muscles, one on top of the other, so that the eye may have a complete knowledge of all the muscles of the man; and if it is possible, I will first represent the nerves, the blood vessels, and the muscles together, because one is the cause of the other, and one takes life and strength from the other.
</margin>

---

There are many nerves that have no other function than to prevent their principal nerves from stretching through the extension of the movement of the limb beyond its natural limit; and such nerves are found everywhere there is movement of the bones, situated beneath the nerves that move those bones.

-> <-

When the bone is moved by the pulling of some muscle, the other muscles shall be represented in another color, and especially those that are beneath those being pulled, so that the eye may better understand their movements; and thus shall it be done with the nerves, so that their path may be better seen, and this shall be done for every movement that the man makes.

<margin>
<note>Isolated notes and labels within the diagram</note>
The hip bone **a r** shall be between one and the other bone of the <term>pecten|the pubic bone or pelvic region</term>
The muscle **.s.** shall be ... <unclear>slack</unclear>
</margin>

<summary>This page explores the mechanical function of the leg muscles and tendons during the transition from kneeling to standing, highlighting Leonardo's plan to illustrate anatomy in layers—from bone to "clothed" muscle. He also discusses a sophisticated color-coding system to help viewers distinguish between overlapping anatomical structures during movement.</summary>

<keywords>biomechanics, kneeling, femur, tibia, muscle layering, anatomical illustration, tendons, pelvic anatomy, leg muscles</keywords>

--- Page 329 ---
<meta>Continuity: Transitioning from the mechanics of the leg and knee on the previous page, Leonardo focuses here on the distal extremity: the foot. He investigates the complex network of tendons and muscles that facilitate the delicate and powerful movements of the toes and the foot as a whole, continuing his "bottom-up" architectural approach to the human body.</meta>

<note>Anatomical studies of the human foot showing muscles, tendons, and nerves from both dorsal (top) and plantar/medial (bottom/side) perspectives.</note>

<margin>
If you wish to see the <term>nerves|Leonardo uses 'nervi' to refer to what we now distinguish as tendons, ligaments, and sinews</term> that pull the feet upward, remove the muscles that are on the <term>collo del pie'|the instep or the dorsal surface of the foot</term> and you will see the exposed nerves which are attached to the toes.

If you wish to see them by the nerves from below, do as before and remove the muscles of the sole of the foot, and you will see the exposed nerves which are attached to the toes from underneath.
</margin>

<note>Detailed dorsal view of the foot showing the extensor tendons.</note>

Let the demonstration of the nerves from below be made before anything else, and of everything that lies between the bone and the skin; and then the nerves from above and all that is interposed between the skin and the bones.

---

<note>Study of the medial side and sole of the foot.</note>

In the bending of the toes by the nerves from below, it is not done by those nerves [themselves], but by the aforementioned nerves which pull the toes upward; and the other nerves perform no other function than to turn the muscles, which shall be demonstrated.

<margin>
In the <term>sossellione|an archaic term likely referring to the flexing or folding action of the joints</term> of the toes, the nerves from below move by the opposite motion, but the nerves from above are the principal <unclear>movers</unclear>.
</margin>

---

<margin>
The 10 toes—
one toe near the other
one above the other
one below the other
</margin>

<note>Glossary of terms used: anatomy, nerves/tendons, muscles, toes, sole of the foot, instep, flexion.</note>

<summary>This page details Leonardo’s anatomical investigation into the mechanics of the foot, specifically focusing on how the tendons (which he calls 'nerves') on both the top and the sole coordinate to move the toes. He emphasizes a layer-by-layer dissection method to reveal the hidden "machinery" of human movement.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, human foot, tendons, muscles, dissection technique, biomechanics, Renaissance science</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: While the previous page (18 verso) was notably blank, this page returns to Leonardo's systematic plan for anatomical demonstration, specifically detailing how he intends to illustrate the foot and leg through progressive layers.</meta>

# ON ANATOMY — FOLIO A — 18 RECTO

...would be the finished foot with all the <term>sentiments<note>original: "sentimẽtj". In Leonardo's anatomical writings, this term refers to the sensory nerves or the capacity for feeling and perception within a limb.</note>. You could make the 11th demonstration in the manner of a transparent foot, where one could see all the things mentioned above.

<margin>Precepts for the study of the leg.</margin>
But first make the demonstration of the sensory nerves of the leg and their branching from 4 perspectives, so that one can clearly see where such nerves derive from; and then make a drawing of a young and graceful foot with few muscles.

<margin>Nerves of the leg.</margin>
All the nerves of the legs in front serve the tips of the toes, as is shown in its big toe.

->noting<-

<margin>General precepts on the method of studying the leg.</margin>
When you make your demonstrations of the bones from different perspectives, then make the <term>panniculi<note>original: "panniculi". Leonardo uses this term to describe the various membranes, fascia, or thin layers of tissue that interpose between muscles and bones.</note> which are interposed between the bones and the muscles; and besides this, when you have illustrated the first muscles and described and shown their function, make the 2nd demonstration over these first muscles, and the 3rd demonstration over the second demonstration, and so continue.

<summary>Leonardo outlines a plan for a multi-layered anatomical study of the foot and leg, proposing a "transparent" view and a systematic method of drawing structures (nerves, membranes, and muscles) in successive layers to show their relationships.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, foot, leg, nerves, sensory nerves, big toe, panniculi, layering technique, scientific method</keywords>

--- Page 332 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page's focus on the general method for studying the leg and nerves, Leonardo now provides a specific 11-step protocol for illustrating the anatomy of the foot and leg, moving from the skeleton to the finished surface form.</meta>

# LEONARDO DA VINCI

First make here the simple bones | and then clothe them successively | in stages in the same | way that nature clothed them.

It is necessary that in the definitions of the foot | it be joined with the leg | as far as the knee, because in that leg | originate the muscles that | move the tips of the toes |—that is, the final bones. ———

In the 1st demonstration, let the bones be somewhat | separated from one another, so that their | true shape may be known. In the 2nd, let these bones be shown | sawn through, to see which is hollow and which is solid. In the 3rd | demonstration, let these bones be joined together. In the 4th, let there be the <term>ligaments<note>original: "legature". The connective tissues or "bindings" that hold joints together.</note> of these bones to one | another. In the 5th, let there be the muscles that strengthen | these bones. 6th: the muscles with their <term>tendons<note>original: "corde". Leonardo uses the word for 'cords' to describe the rope-like structures of the tendons.</note>. | 7th: the muscles of the leg, with the tendons that come | to these toes. 8th: the <term>sensory nerves<note>original: "neruj del sentimẽto". These are the nerves responsible for sensation and the transmission of the "will" to move.</note>. 9th | the arteries and veins. 10th: the muscular skin. | 11th: the foot in its final beauty. ——— And | each of the 4 perspectives should have these | 11 demonstrations. ———

---

First make here the simple bones, and then clothe them successively in stages, in the same way that nature clothed them.

It is necessary that in the definitions of the foot, it be joined with the leg as far as the knee, because in that leg originate the muscles that move the tips of the toes, that is, the final bones.

In the 1st demonstration, let the bones be somewhat separated from one another, so that their true shape may be known. In the 2nd, let these bones be shown sawn through, to see which is hollow and which is solid. In the 3rd demonstration, let these bones be joined together. In the 4th, let there be the ligaments of these bones to one another. In the 5th, let there be the muscles that strengthen these bones. 6th: the muscles with their tendons. 7th: the muscles of the leg, with the tendons that come to these toes. 8th: the sensory nerves. 9th: the arteries and veins. 10th: the muscular skin. 11th: the foot in its final beauty. And each of the 4 perspectives should have these 11 demonstrations.

---

First make here the simple bones, and then cover them successively and gradually, in the same way that nature has clothed them.

The foot, in its definitions, must be joined with the leg up to the knee, because the muscles that move the tips of the toes—that is, the last bones—originate in the said leg.

In the 1st demonstration, it is necessary for the bones to be a little separated from each other, to recognize their true shape. In the 2nd, it is necessary to show these bones sawn through, to see which is hollow and which is solid. In the 3rd demonstration, these bones must be joined together. In the 4th, there must be the ligaments that hold these bones to one another. In the 5th, there will be the muscles that strengthen these bones. 6th: the muscles with their tendons. 7th: the muscles of the leg with the tendons that come to these toes. 8th: the nerves of sensibility. 9th: the arteries and the veins. 10th: the muscular skin. 11th: the foot in its last beauty. And let each of the 4 perspectives have these 11 demonstrations.

<summary>Leonardo prescribes a rigorous 11-step methodology for anatomical illustration, starting with the skeleton and building up through ligaments, muscles, nerves, and vessels to the final surface form of the foot.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, foot, leg, osteology, myology, tendons, ligaments, dissection method, nerves, vascular system</keywords>

->**Color Calibration Target and Measurement Scale**<-

<note>This page is a modern addition used by photographers and archivists during the digitization process. It contains no original content from the manuscript.</note>

The image displays a technical setup used to ensure the accuracy of the digital reproduction. It includes a **color checker chart**—a grid of precisely colored squares—which allows experts to calibrate the colors on their screens to match the physical manuscript perfectly. Alongside it is a **metric and imperial ruler**, providing a scale so that researchers can understand the actual physical dimensions of Leonardo's pages.

<summary>This page consists of technical calibration equipment, including a color checker and a ruler, used to maintain visual and scale accuracy during the digital imaging of the manuscript.</summary>
